American History & Society — A Background Guide

From American History, the free background guide
This guide covers background knowledge. The readings it summarises are not reproduced here.
American History & Society
CoversUS history, law, society and school science
Readings91, in 7 topics
Foundations6 primers, about an hour
Extra themes6 primers (Topic 8)
GlossaryEvery key term, A–Z

This guide gives you the context an American reader picks up in school: the history, people, laws and science that writing about America takes for granted. It is built around 91 short readings in seven topics. Everything is told as short, plain-English stories, so it is easy to follow and easy to remember.

How to use it
  • Read the six “Foundations” first (about an hour; you can spread them over a few sittings). They tell the big stories that most readings assume you know: the story of America, how Congress and the courts work, famous Supreme Court cases, the “isms”, how historians argue, and basic science.
  • Then open a reading card (p.4 = page in the original source). Each card has: In short what the reading says, in plain words · The story the background it assumes, told as a story, ending with what an average American already “knows” · Remember it like this a one-line memory hook · Words to know · Reading tip how the argument is built.
  • Use the search box to find any person, law, or term, and tick “Got it” to track your progress (saved in this browser).
  • Some readings are excerpts that stop mid-argument. Cards mark where the text breaks off, and the background covers what the rest of the reading assumes.
  • Beyond the readings, Topic 8 covers other recurring themes (US economy and markets, challenges to religion, American power, the Cold War, new technology, cell biology). The Glossary collects every key term in one A–Z list.
  • Prefer to listen? The whole guide is also retold as a Hindi audiobook, with names and key terms kept in English. Stream it chapter by chapter, or download the MP3s.
The single most useful insight: almost every social-science reading in this guide follows one pattern. First there is a traditional view. Then newer scholars revise it. Then the author qualifies the revision or partly rejects it. The background below helps you spot the traditional view fast. Still, follow what each author actually says, rather than what you already know. Use background to read faster and with more confidence, not to bring in outside facts.

Foundation 1 · The story of America

American students hear this story three times: in elementary, middle and high school. Most of the readings in this guide sit somewhere inside it, so first work out where each one fits.

Chapter 1 · The first Americans (before 1492)

More than 15,000 years ago, during the last Ice Age, so much water was frozen into ice that the sea was much lower than today. A wide strip of dry land, Beringia, joined Siberia to Alaska. Small bands of hunters walked across it, and some may have come down the Pacific coast.

By about 13,000 years ago, a people that archaeologists call the Clovis culture had spread across North America. Between about 13,000 and 11,000 years ago, many giant Ice Age animals, such as mammoths and giant sloths, died out. One reading in this guide (p.43) is set in this world.

Much later, between about 850 and 1350 CE, farming peoples built towns. In the dry Southwest, the Ancestral Puebloans built stone villages at Chaco Canyon and Mesa Verde. Along the Mississippi River, people raised huge earth mounds, and their biggest city was Cahokia. Further south, the Aztecs in Mexico and the Incas in the Andes later built great empires.

Remember it like this: Ice Age hunters walk over a land bridge from Asia. Thousands of years later come stone villages in the Southwest and mound cities on the Mississippi.

Chapter 2 · Europeans arrive (1492–1700)

In 1492 Christopher Columbus, sailing for Spain, reached the Caribbean. Within fifty years, Spanish soldiers had conquered both great empires. Hernán Cortés defeated the Aztecs in 1519–21, and Francisco Pizarro defeated the Incas in 1532–33.

The English came later. Their first lasting colony was Jamestown, in Virginia, in 1607. In 1619 the first enslaved Africans were brought there. That was the start of slavery in English America.

Further north, English Protestants who wanted a purer church crossed the Atlantic. The Pilgrims sailed on the Mayflower and founded Plymouth in 1620. The Puritans founded Massachusetts Bay, around Boston, in 1630. Connecticut followed in 1636. Together, these northern colonies became known as New England. In 1681 William Penn, a Quaker, founded Pennsylvania, with Philadelphia as its centre. He wanted a colony where people of different faiths could live side by side.

Remember it like this: Spain came first and conquered empires. England came later and built colonies: Jamestown in 1607, slavery in 1619, the Pilgrims in 1620.

Chapter 3 · Taxes, tea and a new country (1754–1791)

Britain and France fought over North America in the French and Indian War (1754–63). Britain won Canada, but the war left it deep in debt, so it began to tax the colonists. The colonists had no members in the British Parliament, so they protested: “No taxation without representation!”

Anger grew year by year. There was the Stamp Act in 1765 and the Boston Massacre in 1770. Then came the Boston Tea Party in 1773, when protesters threw British tea into Boston harbour. Fighting broke out in 1775.

In January 1776, Thomas Paine’s pamphlet Common Sense urged a complete break with Britain. On 4 July 1776 the colonies adopted the Declaration of Independence, written mainly by Thomas Jefferson. Its most famous line is “all men are created equal”. The Americans won the decisive battle at Yorktown in 1781, and the Treaty of Paris confirmed independence in 1783.

Next, the new country wrote its rulebook. The Constitution was drafted in Philadelphia in 1787, and George Washington became the first president in 1789. In 1791 ten amendments, the Bill of Rights, were added to protect freedoms such as speech and religion. Compared with India’s Constitution, America’s is far shorter and much older, and it is still in use.

Remember it like this: 4 July 1776 is America’s 15 August. Debt led to taxes, taxes led to anger, and anger led to war and a new country.

Chapter 4 · A young country grows (1803–1848)

In 1803 the young country made a huge land deal. It bought a vast territory called Louisiana from Napoleon, the ruler of France. This Louisiana Purchase doubled the size of the country. Soon there was another war with Britain, the War of 1812, which lasted until 1815.

The years from about 1815 to 1861 are called the antebellum era. The word means “before the war”, and the war is the Civil War. From the 1820s to the 1840s, the vote spread to almost all white men. This change is called Jacksonian democracy, after Andrew Jackson.

Jackson led the Democrats. Their rivals were the Whigs, led by Henry Clay. This contest between two parties is called the Second Party System. Meanwhile, early factories appeared, such as the Lowell textile mills, where young women worked.

In American history, “Indian” means Native American, not someone from India. As the country grew, these first Americans were pushed off their land. The Indian Removal Act of 1830 led to the Cherokee people’s forced removal in 1838–39. It is remembered as the Trail of Tears.

The country also grew west. In the Mexican–American War of 1846–48, the US beat Mexico. Under the Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo in 1848, Mexico gave up California and the Southwest. Then the California Gold Rush began in 1848–49, as crowds rushed in to dig for gold.

Also in 1848, a meeting at Seneca Falls called for women’s rights. This Seneca Falls Convention was organised by Elizabeth Cady Stanton and Lucretia Mott. It marks the birth of the organised women’s rights movement.

Remember it like this: Picture a map that keeps growing: Louisiana bought in 1803, California and the Southwest taken from Mexico in 1848. In that same year, 1848, women met at Seneca Falls.

Chapter 5 · The road to civil war (1850s)

All that new land raised a hard question. Should slavery spread into the West? Through the 1850s, Americans fought bitterly over this question.

Several events marked these years. In 1850 came the Fugitive Slave Act. A “fugitive” is a runaway, and this law dealt with enslaved people who had escaped. In 1852 the book Uncle Tom’s Cabin came out.

The old party system cracked as well. The Whigs collapsed, and in 1854 a new party was founded, the Republican Party. Back then, it was Lincoln’s anti-slavery party.

In 1857 the Supreme Court, the country’s highest court, decided the Dred Scott case. It ruled that Black people could not be citizens. Four years later, the Civil War began.

Remember it like this: The 1850s in four steps: a law on runaway slaves in 1850, a book in 1852, a new party in 1854 and a court case in 1857. Then, in 1861, war.

Chapter 6 · Civil War and Reconstruction (1861–1877)

In 1861 the country split in two, and the Civil War began. On one side was the Union, the North, led by President Lincoln. On the other was the Confederacy, eleven Southern slave states.

In 1863 came the Emancipation Proclamation. Emancipation means setting people free. The proclamation let Black men join the Union army, and about 180,000 served.

In 1865 the Union won, and an assassin killed Lincoln. That same year the 13th Amendment, a change to the Constitution, abolished slavery.

Next came Reconstruction, from 1865 to 1877. The South was rebuilt under the watch of the federal government, the national government, much like the Centre in India. The 14th Amendment of 1868 granted citizenship and “equal protection” of the laws. Almost every later civil-rights case rests on that phrase.

The 15th Amendment of 1870 protected the right to vote, whatever a person’s race. Black men voted and held office. But in the Compromise of 1877, federal troops left the South, and Reconstruction ended.

Remember it like this: Count 13, 14, 15 as free, citizen, vote. The 13th ends slavery, the 14th gives citizenship and equal protection, and the 15th protects the vote.

Chapter 7 · Railroads, factories and Jim Crow (1877–1900)

After 1877 came the age of railroads and big business. Rockefeller and Carnegie were its giants. Americans call it the Gilded Age. “Gilded” means covered with a thin layer of gold.

Huge numbers of immigrants arrived from southern and eastern Europe. Workers and bosses clashed in major labour conflicts, such as Haymarket in 1886 and the Pullman Strike in 1894. In a strike, workers stop work together to press their demands. In 1886 the AFL, a big alliance of trade unions, was founded.

Some groups were shut out or pushed aside. The Chinese Exclusion Act of 1882 kept Chinese immigrants out. The Dawes Act of 1887 broke up Indian reservations, the lands set aside for Native nations. The Indian wars ended with the Wounded Knee massacre in 1890.

In the 1890s and 1900s, the South built a system called Jim Crow. Southern states imposed segregation, keeping Black and white people apart. They also stripped Black citizens of the vote. In Plessy v. Ferguson (1896), the Supreme Court approved this as “separate but equal”.

Hard times and war marked the 1890s too. The Panic of 1893 was a depression, a long, deep slump in business. In the Spanish–American War of 1898, the US beat Spain and gained Puerto Rico, Guam and the Philippines.

Remember it like this: Gilded means a thin coat of gold. Picture shiny railroads and fortunes on top, with strikes, exclusion and Jim Crow underneath.

Chapter 8 · Reformers, the vote and World War I (1890–1920)

From about 1890 to 1920, a wave of reform swept America. This is the Progressive Era. Muckraking journalists dug up scandals and exposed them. The government went trust-busting, breaking up giant business combinations called trusts.

Other reformers opened settlement houses, community centres in poor city neighbourhoods. One was Jane Addams’ Hull House. Others campaigned against child labour. In 1913 the government created the Federal Reserve, America’s central bank, much like the Reserve Bank of India.

Two big changes came at the end of the era. In 1919 the 18th Amendment brought Prohibition, a nationwide ban on alcohol. In 1920 the 19th Amendment gave women suffrage, the right to vote. The fight for legal rights and the vote, from Seneca Falls in 1848 to 1920, is called the first wave of feminism.

Meanwhile, a huge movement of people began. From 1910 to 1970, about 6 million Black Southerners moved to Northern and Western cities. This is the Great Migration.

World War I broke out in 1914, and the US joined in April 1917. The war ended in 1918, but fear and violence followed at home. A Red Scare, a wave of fear of communists, or “Reds”, swept the country in 1919–20. In 1919 there was also the “Red Summer” of anti-Black riots.

Remember it like this: Two amendments, two years: the 18th took alcohol away in 1919, and the 19th gave women the vote in 1920.

Chapter 9 · Boom, bust and war (1920–1945)

The 1920s are remembered as the Roaring Twenties. It was the age of jazz and of flappers, young women who broke old rules of dress and behaviour. In New York’s Harlem, Black art, music and writing flowered in the Harlem Renaissance. Marcus Garvey also led a movement among Black Americans.

The decade had its battles too. In 1924 the US set immigration quotas, fixed limits on how many immigrants could come. In 1925 the Scopes “monkey” trial was a court fight over teaching evolution.

In 1929 the stock market on Wall Street, America’s Dalal Street, crashed. The crash led to the Great Depression, which lasted until 1939. By 1933 about one worker in four had no job. Then President Franklin Roosevelt, known as FDR, began the New Deal.

The New Deal brought Social Security, government pensions for old age. It also brought the Wagner Act of 1935, a labour law. Labour unions boomed, among them the CIO.

In December 1941, Japan attacked Pearl Harbor, and the US entered World War II. Women took factory jobs, and “Rosie the Riveter” became their symbol. In 1942 Japanese Americans were interned, or locked up in camps. The war ended in August 1945, after the US dropped atomic bombs on Japan.

Remember it like this: Think roar, crash, war: the Roaring Twenties, the Wall Street crash of 1929, then Pearl Harbor in 1941. FDR’s New Deal sits between the crash and the war.

Chapter 10 · Cold War and civil rights (1945–1968)

After 1945 the US and the USSR faced each other in the Cold War, which lasted until 1991. It was “cold” because the two never fought a full war against each other. The Marshall Plan sent American aid to Europe, and the NATO military alliance was formed.

Then came the Korean War of 1950–53, and at home McCarthyism, a hunt for suspected communists. In 1957 the USSR launched the satellite Sputnik, and in 1962 came the tense Cuban Missile Crisis. US troops fought in Vietnam from about 1965 to 1973. In 1969 Americans landed on the Moon.

Meanwhile, from 1954 to 1968, the Civil Rights Movement took on Jim Crow. In Brown v. Board (1954), the Supreme Court ruled that school segregation broke the promise of equal protection, overturning Plessy. In 1955 came the Montgomery Bus Boycott, the protest linked with Rosa Parks and Martin Luther King Jr. In a boycott, people refuse to use something, here the city’s buses.

The March on Washington followed in 1963. Next came two major laws, the Civil Rights Act of 1964 and the Voting Rights Act of 1965. In 1968 King was assassinated.

Remember it like this: Two contests ran side by side. Abroad, the US faced the USSR from 1945 to 1991. At home, the fight against Jim Crow ran from Brown in 1954 to King’s death in 1968.

Chapter 11 · New voices, new fields (1960s–1970s)

The 1960s and 70s also brought second-wave feminism. The first wave had won the vote, and the second turned to work, family, body and culture. Landmarks include The Feminine Mystique, a 1963 book by Betty Friedan, and the Title IX law of 1972. In 1973 the Supreme Court’s Roe v. Wade ruling recognised a right to abortion.

Other groups spoke up too. Native Americans called for “Red Power” through the American Indian Movement. Chicano (Mexican American) and Asian American movements arose as well.

The environmental movement rose at the same time. Its landmarks were the book Silent Spring in 1962, the EPA, a national environment agency, in 1970, and the Endangered Species Act in 1973. In 1974 came the Watergate scandal.

These years also changed American universities. “Women’s history”, “Black history” and “ethnic studies” became academic fields, subjects with their own courses and scholars. Most of the history readings in this guide come from these fields.

Remember it like this: Picture the 1960s and 70s on two stages: marches in the streets and new subjects on campus. Women’s history, Black history and ethnic studies are where most of these readings come from.

Chapter 12 · Modern America (1980–today)

After 1980 came President Reagan, and then the end of the Cold War. In 1991 the USSR itself collapsed. In the 1990s the internet spread, and with it came the dot-com boom, a rush of money into new internet companies. The name comes from the “.com” in web addresses.

On 11 September 2001, terrorist attacks struck the US. Americans call the day 9/11, because they write the month first. Wars in Afghanistan and Iraq followed.

In 2008 a financial crisis shook the economy. In 2009 Obama became the first Black president, and he served until 2017.

In 2020 the COVID-19 pandemic struck America, as it struck India. In 2022 the Supreme Court’s Dobbs decision overturned Roe and returned abortion law to the states. The same year, a boom in AI, or artificial intelligence, began. It is still going on.

Remember it like this: Five dates for today’s America: 1991 the USSR falls, 2001 brings 9/11, 2008 a financial crisis, 2009 Obama, and 2022 Dobbs and the AI boom.

Foundation 2 · How US government & law work

America is still run by a rulebook written in 1787, the Constitution. These stories show who holds power, which changes to the rulebook keep coming up, and how the political parties shifted over time. The legal words that the readings use are collected at the end.

Chapter 1 · One country, fifty states

Picture the year 1787. The new country writes its rulebook, the Constitution, and builds it on an idea called federalism. Power is split between two levels of government.

The top level is the national government, which Americans call the federal government. The second level is the 50 states, and each has its own government. India works in a similar way, with power shared between the Centre and the states. So “federal” in American writing means roughly what “central” means in India.

The federal government is also a big landowner. Federal lands are lands owned by the national government. Today they cover about 28% of the country. In some Western states, most of the land is federal land.

Remember it like this: Federal means national, like India’s Centre. The 50 states are the second level, like India’s states. Federal lands are the nation’s own land, about 28% of the country.

Chapter 2 · Three branches that check each other

The Constitution then splits the national government into three branches. Congress is the legislature, the branch that makes laws. The president heads the executive, the branch that runs the government. The Supreme Court heads the judiciary, the courts.

Congress has two chambers, a bit like India’s Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha. The House of Representatives has 435 members. Each state gets seats according to its population, which is counted every ten years in the census. House members serve two-year terms. The Senate is different: every state, big or small, gets exactly two senators, and they serve six-year terms.

The president serves four-year terms, and at most two of them. When Congress passes a bill, the president can sign it into law or reject it with a veto. The president can also issue executive orders, which are rules that come from the president alone. For example, Executive Order 8802 of 1941 banned racial discrimination in defence industries.

The Supreme Court has nine judges, called justices, who are appointed for life. It can strike down a law that breaks the Constitution, so the law no longer counts. This power is called judicial review, and it dates from the case Marbury v. Madison in 1803.

Some jobs are shared, so each branch can check the others. The president makes treaties, but two-thirds of the Senate must approve, or ratify, each one. The House has no say in treaties. That is why, in 1871, the House forced an end to treaty-making with Indian tribes (p.35).

Remember it like this: Congress writes the law, the president signs or vetoes it, and the Court can strike it down. House seats follow population, while the Senate gives every state two seats.

Chapter 3 · Changing the rulebook: freedom and the end of slavery

The Constitution can be changed. Each change is called an amendment, and amendments are known by their numbers. A handful of them come up again and again.

The first ten amendments, the Bill of Rights, were added in 1791. The 1st Amendment protects freedom of speech, of the press and of religion. It also says the government may not set up an official religion. That rule is the basis of the “separation of church and state”.

Three more came after the Civil War. In 1865 the 13th Amendment abolished slavery. In 1868 the 14th gave birthright citizenship, so anyone born in the country is a citizen. It also promised “due process”, which means fair legal procedures, and “equal protection of the laws”. Equal protection is the basis of almost every civil-rights case. In 1870 the 15th barred anyone from being denied the vote because of race.

Remember it like this: The 14th Amendment promises “equal protection of the laws”. Article 14 of India’s Constitution uses the same words. Fourteen and fourteen: equality lives at number 14 in both countries.

Chapter 4 · Drink, votes and a promise that failed

One amendment came and went. In 1919 the 18th Amendment began Prohibition, a national ban on alcohol. It did not last. In 1933 the 21st Amendment repealed it.

Other amendments opened the vote to more people, step by step. In 1920 the 19th gave women the vote, which is called women’s suffrage. In 1964 the 24th banned poll taxes, fees that people had to pay before they could vote. In 1971 the 26th lowered the voting age to 18.

One amendment never made it. The Equal Rights Amendment, or ERA, was first proposed in 1923. Congress finally passed it in 1972. But an amendment also needs approval from the states, and not enough states ever ratified it.

Remember it like this: The 18th banned the drink and the 21st brought it back. The 19th gave women the vote, and the 26th gave it to 18-year-olds. The ERA is the amendment that got stuck halfway.

Chapter 5 · The parties: a confusing history

American party names have changed over time, so they can easily confuse. In the 1790s the two sides were the Federalists and the Democratic-Republicans. From the 1830s to the 1850s, the Democrats faced the Whigs. This period is called the “Second Party System”. The American Whigs borrowed the name of a British party to oppose Andrew Jackson, the Democrat they nicknamed “King Andrew”.

In 1854 the Republican Party was founded, and the two big parties of today took shape: Democrats and Republicans. In the 19th century the Republicans were Lincoln’s anti-slavery party. The Democrats dominated the white South, which was called the “Solid South”. Then, after the Civil Rights Act of 1964, the South shifted to the Republicans.

So be careful with old party names. Today “liberal” means left and goes with the Democrats. “Conservative” means right and goes with the Republicans.

Smaller “third parties” have existed too. Third parties such as the Socialist Party were strongest in immigrant cities such as New York (p.20). In 1912 the Socialist candidate, Eugene Debs, won about 6% of the presidential vote.

One last warning. In the 18th century, “republican” meant something different: against kings, and for government by elected representatives (see p.24). It has nothing to do with the later Republican Party.

Remember it like this: Lincoln’s Republicans began as the anti-slavery party, and the Democrats once held the “Solid South”. After 1964 the South switched sides, but the party names stayed the same.

Words to know

Civil vs criminal case
A civil case is a private dispute, for example over a debt, a contract or property. In a criminal case, the state prosecutes someone for a crime.
Statute / legislation
A law passed by a legislature. Courts decide what statutes and treaties mean.
Precedent / doctrine
A rule from earlier cases that later courts follow, for example the “Winters doctrine”.
Plaintiff / defendant
The plaintiff is the side that sues. The defendant is the side being sued. A case is named “Plaintiff v. Defendant”.
Sovereignty
The highest authority over a territory. Tribes are “domestic dependent nations” with limited sovereignty.
Jurisdiction
Which government or court has authority over a place or a case.
Common law
Judge-made law that the US inherited from English law. Coverture, the old rule that merged a wife’s legal identity into her husband’s, is a common-law rule. Areas that were once Spanish, French or Mexican used civil-law codes instead. This matters for the Californianas reading, p.28.

Foundation 3 · Supreme Court cases that changed America

Supreme Court cases come up again and again in American history. Three cases below appear in this guide’s own readings: Lone Wolf v. Hitchcock, Winters v. United States and Arizona v. California. The rest are cases that every American student hears about.

Chapter 1 · The power to strike down laws

Picture the Supreme Court: nine justices, appointed for life, at the top of America’s courts. Each case is named after the two sides, with a “v.” between them, as Foundation 2 explains. Some of their decisions changed the country.

Marbury v. Madison (1803) decided that the Court can strike down laws that go against the Constitution. A law that is struck down no longer counts. This power is called judicial review, and this case created it.

Hammer v. Dagenhart (1918) shows the power at work against Congress. In the Progressive Era, reformers campaigned against child labour, and Congress passed a federal child-labour law. The Court struck it down. The case is background to the Progressive fight over child labour (p.26).

Remember it like this: In 1803 Marbury handed the Court a red pen. Since then it has been able to cross out any law that breaks the Constitution, as it did to the child-labour law in 1918.

Chapter 2 · Race and equality

Dred Scott v. Sandford (1857) ruled that Black people were not citizens. It also ruled that Congress could not ban slavery in the territories, the lands that were not yet states. It is widely seen as the worst decision the Court ever made. It helped cause the Civil War, and the 13th and 14th Amendments later overturned it.

Plessy v. Ferguson (1896) ruled that “separate but equal” segregation, keeping Black and white people apart by law, was allowed under the Constitution. It became the legal basis of Jim Crow, the segregation laws of the Southern states, until 1954 (p.47).

Korematsu v. United States (1944) upheld the internment of Japanese Americans during World War II. Internment means the government held them in camps. Today the decision is repudiated, which means it is rejected as wrong. It is a key fact in Asian American history.

Brown v. Board of Education (1954) ruled that segregated schools break the 14th Amendment’s promise of equal protection: the laws must protect everyone equally. It overturned Plessy and marked the start of the civil-rights era. The NAACP is a civil-rights group, and its lawyer Thurgood Marshall argued the case. C. Vann Woodward did research for the NAACP (p.47).

Loving v. Virginia (1967) struck down laws that banned interracial marriage, marriage between people of different races. Like Brown, it belongs to the story of civil rights.

Regents of the University of California v. Bakke (1978) barred racial quotas. A quota sets aside a fixed number of seats for one group, a little like reserved seats in colleges in India. But Bakke allowed colleges to count race as one factor. Then SFFA v. Harvard (2023) ended race-conscious college admissions. Both cases are part of the debate over affirmative action: whether race may count in choices such as college admissions. See also the minority-business reading on p.55.

Remember it like this: In 1896 Plessy put up a wall called “separate but equal”. In 1954 Brown knocked it down in the schools and started the civil-rights era.

Chapter 3 · Women and the family

Muller v. Oregon (1908) upheld a law that set maximum working hours for women. A written argument in the case, the “Brandeis brief”, used social-science data. Laws like this, meant to protect women at work, are called protective labour laws. Feminists debated them in the 1920s.

Roe v. Wade (1973) recognised a constitutional right to abortion. It is central both to second-wave feminism, the women’s movement of the 1960s–80s, and to today’s politics. Then, in 2022, Dobbs v. Jackson overturned Roe. The question of abortion went back to the states.

Obergefell v. Hodges (2015) ruled that same-sex marriage is a constitutional right. Like Brown, it is an equal-protection case.

Remember it like this: Roe opened a door in 1973 by recognising a right. Dobbs shut it in 2022 and gave the key back to the states.

Chapter 4 · Native nations and the Court

Johnson v. M’Intosh (1823), Cherokee Nation v. Georgia (1831) and Worcester v. Georgia (1832) are known together as the “Marshall Trilogy”, after Chief Justice John Marshall, who wrote all three rulings. They are the foundation of federal Indian law. They ruled that tribes hold only a “right of occupancy”: tribes may live on and use their land, but they do not own it outright. Tribes are “domestic dependent nations”, nations that sit inside the United States and depend on it. Yet a state cannot make rules for tribal land.

Lone Wolf v. Hitchcock (1903) ruled that Congress has “plenary” power over Indian affairs. Plenary means nearly unlimited. The Court also said that Congress can break a treaty on its own, without the tribe’s agreement. This decision is called “the Indians’ Dred Scott” (p.35).

Winters v. United States (1908) was about water. A reservation is land set aside for a tribe. The Court ruled that creating a reservation also reserves, without saying so, the water needed to make the land livable. This rule is the “Winters doctrine” of Indian water rights (p.33).

Arizona v. California (1963) divided up the water of the Colorado River. It also applied the Winters doctrine to every kind of federal reservation, not just Indian ones. The case extended Indian water rights (p.33).

McGirt v. Oklahoma (2020) was about the Creek, a Native nation. The Court ruled that, for federal criminal law, much of eastern Oklahoma is still a Creek reservation. The case is a recent landmark in tribal sovereignty, a tribe’s power to govern itself.

Remember it like this: The Marshall Trilogy calls tribes nations inside a nation. Winters says the water comes with the land, just as a rented flat comes with its water connection.

Foundation 4 · The “isms” & feminism’s waves

This Foundation tells two stories. The first is about the “isms”: big ideas about who should own things, who should rule, and how a person fits into the group. The second is feminism’s story, told in “waves”, together with the ideas that keep coming up in women’s history.

Chapter 1 · Free people and free markets (1776)

Every society must decide how much power its government should have over people and their property. One answer is classical liberalism. It stands for individual rights, limited government and free markets. It is the tradition of thinkers such as John Locke and J.S. Mill.

In 1776, the same year as America’s Declaration of Independence, Adam Smith published The Wealth of Nations. It is the classic text of capitalism. In capitalism, private owners hold businesses and property. Markets set prices, and the hope of profit decides what gets made.

The book’s famous image is the “invisible hand”. No one plans a market, yet prices and profit steer it, as if an unseen hand were guiding it. Think of a vegetable market in any Indian town. No one fixes the price of onions, yet each day it settles where buyers and sellers meet.

Remember it like this: 1776 is a double birthday: America’s Declaration of Independence and Smith’s The Wealth of Nations. The “invisible hand” is the onion price that nobody sets, yet everybody pays.

Chapter 2 · Sharing the wealth: socialism and Marx (1848–1867)

Not everyone was happy to leave the economy in private hands. Some people worried about the gap between rich and poor. Their answer was socialism: society or the state should own or control the major industries, in order to reduce inequality.

Socialism comes in different strengths. At the mild end is democratic socialism. It works through elections and a welfare state, in which the government looks after people’s basic needs. At the far end are revolutionary forms.

In 1848 Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels published the Communist Manifesto, and Das Kapital followed in 1867. Their ideas are called Marxism or communism.

They saw history as a struggle between classes. On one side stood the bourgeoisie, the owners of capital. On the other stood the proletariat, the people who work for wages. They believed the struggle would end in revolution and a classless society.

In Marxist language, “bourgeois” means middle-class or capitalist, and it is often a put-down. So “bourgeois feminism” means liberal, middle-class feminism (p.30).

Remember it like this: Picture a tug-of-war, with the bourgeoisie, who own capital, at one end and the proletariat, who work for wages, at the other. Marx and Engels predicted that it would end in revolution and a classless society.

Chapter 3 · “Survival of the fittest” misused (after 1859)

Darwin and Wallace explained how living things change through natural selection, and Darwin’s 1859 book made the idea famous. Living things vary and pass their traits on to their young, but some survive and reproduce more than others. Over time, this makes them better suited to their surroundings. The catchy phrase “survival of the fittest” came from Herbert Spencer.

Some people carried the phrase out of biology and into human society. They used it to justify inequality, laissez-faire economics and racism. Laissez-faire is the idea that government should leave business alone. In this view, if the strong rose and the weak fell, that was simply nature at work.

This misuse is called Social Darwinism. But it is not Darwin’s biology. In biology, “fitness” means success in having offspring. It is not a measure of wealth, power or worth.

Remember it like this: In biology, the “fittest” is the one that leaves the most offspring, not the one with the biggest bank balance. Social Darwinism swapped babies for bank balances.

Chapter 4 · Communism in power and the Cold War (1917–1991)

In the twentieth century, whole countries put Marx’s ideas into practice. Lenin’s USSR did so from 1917, or 1922 by another count, until 1991. Mao’s China has done so since 1949.

From 1947 to 1991, two camps faced each other in the Cold War. On one side stood the US-led capitalist democracies. On the other stood the Soviet-led communist states. The war was “cold” because the two sides never fought each other directly.

Instead they fought proxy wars, backing opposite sides in other countries’ wars, as in Korea, Vietnam and Afghanistan. They also ran an arms race, building ever more weapons, and a space race to lead in space. India, under Nehru, chose to join neither camp. The Cold War ended in 1991, the same year the USSR came to an end.

Remember it like this: The Cold War was two rival giants who raced for weapons and space and backed opposite sides in other people’s quarrels, but never came to blows. It ended with the USSR, in 1991.

Chapter 5 · Two tricky American words: “liberal” and “republic”

Be careful with the word “liberal”. Classical liberalism, from the first chapter, stands for individual rights, limited government and free markets. But in US politics today, a “liberal” is someone left of centre. The two meanings are different, so do not mix them up.

The words “democracy” and “republic” can also confuse readers. The US is a constitutional federal republic. A constitution sets its basic rules.

Power is shared between the national, or “federal”, government and the states. Elected representatives run the country, so it is also a representative democracy: the people rule through those they elect.

India works in a similar way. It too is a republic with a constitution, and power is shared between the centre and the states.

Remember it like this: “Liberal” has two lives: small government in old books, left of centre in today’s US news. And America, like India, is a republic where the people rule through the representatives they elect.

Chapter 6 · The self-made man and the family self

The US is the world’s most individualistic culture. Its ideal is the “self-made man”, who rises through his own effort. This outlook, which puts the single person first, is called individualism.

Many Asian, African and Latin societies see a person differently. They define the self through family and group (p.22 and p.31). This outlook is called collectivism.

Picture the difference this way. Asked “Who are you?”, an individualist might begin with a job or an achievement. A collectivist might begin with a family or a group.

Remember it like this: The individualist says “I made myself”. The collectivist says “My family and my group made me who I am”.

Chapter 7 · The wife who disappeared in law: coverture

Now turn to women’s history, and picture a bride in early America. Under English common law, the judge-made law that America inherited, her legal identity merged into her husband’s when she married. She became a feme covert, a “covered woman”. This rule is called coverture.

A married woman could not own property, sign contracts, keep her own wages or sue anyone in court. A single woman or a widow was different. She was a feme sole, a “woman alone”, and she could do all these things.

Coverture did not vanish overnight. Laws called the Married Women’s Property Acts chipped away at it, starting in 1839, or 1848 by another count.

Scholars also have a name for a society in which men hold authority in both the family and the state. They call it a patriarchy.

Remember it like this: A feme covert is a wife “covered” by her husband’s legal umbrella: she cannot own, sign or sue. A feme sole is a woman “alone”, single or widowed, who can do all three.

Chapter 8 · Mothers of the republic and the “true woman”

After the American Revolution, mothers had a civic duty, a duty to the nation: to raise virtuous citizens. This idea is known as republican motherhood (p.24). Here “republican” means “of the republic”. It has nothing to do with today’s Republican Party.

Another idea split life into separate spheres. The public world of work and politics was for men. The private world of the home was for women.

In the 1800s this took the form of the cult of domesticity, also called “true womanhood”. The ideal true woman had four virtues: piety, purity, submissiveness and domesticity. In plain words, she was religious, sexually pure, obedient and devoted to her home.

Remember it like this: Draw two circles: the busy public world for men, the home for women. Inside the home circle stands the “true woman”, pious, pure, obedient and home-loving, like the perfect bahu of an Indian TV serial.

Chapter 9 · The first wave: winning the vote (1848–1920)

In the US and Britain, feminism’s history is told as a series of “waves”. Like waves at sea, each one rises, falls back and is followed by another.

The first wave began in 1848, the same year as the Communist Manifesto. That year a meeting at Seneca Falls, in New York State, launched the organised women’s rights movement. The first wave’s leaders included Susan B. Anthony and Elizabeth Cady Stanton. They fought for legal rights and the vote.

The right to vote is called suffrage. American campaigners called themselves suffragists. “Suffragettes” was the label for the more militant, or combative, British campaigners.

Victory came in 1920, when the 19th Amendment to the Constitution gave American women the vote. That was more than seventy years after Seneca Falls. In India, by contrast, women could vote in the country’s very first general election.

Remember it like this: The first wave runs from Seneca Falls in 1848 to the 19th Amendment in 1920. The extra “-ette” marks the British militants, while the Americans were suffragists.

Chapter 10 · Between the waves (1920–1960)

Winning the vote did not end the story. In the years that followed, the movement split. Some activists pushed for an Equal Rights Amendment, or ERA, to write equal rights for women into the Constitution. Others backed protective labour laws, which gave women workers special protection.

During the Great Depression, the economic slump that followed the Wall Street crash, President Roosevelt answered with the New Deal. Two women from that story are worth knowing: Frances Perkins and Eleanor Roosevelt, the president’s wife.

Then came World War II. Women took factory jobs, and their symbol was “Rosie the Riveter”, a woman fastening metal parts in a factory. After the war came the baby boom, a sharp rise in births, and an era of domesticity, with life centred on home and family.

Remember it like this: Between the waves, picture three snapshots: activists quarrelling over the ERA, Rosie the Riveter in a wartime factory, and a mother pushing a pram in the baby boom.

Chapter 11 · The second wave (1960s–80s)

In the 1960s a second wave rose. It went beyond the vote to work, family, the body and culture. Its slogan, “the personal is political”, meant that private life was a political matter too.

One landmark was Betty Friedan’s book The Feminine Mystique, in 1963. The same year brought the Equal Pay Act. In 1964 came Title VII, a part of the Civil Rights Act. In 1966 activists founded the National Organization for Women, or NOW.

In 1972 came Title IX, another numbered part of a national law. In 1973 the Supreme Court’s Roe decision recognised a constitutional right to abortion. It was overturned in 2022.

Universities changed too. Women’s studies and women’s history became academic fields, and the first programme opened in 1970.

Remember it like this: The first wave fought for the ballot box. The second wave carried the fight into the office, the kitchen and the lecture hall, under the slogan “the personal is political”.

Chapter 12 · The third and fourth waves (1990s–today)

A third wave rose in the 1990s. It stressed that women are not all alike. Its key idea is intersectionality, a term from Kimberlé Crenshaw in 1989. It looks at how race, class and gender combine in one person’s life.

The word comes from “intersection”, a place where roads cross. Picture a woman standing where the roads of race, class and gender meet. Her life is shaped by all three at once. An Indian reader might think of how caste, class and gender can combine.

A fourth wave began in the 2010s. It is known for online activism, above all the #MeToo campaign of 2017.

Remember it like this: The third wave is a crossroads where race, class and gender meet. The fourth wave is a hashtag: #MeToo.

Chapter 13 · Same or different? A question that keeps coming back

One question keeps coming back in women’s history. Should women be treated the same as men, or should their difference, such as motherhood, be valued and protected?

The first answer is called individualist. The second is called relational or maternalist, from “maternal”, meaning motherly.

Together, the two answers make up the debate over equality and difference (p.8). “Equality” stands for the first answer, and “difference” for the second.

Remember it like this: Equality says “Treat us the same”. Difference says “Value what makes us different, such as motherhood”.

Words to know

Capitalism
Businesses and property are privately owned. Markets set prices, and profit drives what gets made. The classic book is The Wealth of Nations by Adam Smith, published in 1776. Its famous image is the “invisible hand”.
Socialism
Society or the state owns or controls the major industries, to reduce inequality. It ranges from democratic socialism, with elections and a welfare state, to revolutionary forms.
Communism / Marxism
Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels saw history as a class struggle. On one side was the bourgeoisie, who own capital. On the other was the proletariat, who work for wages. They said the struggle would end in revolution and a classless society. The key books are the Communist Manifesto of 1848 and Das Kapital of 1867. Lenin’s USSR practised it from 1917, or 1922 by another count, until 1991. Mao’s China has practised it since 1949.
“Bourgeois”
In Marxist usage, this word means middle-class or capitalist. It is often a put-down. “Bourgeois feminism” means liberal, middle-class feminism (p.30).
Liberalism
Classical liberalism stands for individual rights, limited government and free markets. It is linked with John Locke and J.S. Mill. In US politics today, “liberal” means left-of-centre. Do not mix the two up.
Democracy vs republic
The US is a constitutional federal republic. It is run by elected representatives, so it is also a representative democracy.
Cold War (1947–1991)
The US-led capitalist democracies stood against the Soviet-led communist states. There were proxy wars in Korea, Vietnam and Afghanistan, an arms race and a space race. But the two sides never fought a direct war.
Individualism vs collectivism
The US is the world’s most individualistic culture, the land of the “self-made man”. Many Asian, African and Latin societies define the self through family and group (p.22 and p.31).
Social Darwinism
The misuse of Herbert Spencer’s phrase “survival of the fittest” to justify inequality, laissez-faire economics and racism. It is not Darwin’s biology.

Foundation 5 · How historians argue (the hidden grammar of history writing)

History writing shares a hidden grammar: the ways historians argue with each other. The first stories explain how historians work and how their field changed. Then come six shapes of argument that you will see again and again, and the tone words to notice. The key words are collected at the end.

Chapter 1 · Two kinds of evidence

Picture a historian at a desk with two piles. The first pile holds things made at the time: letters, diaries, census records, wills, court records and newspapers. These are primary sources.

The second pile holds books that historians wrote later. Each one is an interpretation, an explanation of what the old material means. These are secondary sources. A letter written by Gandhi is a primary source. A historian’s book about Gandhi, written later, is a secondary source.

Over the years, many historians write about the same subject, and their views differ. The history of those views, one after another, is called historiography. So “the historiography of the 1920s” means the series of scholarly views on the 1920s.

Remember it like this: Primary was made then. Secondary was written later. Historiography is the history of the history books: how the views changed, one after another.

Chapter 2 · Old view, new view, blended view

Think of a family story. For years the grandparents tell it one way, and everyone goes along with it. Then a grandchild tells it differently. Later, a cousin puts the two versions together. Historians’ views on a subject often change in the same three steps.

The first view, the accepted one, is called the orthodox or traditional view. Then comes a revisionist view, a new interpretation that overturns the accepted one. In academic writing, “revisionist” is a neutral word. It is not an insult.

The third step is the post-revisionist view. It is a synthesis, which means it blends parts of the earlier views into something new.

Remember it like this: Old story, rebel story, blended story: orthodox, revisionist, post-revisionist. In a university, “revisionist” is a plain label, not an insult.

Chapter 3 · History from below (1960s–70s)

History can be told from the top, through kings and presidents. It can also be told from below, through ordinary people such as workers, women, slaves and immigrants.

A new kind of history that looks from below started in the 1960s–70s. It is called social history, or “history from below”. In the same years, women’s history, Black history and ethnic studies also became academic fields.

Social historians often work with quantitative data, which means data in numbers. Census figures and election returns are typical examples. Economic history done with statistics even has its own name: cliometrics.

Remember it like this: History from above watches from the palace balcony. History from below stands in the street and counts the crowd, with census figures and election returns.

Chapter 4 · Two more ways into the past

Some peoples left few written records of their own. Native Americans and the Aztecs are two examples.

To study such peoples, historians combine the documents that exist with the methods of anthropology, the study of human cultures. This mix is called ethnohistory.

Historians also use interviews. Oral history means recorded interviews with eyewitnesses, people who saw events for themselves (see p.4). Picture sitting with your grandparents and recording them as they describe the village where they grew up.

Remember it like this: Ethnohistory is history plus anthropology, for peoples who left few papers of their own. Oral history is history you can hear: press “record” while an eyewitness talks.

Chapter 5 · Two big ideas: agency and ideology

Historians use the word agency for people’s ability to act and shape their own lives. The opposite picture shows people only as passive victims of what others do. Agency is a favourite theme of women’s history and of minority history.

The second big idea is ideology, a system of beliefs that explains or justifies how a society is arranged. Think of the reasons elders give for how a household runs: who cooks, who earns and who decides.

An ideology does that job for a whole society. One example is the “ideology of domesticity”, a set of beliefs about women and the home.

Remember it like this: Agency is being the driver of your own life, not just a passenger. An ideology is the set of reasons a society gives for who sits where.

Chapter 6 · Time, culture and eras

Scholars often warn against anachronism, also called presentism. It means projecting today’s categories onto the past, or onto other cultures. In plain words, it is sorting the past, or another culture, into today’s boxes.

A related word is ethnocentrism: judging other cultures by your own culture’s standards (see p.37). Here the measuring stick is your own culture rather than the present day.

Historians also divide the past into eras, such as the “Renaissance” or the “Progressive Era”. This is called periodization. Feminists asked whether these eras mean the same thing for women (see p.23).

Remember it like this: Anachronism is a Mughal emperor checking a wristwatch, with today’s ideas in place of the watch. Ethnocentrism is judging cricket by football rules. Periodization is where history’s chapter breaks go.

Chapter 7 · Six shapes of argument, part one

Now for the arguments themselves. You will see six shapes of argument again and again. Here are the first three.

The first shape is old view, new evidence, revision. It sounds like this: “Historians long assumed X, but newly found records suggest Y.”

The second shape is two camps, then the writer’s own position. Two groups of scholars disagree. The writer may side with one camp, find both partly right, or find neither good enough.

The third shape is part, not all. A theory explains part of a phenomenon, something that happens, but not all of it. Something else is needed to explain the rest.

Remember it like this: Shape 1 is a court case reopened by fresh evidence. Shape 2 is a judge weighing two lawyers. Shape 3 is a key that opens some doors but not all.

Chapter 8 · Six shapes of argument, part two

The fourth shape is methodological caution, a warning about method. A source or method has hidden limits or biases, which tilt the picture one way. A memoir, for example, may be written to justify its writer. A sample may over-represent one group, which means it includes too many people from that group.

The fifth shape is irony or paradox. Something produces the opposite of what you would expect, or the opposite of what it was meant to do.

The sixth shape is the scope check. The writer accepts a claim but limits how far it goes. It sounds like this: “X is right that …, but …”.

This guide deliberately does not say which reading follows which shape. Spotting the shape yourself is part of the skill. And each reading must still be judged by its own words.

Remember it like this: A memoir can be a selfie: the writer chose the angle. Irony is a medicine that makes the illness worse. A scope check says “yes, but only this far”.

Chapter 9 · Reading the tone

Last, notice the tone. Six tone words are worth knowing well.

Qualified means partly agreeing. Tentative means hesitant and not yet sure. Sceptical means doubtful. Dismissive means brushing an idea aside as not worth serious attention.

Revisionist, as in Chapter 2, means overturning the accepted view. Nuanced means alert to fine differences, not black and white.

Academic authors are almost never extreme. Their tone is usually “qualified” or “moderate”.

Remember it like this: Think of tone as spice. Academic writers almost always cook it medium: rarely fiery, rarely sugary. So expect “qualified” or “moderate”.

Words to know

Historiography
The history of how historians have interpreted a subject. “The historiography of the 1920s” means the series of scholarly views on the 1920s.
Primary vs secondary source
Primary sources were made at the time: letters, diaries, census records, wills, court records and newspapers. Secondary sources are later interpretations, such as books by historians.
Revisionist
A new interpretation that overturns the accepted one. In academic writing the word is neutral, not an insult. The sequence is “orthodox” or “traditional”, then “revisionist”, then “post-revisionist”, a synthesis.
Social history / “history from below”
History that started in the 1960s–70s. It studies ordinary people, such as workers, women, slaves and immigrants, rather than kings and presidents. It often uses quantitative data, such as census figures and election returns. Economic history done with statistics is called “cliometrics”.
Ethnohistory
Using documents plus anthropology to study peoples who left few written records of their own, such as Native Americans and the Aztecs.
Oral history
Recorded interviews with eyewitnesses (p.4).
Agency
People’s ability to act and shape their lives, rather than being passive victims. It is a favourite theme of women’s and minority history.
Ideology
A system of beliefs that explains or justifies how a society is arranged, for example the “ideology of domesticity”.
Anachronism / presentism
Projecting today’s categories onto the past or onto other cultures. Scholars often warn against it.
Ethnocentrism
Judging other cultures by your own culture’s standards (p.37).
Periodization
How history is divided into eras, such as the “Renaissance” or the “Progressive Era”. Feminists asked whether these eras mean the same thing for women (p.23).

Foundation 6 · School-science toolkit

In American high schools, students take biology, chemistry and physics, and often earth and space science too. The science readings assume you already know the ideas in this toolkit, so here they are as short stories.

Chapter 1 · The detective’s method

A scientist works much like a detective. Picture a detective at a crime scene, studying the clues and naming a suspect. In science, that suspect is a hypothesis: a proposed explanation that can be tested.

A good suspicion can be checked. Some clue, such as a solid alibi, could clear the suspect. Science has the same rule: a claim counts as scientific only if some observation could prove it wrong. Such a claim is falsifiable, or testable.

Sometimes the tools for a test do not exist yet. The Milankovitch theory, for example, was “untestable” until dating methods, ways of telling how old something is, appeared (p.67).

Next comes the test itself. An experiment changes one thing, the variable, and compares the result with a control group that does not get the change. Think of two pots of dal cooked the same way, with a new spice added to only one. The plain pot is the control, so any difference in taste comes from the spice.

Do not confuse a hypothesis with a theory. In everyday English, “theory” can mean a guess. In science, a theory is a broad explanation that has been well tested, such as evolution or gravity. If a hypothesis is one suspect, a theory is the full story of the case, checked against clue after clue.

Remember it like this: A hypothesis is a suspect, and a fair suspect can be cleared by an alibi. The control is the pot of dal without the new spice.

Chapter 2 · When clues mislead

Some clues mislead. A man seen near two crime scenes may simply live nearby. In the same way, two things that move together show a correlation. That does not prove causation, that one of them causes the other.

Think of the monsoon. Umbrella sales and muddy roads rise together, yet umbrellas do not make roads muddy. The rain drives both.

A detective may also replay the crime: if the thief left at nine, the footprints should point this way. Scientists do the same with a computer model, or simulation. It works out what should happen under a set of assumptions. You will meet a model of a meteor stream as a “pipe” (p.69) and one of asteroid collisions (p.72).

Then there is the clue that does not fit, like a dog that stays silent when it should bark. An observation that departs from what was expected is an anomaly. Anomalies often lead to a new theory.

Last, a good detective asks whether the clues themselves are lopsided. Perhaps only some witnesses were questioned, or rain washed away some footprints. In science, such a lopsided picture is bias: a systematic, one-sided distortion in the data. Sampling bias comes from how the data were gathered, and preservational bias from what happened to survive (p.82).

Remember it like this: Umbrellas and muddy roads rise together, but the rain causes both: correlation is not causation. An anomaly is the dog that does not bark.

Chapter 3 · The cell and its recipe book

The cell is the basic unit of life, just as a brick is the basic unit of a wall. A nucleus is a walled-off inner core inside a cell. Prokaryotes, such as bacteria and archaea, have no nucleus.

Plants, animals, fungi and protists are eukaryotes. Their cells do have a nucleus. They also have organelles, small working parts inside the cell, such as mitochondria.

Cells carry DNA, which works like a master recipe book. A gene is one recipe: a stretch of DNA that codes for a protein. The cell copies a recipe into RNA, a working copy, and uses that copy to build the protein. So the instructions pass from DNA to RNA to protein.

A mutation is a change in the DNA, like a change in the wording of a recipe. Proteins are chains built from 20 kinds of amino acids, like beads on a string. Enzymes, which speed up chemical reactions in living things, are proteins too.

Remember it like this: DNA is the recipe book and a gene is one recipe. RNA is a photocopy taken to the kitchen, and the protein is the finished dish.

Chapter 4 · Evolution by natural selection (1859)

Darwin and Wallace put forward the idea of evolution by natural selection, and Darwin’s 1859 book made it famous. It explains how living things change over many generations, and it needs three ingredients.

First, members of a species differ from one another. This is variation. Second, parents pass traits to their young. This is heredity.

Third, some individuals survive and reproduce more than others. Over many generations, helpful traits become more common, and living things become better suited to their surroundings. This fit is called adaptation. A farmer who saves seed only from the best plants improves a crop in a similar way, but in nature no farmer does the choosing.

In everyday English, “fit” means strong or healthy. In biology, fitness means reproductive success: how many young a living thing leaves behind. Some traits spread because they help win a mate, either because mates prefer them or because they help beat rivals. This is sexual selection.

Biologists also sort living things into nested groups. This is taxonomy. The ranks run from the broadest to the narrowest: domain, kingdom, phylum, class, order, family, genus and species. A species is one kind of living thing, and humans, for example, are the species Homo sapiens.

Remember it like this: In biology, fitness is counted in babies, not muscles. Taxonomy is life’s postal address: it narrows from domain down to species, as an address narrows from country down to one house.

Chapter 5 · Who eats whom, and who keeps warm

Ecology studies how living things relate to one another and to their surroundings. A food web maps who eats whom. Predators are the hunters, and prey are the hunted.

Each feeding step in the web is a trophic level: plants, then plant-eaters, then the meat-eaters that eat them. A species’ niche is its role and way of life, much like a person’s trade in a village. A population is all the members of one species in one area.

Ecologists use two more labels. A keystone species is named after the keystone, the top stone that locks an arch together. An invasive species is a newcomer that spreads into a place where it did not live before.

Animals also differ in where their body heat comes from. Mammals and birds are endotherms, the “warm-blooded” animals, and they make their own heat. Reptiles, amphibians and fish are ectotherms, the “cold-blooded” ones, and they depend on heat from outside. Endo means inside and ecto means outside.

Remember it like this: A food web is the menu of the wild, showing who eats whom. Endotherms run their own heater, while ectotherms warm up in the heat around them.

Chapter 6 · A train through deep time (4.54 billion–150 million years ago)

Picture a train that sets off when Earth forms and runs all the way to today. The dates on this line are so old that we count them in millions and billions of years, so scientists call this huge span deep time. Geologists split it into named stretches, much as historians split Indian history into ancient, medieval and modern times. The full list of names and dates is the geologic timescale.

The first station is 4.54 billion years ago, when Earth forms. Life appears by about 3.5 billion years ago or earlier, as cells much like bacteria. This train stops only at a few big stations, so it now skips far ahead.

About 540 million years ago comes the Cambrian explosion, when most animal groups appear. Conodonts first appear a little later, about 520 million years ago (p.84).

At 252 million years ago the train passes the Permian extinction, the largest extinction ever. It opens the Mesozoic, the “age of dinosaurs”, which has three parts: the Triassic, then the Jurassic, then the Cretaceous. About 150 million years ago, in the Late Jurassic, comes Archaeopteryx, the oldest known bird (pp.79–80).

Remember it like this: Earth forms at 4.54 billion, most animal groups appear at 540 million, and the largest extinction strikes at 252 million. Then the age of dinosaurs pulls three coaches: Triassic, Jurassic, Cretaceous.

Chapter 7 · The train reaches our time (66 million years ago–today)

At 66 million years ago the train passes a disaster. An asteroid strikes at Chicxulub, in Mexico, and ends the non-bird dinosaurs. This begins the Cenozoic, the “age of mammals”.

About 7–6 million years ago, the family line that leads to humans and the one that leads to chimps split apart. Scientists call such family lines lineages. Later, the australopithecines, early human relatives, live about 4.2–2 million years ago (p.83).

From 2.6 million to 11,700 years ago runs the Pleistocene, the “Ice Age”. It brings repeated glaciations, long cold spells when great sheets of ice spread across the land (p.67). It is also the age of the megafauna, very large animals such as mammoths and giant sloths. Fossils from this age lie in the La Brea tar pits (p.82).

About 300,000 years ago, Homo sapiens, our own species, appears in Africa. The last stretch of the line is the Holocene, the last 11,700 years. It brings farming and civilisation, and we still live in it today.

Remember it like this: The non-bird dinosaurs get off at 66 million. Our line parts from the chimps at 7–6 million, and Homo sapiens boards only about 300,000 years ago, near the end of the line.

Chapter 8 · Locks and keys in the brain

A neuron is a nerve cell, and neurons pass messages from one to the next. Where two neurons meet, a tiny gap separates them. This meeting point is called the synapse.

To cross the gap, the first neuron releases a chemical messenger called a neurotransmitter. The next cell carries receptors, and the messenger fits a receptor the way a key fits a lock. So the message travels from a neuron, across the synapse, carried by a neurotransmitter, to a receptor.

Other chemicals can fit the same locks. An agonist is a key that fits and turns, so it switches the receptor on. An antagonist blocks the receptor instead, like a wrong key that fits but will not turn.

A wrong key stuck in a lock also keeps the right key out. Caffeine works this way: it is an antagonist that blocks adenosine (p.60).

Remember it like this: The neurotransmitter is the key and the receptor is its lock. An agonist turns the lock. An antagonist, such as caffeine, jams it so the right key cannot get in.

Chapter 9 · Gravity and light (1687–1915)

In 1687 Newton set out his law of gravity: every mass attracts every other mass. An orbit is the path one body follows around another, as the Moon circles Earth.

Orbits hold a useful secret. How fast something orbits reveals how much mass lies inside its orbit. This is the key trick behind the study of double stars, black holes and dark matter (pp.65, 68, 73).

Then came Einstein. In 1905 he put forward special relativity, which says that nothing travels faster than light. Light covers about 300,000 km every second, a speed that physicists write as c.

In 1915 his general relativity said that gravity bends light, much as a glass lens bends light. This effect is called lensing (p.76).

Remember it like this: Newton: every mass pulls every other. Orbits: speed gives away the mass inside. Einstein: light sets the speed limit, and gravity bends light like a lens.

Chapter 10 · A journey out into space

Now take a second journey, this time outward from Earth. The first stop is the Moon, about 384,000 km away. Beyond that, distances grow so large that kilometres stop being handy.

So astronomers describe distance by how long light takes to cover it, just as we say a town is “two hours away”. The Sun is 8 light-minutes away, because its light takes about 8 minutes to reach us. A light-year is likewise a distance, not a time: how far light travels in one year.

The nearest star beyond the Sun is 4.2 light-years away. Our own galaxy, the Milky Way, is about 100,000 light-years across and holds between 100 and 400 billion stars.

Further out lies Andromeda, another galaxy, 2.5 million light-years away. The light we see from it set out about 2.5 million years ago. The journey ends with the biggest number of all: the universe itself is about 13.8 billion years old.

Remember it like this: A light-year is a distance, like a town “two hours away”. Light from Andromeda is a letter posted 2.5 million years ago, and we are only reading it now.

Chapter 11 · Inside the atom

An element is a basic substance, such as oxygen or carbon. Neutrons are tiny particles found at the centre of atoms.

Atoms of the same element can hold different numbers of neutrons. These different versions are called isotopes. Oxygen-16 and oxygen-18, for example, are two isotopes of oxygen (p.67). Carbon-14, an isotope of carbon, gives its name to carbon-14 dating, a method of working out ages.

Quantum mechanics describes the world at the scale of atoms. There, particles behave like waves of probability, and the wave shows how likely a particle is to be found in each place. One surprising result is tunnelling: a particle can pass through a barrier that seems impossible to cross (p.77).

Remember it like this: Isotopes are like two tiffin boxes of the same make, one packed with more rotis: same element, more neutrons. At atomic scale, particles act like waves of chance.

Words to know

Hypothesis vs theory
A hypothesis is a proposed explanation that can be tested. A scientific theory is a broad explanation that has been well tested, such as evolution or gravity. It is not a “guess”.
Falsifiable / testable
A claim is scientific only if some observation could prove it wrong. The Milankovitch theory was “untestable” until dating methods appeared (p.67).
Control / variable
An experiment changes one variable and compares the result with a control group.
Correlation ≠ causation
When two things move together, that does not prove that one causes the other.
Model / simulation
A computer model works out what should happen under a set of assumptions. Examples are the meteor stream “pipe” (p.69) and asteroid collisions (p.72).
Anomaly
An observation that departs from what was expected. Anomalies often lead to a new theory.
Bias (sampling, preservational)
A systematic distortion in the data (p.82 rules out several biases).

Topic 1 · Women / Feminism 28 readings · pp.4–31

This is the biggest group of readings in this guide. Nearly all of them deal with the history of American women, as scholars wrote it between the 1970s and the 2000s. Before you start, read Foundation 4 again for coverture, separate spheres and the “waves” of feminism. Many readings set the views of earlier scholars against newer work, so keep track of who holds which view.

p.4Women’s oral narratives as historical evidencehistoriography · oral history · WWII · second-wave feminism

In short: Feminist theory treats women’s oral narratives, or spoken life stories, along with written autobiography, as the method that comes closest to the “reality” of women’s lives. The author warns that oral accounts are no more disinterested, or impartial, than written ones. They are shaped by the usual rules of storytelling, and by cultural and political forces the speaker may not notice.

The story

For a long time, history books were about the public lives of men: wars and governments. Then, in the late 1960s and the 1970s, came second-wave feminism, the women’s movement of those years. Its slogan was “the personal is political”, meaning that private life is political too. That slogan is why feminist theory values women’s own stories.

Alongside the movement, women’s history became a proper academic field. Because the old history was about men in public life, its scholars turned to new sources: diaries, letters and interviews. Oral history means recorded interviews with people who saw events for themselves. Think of recording a grandparent’s memories of Partition before they fade.

In America, Studs Terkel made oral history popular, for example with his book “The Good War” in 1984. Other projects interviewed women who had worked in factories during the Second World War. In that war, millions of men were in uniform. About 6 million more women took paid jobs, many in defence plants, the factories that made war goods.

Government posters, with “Rosie the Riveter” and the slogan “We Can Do It!”, made this work look glamorous. After 1945, most of these women were laid off or pushed back into the home. Today every American knows Rosie, and the familiar story that the war “brought women into the workforce”.

Remember it like this: A disinterested umpire has nothing riding on the match, so he can judge fairly. An uninterested umpire is simply bored. Many people mix up the two.

Words to know

Disinterested
Impartial, without bias. It does not mean “uninterested”. Mixing the two up is a classic vocabulary mistake.
Methodology
The method or type of evidence a scholar uses.
At face value
Taken just as it appears, without checking it closely.
Political rhetoric
Public language meant to persuade, such as wartime propaganda.

Reading tip: Name the job of each part of the text in your own words. A fair one-line summary of the whole must cover every part, and its verb should be no stronger than what the author actually does.

p.5Women’s suffrage and the 1920s19th Amendment · Jane Addams · 1920s · ERA

In short: Recent feminist scholars reject the old verdict that suffrage, women’s right to vote, had failed. That verdict rested on two things. No women’s voting bloc appeared, and women’s votes did not produce moral government free of corruption. Yet in some ways these scholars still describe the late 1920s as a decline: the movement lost its unity and won few laws. The excerpt breaks off at that point.

The story

In August 1920, the 19th Amendment was added to the US Constitution. It forbids denying anyone the vote “on account of sex”. It ended a 72-year campaign that began at Seneca Falls in 1848. Its leaders included Susan B. Anthony, Elizabeth Cady Stanton, Carrie Chapman Catt and Alice Paul.

The campaigners, called suffragists, had made a promise. Women would vote together as a moral bloc, a women’s “vote bank”, as Indians might say. They would clean up politics, ending corruption and closing the saloons, or bars. The promise was tied to the temperance movement against alcohol, and to Prohibition, the national ban on alcohol that came in 1919.

In fact, once women could vote, they voted much like the men of their own class, region and party. So critics declared suffrage a “failure”. Jane Addams, a leading Progressive reformer who founded Hull House in Chicago, made a quip about this charge. Its point was that nobody asks whether men’s suffrage has failed.

The early 1920s brought some wins. The Sheppard–Towner Act of 1921 gave federal money for the health of mothers and babies, until it lapsed in 1929. The Cable Act of 1922 gave married women their own citizenship.

After about 1925, the mood turned conservative and pro-business, and reformers faced “Red Scare” attacks, born of a fear of communism. Reformers also split over the ERA, the Equal Rights Amendment proposed by Alice Paul in 1923. Some backed it, and others backed protective labour laws for women.

Today, Americans learn that 1920 is the year women got the vote. They know Susan B. Anthony, who was once on a dollar coin, and the “flapper”, the popular image of the 1920s. She stood for a younger generation that saw organised feminism as old-fashioned. American campaigners called themselves “suffragists”, and “suffragette” was mainly a British word.

Remember it like this: “19 in ’20”: the 19th Amendment gave women the vote in 1920. Americans who fought for it were suffragists. The “-ette” word, suffragette, was mainly British.

Words to know

Suffrage / franchise
The right to vote. To “enfranchise” someone is to give them the vote.
Voting bloc
A group that votes together as a unit.
Gender consciousness
Women’s awareness that they share interests as women.

Reading tip: There are three positions: the old view (failure), the new scholarship (not a failure, but decline after the mid-1920s), and the author, whose own position you must infer from how the text treats that scholarship. Keep them separate.

p.6Antebellum Virginia women and party politicsantebellum · Second Party System · Whigs · 1840 campaign

In short: Political historians, who study voting records and newspapers, give women little attention because women could not vote. Women’s historians, who study diaries, wills and associations, show little interest in party politics. Most historians underestimate women’s party loyalty. Virginia’s Whigs tried to win over women at rallies in the 1840s. They said that politically informed women would raise families loyal to the party. The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

Antebellum means “before the war”. Americans use it for the years from about 1815 to 1861, before the Civil War. In those years, two big parties fought for power. Historians call this the Second Party System, and it ran from about 1828 to 1854.

On one side stood the Democrats of Andrew Jackson, who spoke for farmers, states’ rights and the “common man”. On the other stood the Whigs, founded in 1834 to oppose “King Andrew”. They backed Henry Clay’s plan of tariffs, or taxes on imports, plus a national bank and new roads and canals. They were strong among middle-class, evangelical Protestant reformers.

In 1840, William Henry Harrison ran the “Log Cabin and Hard Cider” campaign, with the slogan “Tippecanoe and Tyler Too”. It was the first modern mass campaign, with parades, songs, barbecues and huge rallies, much like an Indian election season. About 80% of eligible voters turned out, and women came to the rallies and sewed banners. This was the “new American political nation”.

By the 1840s nearly all white men could vote. Women, enslaved people and most free Black men could not. The organised “woman’s rights movement” began at Seneca Falls in 1848. Whig “female politics” was different: women took part as moral supporters of a party and as influencers, not as people seeking the vote.

The Whigs fell apart over slavery in the mid-1850s, and the Republican Party replaced them. Today Americans know Andrew Jackson, whose face is on the $20 bill, but they know the Whigs only vaguely. In school textbooks, the 1840 campaign is a standard “first” of mass politics.

Remember it like this: Antebellum comes from Latin: ante means “before” and bellum means “war”. In those years, Democrats were the party of Jackson and the “common man”. Whigs were the party of Clay, with roads, canals and a bank.

Words to know

Partisan
Loyal to a political party.
Propaganda
Material spread to promote a cause. Here it means the Whig party’s own publicity.
Allegiance
Loyalty.

Reading tip: To judge what a group of scholars would agree with, find the sentence where the author discusses them and stick to its exact claim.

p.7The cult of female domesticity & the ideology of “female benevolence”separate spheres · 19th-century reform · coverture · philanthropy

In short: Nineteenth-century Americans believed that women were morally superior. This idea was at the core of the cult of domesticity. Ironically, this “female benevolence” let women leave the home for prisons, hospitals, battlefields and slums. Some came to hold real authority over a community’s resources.
