Their actual work was businesslike. They got legal charters for their societies and raised money. The law limited their right to control money and property, so they found clever legal ways to run and fund their charity work. Some clung to the sentimental image, because it gave their real authority an air of altruism, or selflessness. The excerpt breaks off just as the text begins to say what clinging to it also prevented.

The story

Picture America in the 1820s. Industry is growing, and with it a new middle class. Paid work is moving out of the home. So people come to see the home and the world of paid work as separate spheres.

In 1966 the historian Barbara Welter gave this age’s ideal of womanhood a name: the cult of true womanhood, also called the cult of domesticity. The true woman was pious, or deeply religious, and pure. She was also submissive, or obedient, and domestic, devoted to her home.

Benevolent work, or organised charity, took many forms. Volunteers ran orphan homes and Sunday schools, campaigned against prostitution, and fought alcohol through the temperance movement. The Women’s Christian Temperance Union was founded in 1874.

Others worked for abolition, the ending of slavery, or for relief in the Civil War. In that war, a giant civilian relief agency, the US Sanitary Commission, ran largely on women’s volunteer labour.

The law set limits. Under coverture, a married woman could not own property or sign contracts. So charitable societies got charters, acts of state legislatures that made them legal bodies, much like registering a society or a trust in India. They also used men as trustees, or chose single women and widows as officers.

Today, students in APUSH, an advanced US history course in high school, memorise a sequence. First came Republican Motherhood, the duty of mothers to raise good citizens, then the Cult of Domesticity, then Seneca Falls. They also learn about two Civil War nurses. Clara Barton founded the American Red Cross in 1881, and Dorothea Dix also reformed asylums for the mentally ill.

Remember it like this: Turn the four virtues into four orders: pray, stay pure, obey, stay home. That was the “true woman” of the cult of domesticity.

Words to know

Benevolence / philanthropy
Charitable good works and organised charity.
Charter
A legal grant that creates a company or a society.
Altruism
Selfless concern for others.
Tenaciously
Firmly, refusing to let go.

Reading tip: Signal words (“Moreover”, “In contrast”, “But”) show how each sentence relates to the one before, so mark them as you read.

p.8“Relational” vs “individualist” feminismequality vs difference · Anglo-American rights tradition · Karen Offen

In short: Western feminism has used two ways of arguing. Relational feminism argues for “equality in difference”, which means equity, not sameness. In its view, biology creates a division of labour between the sexes, and motherhood is undervalued. Individualist feminism stresses individual rights and autonomy, or independence, and plays down gender roles. The two existed side by side until the late 19th century. Between 1890 and 1920, relational feminism, still dominant in continental Europe and outside the West, lost ground in England and the US. The excerpt breaks off as the text begins to explain why.

The story

Feminists have long argued over one question. Should women be treated the same as men? Or should their differences, such as motherhood, be valued and protected? This is the equality-or-difference debate.

England and America share a long tradition of rights. It runs from Magna Carta in 1215, through the English Bill of Rights of 1689, to the US Declaration of Independence in 1776. Along the way, John Locke argued that every person has natural rights to life, liberty and property. This is the Anglo-American rights tradition.

Feminists in this tradition argued that women are individuals with the same rights as men. Mary Wollstonecraft made the case in A Vindication of the Rights of Woman in 1792. At Seneca Falls in 1848, campaigners declared that “all men and women are created equal”. J.S. Mill followed with The Subjection of Women in 1869.

On the continent of Europe, especially in France, another tradition stressed the family, motherhood and a woman’s duty to the nation. It led to maternalist policies that support mothers, such as paid maternity leave and family allowances. Europe has such policies, and the US largely lacks them. The US has no national paid maternity leave, and a 1993 law, the FMLA, guarantees only unpaid leave.

The same split came back in America in the 1920s. Supporters of the ERA, the Equal Rights Amendment, wanted women treated the same as men. Supporters of protective labour laws wanted special rules for women workers, such as limits on their hours. Today, Americans tend to assume that “equal rights” is the only kind of feminism.

Remember it like this: Equality is a school uniform: everyone wears exactly the same thing. Equity is a tailor: each person’s clothes are cut to their own size.

Words to know

Equity vs equality
Equity is fairness, which may mean treating people differently. Equality is treating everyone the same.
Procreative labour
Having and raising children, seen as work.
Autonomy
Self-rule, independence.

Reading tip: Pin down each school’s definition precisely, because it is easy to swap features between the two.

p.9Florentine textile workers, human capital theory & “overcrowding”economics · gender wage gap · 17th-century Italy · supply & demand

In short: Human capital theory explains why women in 1600s Florence held low-skill jobs. Having children interrupted their working lives, so they learned fewer skills. The theory also explains why they chose jobs that could be done at home, such as weaving. But it can’t explain the pay gap between two jobs that were both low-skill, such as men in building work and women weaving taffeta. That gap comes from splitting jobs by gender. So few jobs were open to women that their trades were overcrowded, which pushed women’s pay down and men’s pay up.

The story

Picture Florence in the 1600s. Its golden age, the Renaissance, is over, and the Medici family now rule the city as Grand Dukes. But Florence still lives on cloth. Its wool and silk sell all over Europe. Two powerful guilds, one for wool and one for silk, decide who may learn the trade, who may join it, and what counts as good work.

Making fine cloth takes several steps. First, workers comb or card the raw fibre to untangle it. Spinners then twist it into yarn, and weavers turn the yarn into cloth on a loom. Last, the cloth is finished and dyed. One luxury product is taffeta, a crisp, smooth silk.

Not all of this work needs a workshop. Under the putting-out system, a merchant drops off raw material at a worker’s home and later pays for each finished piece. So weaving can be done at home.

Now jump forward three hundred years. Economists want to know why women earn less than men, and two answers stand out. The first is human capital theory, made famous by the American economist Gary Becker, who won the Nobel Prize in 1992. It treats skills like savings in a bank: the more training you put in, the more you earn later. A worker who expects long breaks from work puts in less, so she earns less.

The second answer is crowding, an idea of the economist Barbara Bergmann in the 1970s. Imagine a town where women may enter only a handful of trades. They all pile into those few jobs, so employers can pay them less. Men’s trades face less competition, so they pay more. It is simple supply and demand.

American economics students still meet these two answers when they study today’s gender pay gap. One side stresses choices and skills. The other stresses segregation and discrimination.

Remember it like this: Human capital is a savings account of skills: put more in, earn more later. Crowding is twenty tomato sellers squeezed into one small market: everyone has to sell cheap.

Words to know

Human capital
The skills, education and experience that make a worker productive. Schooling and training are “investments” in it that should raise pay later.
Occupational segregation
Men and women, or other groups, working in different jobs.
Crowding
Squeezing too many workers into a few jobs, which drives pay down in those jobs.
Guild
A medieval or early-modern club of craftsmen or merchants that controlled training, quality, and who could join a trade.
Putting-out system
The pre-factory system: merchants gave workers raw materials at home and paid them for each finished piece.
Piece rate
Pay for each item made, not for each hour worked.

Reading tip: Note exactly what the text says each theory explains, and use only that.

p.10Sarah Eisenstein: working women’s consciousness, 1890–WWIlabour history · ideology of domesticity · Triangle fire · Progressive Era

In short: The late Sarah Eisenstein left some essays unfinished. In them, she argued that working women at the turn of the century neither fully accepted nor rejected the dominant “ideology of domesticity”. In her view, they adapted this ideology to their own experience, and they did the same with feminism, socialism and trade unionism. So, she held, wage work helped to produce a new “consciousness”. The excerpt breaks off just as the text says this “to some extent challenges the recent, controversial” view of another scholar.

The story

Sarah Eisenstein lived from 1946 to 1978. She died young, and her work was left unfinished. Her book came out after her death, in 1983. Its full title is Give Us Bread but Give Us Roses: Working Women’s Consciousness in the United States, 1890 to the First World War.

Picture America between 1890 and 1917. Millions of young single women work for wages, and many are immigrants who are Jewish, Italian or Irish. They sew in garment shops, run machines in textile mills, sell in department stores or work as servants in other people’s homes. Think of the young women in garment workshops in Indian cities today.

These years saw famous strikes. In 1909, shirtwaist makers in New York, who sewed women’s blouses, walked out in the “Uprising of the 20,000”. In 1912, textile workers in Lawrence, Massachusetts, held the “Bread and Roses” strike. The Women’s Trade Union League, founded in 1903, linked middle-class reformers with the workers.

On 25 March 1911, fire swept through the Triangle Shirtwaist Factory in New York. It killed 146 people, mostly young immigrant women trapped behind locked doors. The disaster led to factory-safety laws. Every US history textbook treats the Triangle fire as a turning point in labour law.

Remember it like this: Count 9, 11, 12: the shirtwaist strike in 1909, the Triangle fire in 1911, and the “Bread and Roses” strike in 1912.

Words to know

Ideology of domesticity
The belief that a woman’s proper place, and her fulfilment, lie in home, marriage and motherhood.
Consciousness
In Marxist and feminist writing, awareness of the position and interests one shares with others, as in “class consciousness” or “gender consciousness”.
Trade unionism
Organising workers into unions.

Reading tip: Several positions are in play: the dominant ideology, Eisenstein’s reading, and the controversial view the text turns to. Note the exact degree of agreement or disagreement between them, because the meaning often turns on qualifiers.

p.11Female service workers & the persistence of sex segregation at workservice work · “female jobs” · sex segregation · “unfinished revolution”

In short: Early labour historians studied women in factories. They assumed that industry was blind to gender and set women free. They largely ignored service workers such as salesclerks, servants and secretaries. Yet sex segregation, the division of jobs by sex, survived even in industry. Historians now stress how the prevailing idea of femininity often decides which jobs go to women, even when that no longer fits new conditions. The excerpt breaks off at “For instance,” just before the examples.

The story

Picture Lowell, Massachusetts, between the 1820s and the 1840s. It was America’s first big factory town. Young women from New England farms, the mill girls, ran its textile machines. They lived in company boarding houses, a bit like a college hostel attached to the mill.

From about 1880 to 1930, the service sector, work that sells services rather than goods, grew fast. Department stores filled with “shopgirls”. The typewriter, which arrived in the 1870s, turned office work from a man’s job into a woman’s. By 1900, telephone operators were almost all women.

Yet until about 1940, the largest job for women, especially immigrant and Black women, was domestic service. These women worked as maids, cooks and servants in other people’s homes.

During World War II, when the US was at war from 1941 to 1945, millions of women took jobs in war plants. “Rosie the Riveter” became their symbol. When the men came home, many were laid off, and the baby boom followed.

Marxist and liberal thinkers, such as Friedrich Engels, had expected industrial wage work to emancipate women, or set them free. The gap between that hope and reality is called the “unfinished revolution”.

Today, the Lowell mill girls and Rosie the Riveter are standard textbook images. Many fields are still heavily sex-typed: most nurses and teachers are women, and most engineers are men. This sorting of jobs by sex, called occupational segregation, is a major driver of the gender pay gap.

Remember it like this: Flip through three snapshots of working women: a Lowell mill girl in the 1820s, a shopgirl or typist around 1900, and Rosie the Riveter in the 1940s.

Words to know

Sex-typed job
A job seen as suitable for only one sex, as in “women’s work” or “men’s work”.
Service sector
Work that provides services, not goods, such as sales, office work, domestic and personal service.
Clerical work
Office work such as typing, filing and bookkeeping.
Domestic service
Paid household work done by servants, maids and cooks.
Mill girls
Young New England women who worked in the Lowell textile mills in the 1820s–40s.
Emancipate
To set free from legal, social or economic limits.

Reading tip: Map the two camps: what earlier labour historians assumed and studied, and what newer historians emphasise instead. Then ask what job each example does in the argument.

p.12The 19th-century US census and women’s occupationscensus · household economy · 1850 · antislavery & women’s rights

In short: Census data on women’s work grew more detailed and precise over the 19th century, as society changed. Up to 1840, only the head of each household, presumed to be male, was named. Everyone else was simply counted. This mirrored a home-based farm economy and a social order of higher and lower ranks. In 1850, partly in response to the movements against slavery and for women’s rights, the census began to collect specific information about each person. The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

The US Constitution, in Article I, requires a census, or count of the people, every 10 years. The first was held in 1790. Its purpose is to divide seats in the House of Representatives, America’s Lok Sabha, among the states. Until 1865, an enslaved person counted as only “three-fifths” of a person for that purpose.

From 1790 to 1840, the census named only the head of household. Everyone else in the home was counted by age, sex, and free or enslaved status. The household, not the individual, was the economic unit. A wife’s work, and her children’s, was credited to the head.

The 1850 census was the first to list every free person by name. It recorded each person’s age, sex and birthplace, and for men it also recorded an occupation. Enslaved people went on a separate “slave schedule”, listed under their owners’ names. Their own names were usually left out.

Counting women’s work is still a matter of debate. Today, unpaid housework and care are left out of GDP, the total value of what a country produces. So a homemaker who cooks and cares for a family all day adds nothing to it. Americans fill in the census once a decade, and they learn about the Three-Fifths Compromise in school.

Remember it like this: Three-fifths: when House seats were shared out, every five enslaved people were counted as only three.

Words to know

Enumeration
The act of counting people.
Head of household
The person, presumed to be a man, listed as in charge of a household.
Gainful employment
Paid work, as opposed to unpaid work in the home.

Reading tip: Dates and causes are easy to mix up. Make a quick timeline (through 1840; 1850; later) and attach each reason the text gives to the right date.

p.13Latin American women & urban migrationBrazil · urbanisation · class · exchange networks · patronage

In short: Social scientists claim that migration to cities hurt Latin American women’s status unequivocally, or beyond any doubt. The author says the effects are “more complex than these theorists presume”, and can vary with women’s financial condition and social class. Very poor Brazilian women have relatively more job opportunity and security in cities than poor men do. So they may gain status relative to men without any gain in economic standing. Working outside the home also helps them extend their exchange networks of gifts, loans and childcare. The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence as a further point begins.

The story

Between the 1940s and the 1980s, Latin America urbanised very fast. In other words, its people moved from the countryside into the cities. Today it is about 80% urban, one of the most urbanised regions on Earth. Many of the poor live in large informal settlements, such as Brazil’s favelas, much like the slums of Mumbai.

Poor women who moved to the cities often worked as domestic servants in middle-class homes. In Brazil, this has historically been the largest job for poor women in the cities. Others worked in the informal sector, the world of small, unregistered work such as street vending and laundry. Poor men relied on unstable building work and day labour.

To survive, the poor build reciprocal exchange networks. Relatives and neighbours swap childcare, loans and food, and each favour is returned in time. A classic study of this is Larissa Lomnitz’s 1977 work on a shantytown in Mexico City.

Another kind of tie links people who are not equal. In a patron–client tie, also called clientelism, a richer patron gives loans, jobs or medical help. In return, the poorer client gives loyalty and service. These ties are strengthened by compadrazgo, or godparenthood.

Remember it like this: Exchange networks run sideways, between relatives and neighbours. Patron–client ties run up and down, between a richer patron above and a poorer client below.

Words to know

Status vs economic standing
Status is a person’s position compared with others, here compared with men. Economic standing is a person’s own income or wealth, not compared with anyone.
Socioeconomic class
A person’s position in society, measured by income, job and education.

Reading tip: Notice the exact wording of each claim the text reports and any conditions (class, measures of status) attached to it.

p.14Women in CIO industrial unions (Faue & Gabin)AFL vs CIO · New Deal · UAW · Minneapolis · gender ideology

In short: The CIO’s industrial unions of the 1930s–40s preached nondiscrimination, but women’s roles mirrored the gender ideology of the time. Elizabeth Faue, in her study of Minneapolis, stresses that women were vital at the community level when unions were being built. In her account, as unions became bureaucratic and separated from community politics, that base became increasingly irrelevant, and women were pushed to the margins. The excerpt breaks off as the text turns to Nancy Gabin’s study of CIO unions.

The story

The AFL, the American Federation of Labor, was founded in 1886 and led by Samuel Gompers. It was made up of craft unions, each for skilled tradesmen in one craft, such as carpenters or machinists. It often shut out women, Black workers and the unskilled.

In 1935, John L. Lewis led the founding of the CIO, which began as a Committee and became the Congress of Industrial Organizations in 1938. It built industrial unions: one union for all the workers in an industry, such as autos, steel or rubber, whatever their skill. It was far more inclusive. The two groups merged as the AFL-CIO in 1955.

The Wagner Act of 1935, part of the New Deal, guaranteed workers the right to organise and to bargain collectively. That means negotiating pay and conditions as a group. Union membership roughly quadrupled, to about 14–15 million by 1945.

Two strikes became landmarks. The first was the 1934 strike in Minneapolis by the Teamsters, the truck drivers’ union. There, the local community and the women’s auxiliaries, or support groups, were crucial.

The second was the 1936–37 sit-down strike in Flint by the United Auto Workers, or UAW, against General Motors. The workers sat down inside the plants and refused to leave, a bit like a dharna inside the factory. A Women’s Emergency Brigade supported them.

Remember it like this: Think of the AFL as separate clubs for each craft: one for carpenters, one for machinists. The CIO is one big club for everyone in the car plant, skilled or not.

Words to know

Bureaucratization
Becoming a formal organisation run by rules, with paid staff, contracts and set ways of handling complaints, in place of informal community networks.
Gender ideology
Shared beliefs about the proper roles of men and women. One example is the man as the “breadwinner”, earning a “family wage”.
Seniority
Job rights, such as protection from being laid off, based on how long a worker has served.

Reading tip: Note what each scholar studied (place, period, scope). Anything said to be true of both studies must fit both, so check it against each one.

p.15Women and social classclass theory · coverture · Mary Ryan · intersectionality

In short: Traditional models of class give women the class of their fathers or husbands. Some feminists answered that all women form one class, based on gender. Mary Ryan, for example, argued that in the early 19th century, working-class and middle-class married women had the same legal status: unpaid housework, no property and no vote. In her view, this outweighed their differences. Recently, other feminist analysts have questioned this model by examining how working-class women’s condition differed. The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

What decides a person’s class? For Karl Marx, it was your relation to the means of production, the land, tools and factories used to make goods. You either owned them or worked for those who did. Max Weber added status and power, while American sociologists usually measure socioeconomic status by income, job and education.

Married women also lived under coverture. In 1765 the English legal scholar William Blackstone wrote that “the husband and wife are one person in law”. A wife could not own property, sign contracts, keep her wages or sue.

Married Women’s Property Acts, which began to chip away at coverture, started in Mississippi in 1839 and New York in 1848. No women voted anywhere in the US, except propertied women in New Jersey from 1776 to 1807.

In this period, “free” women means women who were not enslaved. Enslaved women lived in an entirely different condition. Later scholars studied the differences among women, by class, race and ethnicity. This work led to intersectionality, a term coined by Kimberlé Crenshaw in 1989.

Remember it like this: Think of coverture as a merger: husband and wife become one person in law, and only the husband’s name survives.

Words to know

Means of production
The land, tools, factories and capital used to make goods. Marx defined class by whether you own them.
Socioeconomic status (SES)
One measure that combines income, education and job.
Coverture
The common-law rule that a married woman’s legal identity merged into her husband’s. As a “feme covert”, she could not own property, sign contracts, keep her wages or sue.
Intersectionality
How overlapping identities such as race, class and gender combine to shape a person’s experience. Kimberlé Crenshaw named it in 1989.

Reading tip: Keep each group’s view separate (who holds it, what evidence it rests on). It is easy to attach one group’s view to another group.

p.16Aztec women’s production: weaving & cookingAztecs · ethnohistory · women’s work · public/private anachronism

In short: Sixteenth-century documents suggest that Aztec women mostly wove and cooked, largely at home. So modern historians who apply the Western divide between “private” and “public” production may undervalue that work. A closer look, the author says, shows that treating their production that way would be a mistake, beginning with its importance for population growth. The excerpt breaks off as the evidence begins.

The story

In 1325, a people called the Mexica, better known as the Aztecs, founded the city of Tenochtitlan on an island in Lake Texcoco. Today it is Mexico City.

In 1428 they joined two other cities in the Triple Alliance and built a tribute empire over central Mexico. Conquered peoples had to send them goods, much as defeated kings in Indian history paid tribute to an emperor. The Basin of Mexico held 1–2 million people.

In 1521, the Spanish conqueror Hernán Cortés took Tenochtitlan. He had the help of thousands of local allies, the Tlaxcalans, and of smallpox, a deadly disease. The conquered land became “New Spain”.

Ethnohistory studies peoples who left few written records of their own, such as the Aztecs, by combining documents with anthropology. Its documents were written after the conquest by Spanish friars or by native scribes. Two famous ones are the Codex Mendoza, from about 1541, and the Florentine Codex, put together by Bernardino de Sahagún in the 1570s.

At birth, an Aztec girl was given spinning and weaving tools as a symbol, and a boy was given a shield and arrows. Making maize tortillas took hours of grinding every day, much like grinding grain on a stone hand-mill, the chakki, in an old Indian village home.

Remember it like this: Three dates, three pictures: 1325, a city rises on a lake island. 1428, three cities join hands. 1521, Cortés takes the capital.

Words to know

Tribute
Goods that conquered peoples must pay to the ruling state.
Demographic
Relating to population size and growth.
Ethnohistory
The history of peoples who left few written records of their own, pieced together from documents and anthropology.

Reading tip: To judge what the author would agree with, find the sentence that discusses the item and stay close to its wording. Don’t go further than the text does.

p.17History becomes a “scientific” disciplineRanke · “scientific” history · archives · public vs private sphere

In short: In the second half of the 19th century, history changed from a kind of literature into a “scientific” discipline. Women vanished as subjects of history, and the change also had important consequences for women as historians. There were two ironies. First, older works on women relied on secondary sources, which the strict new methods threw out. Second, research in archives did turn up new evidence about women. But historians judged it largely irrelevant, because they saw history as political history and saw women as belonging to the private sphere.

The story

Picture Germany in the 19th century. There, Leopold von Ranke founded modern history based on sources. He insisted on primary sources, records made at the time and kept in archives. He also insisted on critical editions of them, checked and printed with notes. He invented the research seminar and famously aimed to show the past “wie es eigentlich gewesen”, “as it actually was”.

Then history became a profession. Johns Hopkins started a PhD programme in 1876, journals appeared, and the American Historical Association was founded in 1884. E. A. Freeman gave the age its motto, displayed at Johns Hopkins: “History is past politics”. That meant states, wars, diplomacy and constitutions, all male, public spheres, like an old school textbook full of kings and battles.

Before this, many amateur women writers, not trained as historians, had written popular histories and life stories. One example is Agnes Strickland’s Lives of the Queens of England, from the 1840s. Now such books were dismissed as unscientific, and universities rarely hired women.

Remember it like this: A diary written during India’s freedom struggle is a primary source. A history of that struggle written decades later is a secondary source.

Words to know

Primary vs secondary sources
See Foundation 5.
Critical editing
Publishing old documents after experts have checked them, with notes.
Public vs private sphere
The public sphere is politics and work. The private sphere is home and family.

Reading tip: For a phrase such as “elements of irony”, locate each element in the text (“On the one hand …”, “On the other hand …”) and tie it to those exact lines.

p.18Eleanor Roosevelt (1884–1962)FDR · New Deal · biography · women’s reform networks · Joseph Lash

In short: When women’s history took off in the 1970s, Eleanor Roosevelt was one of the few American women whom both historians and the public knew. Yet she was still seen mainly as FDR’s wife, and her work was treated as a complement to his. This was so even though she was active in reform before his presidency and championed causes he did not. Joseph Lash wrote two sympathetic biographies, Eleanor and Franklin in 1971 and Eleanor: The Years Alone in 1972. They showed a complex woman. But they made her look idiosyncratic, a “mother to the nation” or a “busybody”, not a representative social type. The excerpt breaks off there.

The story

Eleanor Roosevelt was born in 1884, the niece of President Theodore Roosevelt. In 1905 she married a distant cousin, Franklin Delano Roosevelt, known as FDR. He was President from 1933 to 1945 and was elected four times. He led the country through the Great Depression and World War II, and he died in office in April 1945.

Eleanor was busy long before the White House. As a young woman she worked in a settlement house, a centre in a poor neighbourhood where middle-class reformers lived and offered help. In 1921 FDR was paralysed by polio. Through the 1920s Eleanor was active in the League of Women Voters, the Women’s Trade Union League and the women’s division of the New York Democratic Party. She also taught school, and she became his political “eyes and ears”.

In 1933 she became First Lady, the President’s wife. She held press conferences for women reporters only, so newspapers had to employ some women. She wrote a daily column, “My Day”, a bit like a public diary. She pushed for civil rights, young people and workers, often ahead of FDR. In 1939 the Daughters of the American Revolution barred the Black singer Marian Anderson from its hall, and Eleanor resigned from the group.

After 1945 she kept working. As a US delegate to the United Nations, she chaired the Human Rights Commission. It drafted the Universal Declaration of Human Rights in 1948, a statement of the basic rights every person should have. Then, in 1961–62, she chaired President John F. Kennedy’s Commission on the Status of Women.

Women’s history itself moved through stages. Early work celebrated exceptional “great women”. Later, social history, the history of ordinary people, asked what made a woman typical of a group or generation.

In America, Eleanor Roosevelt is a household name. In her lifetime she topped Gallup’s poll of the “most admired woman” year after year. FDR’s New Deal, his 1930s programmes of relief, recovery and reform, is a core part of what American students learn.

Remember it like this: FDR: four elections, one Depression, one world war. Eleanor: women’s groups before 1933, “My Day” in the White House, and human rights at the UN after 1945.

Words to know

Idiosyncratic
Peculiar to one person, and not explained by any general pattern.
Social type
A person who is typical of a group or generation.
First Lady
The President’s wife. The role is unofficial but very visible.
New Deal
FDR’s programmes of the 1930s for relief, recovery and reform (see Foundation 1).

Reading tip: Note whose view each sentence reports (the public’s, Lash’s, a later biographer’s). When you sum up one scholar’s view, use only what that scholar is reported to say.

p.19Colonial Connecticut women in court (Cornelia Dayton)colonial law · civil courts · promissory notes · percentages vs numbers

In short: Between 1670 and 1719, women took part in 1 of every 6 civil cases in Connecticut, cases mostly about debt. After 1719 the figure was 1 in 10, and by the 1770s it was 1 in 20. Cornelia Hughes Dayton’s 1995 book Women Before the Bar shows that this picture is deceptive. Both the number and the share of adult women who sued grew. But men’s legal activity grew much faster. Men’s credit networks spread far and wide, partly because promissory notes could be bought and sold. Women’s credit networks stayed local and personal. The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence, just as Dayton begins a conclusion: “although still performing crucial economic services in …”.

The story

Puritans, strict English Christians, founded the colony of Connecticut, starting with Hartford in 1636. In 1639 the settlers wrote down their rules in the Fundamental Orders, often called the first written constitution in America. Dayton’s book starts there and ends in 1789, the year the US Constitution took effect.

A civil case is a private dispute over a debt, a contract or property. In a criminal case, the state prosecutes an offence. Foundation 2 explains more.

Coins were scarce in the colonies, so trade ran on credit. Shopkeepers kept book debts, running accounts in a ledger, much like the khata at an Indian kirana shop. Neighbours also swapped IOUs, short notes that say “I owe you”. So suing a neighbour for debt was routine, not hostile.

A promissory note was a written promise to pay. Such notes were negotiable: the holder could sign one over or sell it to someone else, much as you can endorse a cheque. So credit became impersonal, and it could stretch to distant merchants. It was part of two bigger 18th-century changes: a more commercial Atlantic economy and the rise of professional lawyers.

Marriage decided whether a woman could sue in her own name. A single woman or a widow, a feme sole, could. A married woman, a feme covert, lived under coverture, which merged her legal identity into her husband’s. So she generally could not sue, except as her husband’s agent. The historian Laurel Thatcher Ulrich called such wives “deputy husbands”.

Remember it like this: Feme sole sounds like “solo”: a single woman or widow, who can sue in her own name. Feme covert is “covered” by her husband, so a wife usually cannot.

Words to know

Before the bar
In court. The “bar” is the railing in a courtroom. The word also means the legal profession.
Creditor / debtor
The one who is owed money / the one who owes it.
Far-flung
Spread over great distances.

Reading tip: Don’t confuse a falling percentage with a falling number. A group’s share can shrink while its count grows, if the total grows faster.

p.20The 1917 New York food boycott & the Socialist PartyWWI · cost of living · Socialist Party of America · New York City

In short: In February 1917, thousands of women in New York City boycotted chicken, fish and vegetables to protest against the high cost of living. They acted independently of any party. Food prices had been driven up partly by food exports to Europe since World War I began. The boycott shut much of the city’s food market for two weeks. The Socialist Party was strong in New York City and focused on elections and unions. It quickly organised cost-of-living protests to steer the movement toward Socialist goals. Underneath, though, most Socialists were indifferent to food prices and wanted to channel the energy elsewhere.

The story

World War I began in Europe in August 1914. The US stayed neutral until 6 April 1917, when it declared war, about two months after the New York boycott. In between, the US sold vast amounts of food and munitions to Britain and France, and that helped push up prices at home. Indians know this logic from onions: when large amounts go abroad, prices in the local market rise.

The Socialist Party of America was founded in 1901. Its leader, Eugene V. Debs, won about 6% of the vote for President in 1912. The party was strong among Jewish immigrant garment workers on New York’s Lower East Side. That district even sent a Socialist, Meyer London, to Congress.

The party opposed entering the war, and it was then repressed. In 1918 Debs was convicted under the wartime Espionage Act for an anti-war speech. The Red Scare of 1919–20, a wave of fear of radicals, followed.

The point of production is the workplace, with its strikes and unions. The point of consumption is the shop, where people buy. Marxists saw the workplace, along with elections, as the real arenas of class struggle, the fight between workers and owners. Housewives’ protests as shoppers, such as boycotts and rent strikes, looked secondary to them. That explains the Socialists’ “basic indifference”.

New York housewives had protested like this before. In 1902 Jewish housewives on the Lower East Side boycotted kosher meat, meat prepared under Jewish religious law, much as halal meat is under Muslim law. In 1907–08 came rent strikes, when tenants refused to pay rent. Later in 1917, in November, New York women won the vote in state elections. That was three years before the 19th Amendment gave women the vote nationwide.

Remember it like this: The point of production is the factory gate, where workers strike. The point of consumption is the shop counter, where shoppers boycott.

Words to know

Boycott
A group refusal to buy. The word comes from Captain Charles Boycott, in Ireland, in 1880.
Cost of living
The price of everyday needs such as food, rent and fuel.
Riveting
Grabbing and holding attention.
Political affiliation
Belonging to a party, or siding with one.

Reading tip: Keep each group’s goals and actions separate. Be clear about who did what, and why.

p.21Quaker schools & unmarried women in colonial Philadelphia (Wulf)Quakers · literacy · commonplace books · critique of marriage

In short: Karin Wulf, in her 2000 book Not All Wives, argues that educated young women in colonial Philadelphia, especially Quakers, resisted patriarchal marriage. In her account, they did so by copying poems critical of marriage into their commonplace books and exchanging them. She suggests that Quaker schools spread this critique to poor girls of diverse backgrounds. The author agrees that the critique circulated widely but says that Wulf probably overstates the schools’ role. Real literacy took about 3 years of study. Wulf’s own data for 1765, covering 128 students, imply that only about 17 poor non-Quaker girls studied that long.

The story

The Quakers, or Religious Society of Friends, were founded by George Fox in England in the 1650s. They believe in an “Inner Light” in every person. If everyone carries the same light, then women and men are spiritually equal. So Quaker women could preach, and they ran their own “women’s meetings”. Quakers were also pacifists, who refused to fight in wars, and they were among the first anti-slavery activists.

In 1681 the Quaker William Penn founded Pennsylvania as a “holy experiment” in religious tolerance. Its main city, Philadelphia, was founded in 1682 and nicknamed the “City of Brotherly Love”. By the 1770s it was the largest city in British North America. Like Mumbai today, it was very mixed. Quakers lived beside Anglicans, German sects, Scots-Irish Presbyterians, and free and enslaved Africans.

Quakers wanted both girls and boys to be literate. Their schools kept free “charity” places for poor children and for children who were not Quakers. The Quaker teacher Anthony Benezet, for example, ran schools for girls and for Black children.

Many readers of the 1600s and 1700s kept a commonplace book, a personal notebook for copying out favourite poems, quotations and recipes. Poems also passed among friends in handwritten copies, much as people forward a good poem on their phones today.

Under coverture, marriage ended a woman’s separate legal identity. A single woman or a widow, a feme sole, kept hers. That is why the large group of never-married and widowed women, the women who were “not all wives”, interests historians.

In colonial schools, reading was taught before writing. Many colonial women could read but not write. So “literacy competence” was a real barrier.

Remember it like this: Quakers: one Inner Light in everyone, so women may preach. A commonplace book: a handwritten scrapbook of favourite poems and quotations.

Words to know

Quakers (Religious Society of Friends)
A Protestant group founded in 1650s England. It is known for the “Inner Light”, the spiritual equality of women and men, pacifism and early opposition to slavery.
Commonplace book
A personal notebook for copying out favourite poems, quotations and other passages.
Patriarchy
A social system in which men hold the main authority in the family and in society.
Charity school
A free school for poor children, paid for by donations or by a religious body.
Feme sole
A woman, unmarried or widowed, who kept her own legal identity. She could own property and make contracts.

Reading tip: When an author responds to a named scholar, separate the scholar’s claims from the author’s, and note the evidence offered for each.

p.22Alessandra Strozzi & the Western idea of the “individual”Renaissance Florence · personhood · anthropology vs history

In short: Historians of Renaissance women measure “independence” and “options”. In doing so they assume the Western idea of a person as a bounded, autonomous unit. Anthropologists point out that many societies define a person through a web of relationships. One historian treats the 15th-century Florentine widow Alessandra Strozzi as an individual with goals separate from her sons’. Yet that historian’s own research shows that she mainly championed her sons’ interests. So she fits the anthropologist’s model better.

The story

Picture Florence in the 1400s. The Strozzi are a rich banking family, but in 1434 the powerful Medici exile them. Alessandra Macinghi Strozzi, born about 1407, is widowed young, in the 1430s. She stays in Florence while her sons live in exile, in Naples and Bruges.

About 70 of her letters to them survive. They are a famous source on Renaissance family life: arranging marriages, managing property and lobbying to end the exile. The exile was lifted in 1466, and her son Filippo later built the grand Palazzo Strozzi.

Florentine families of the time were patrilineal: they traced the family line through men. The male line, the family’s honour, dowries and arranged marriages counted for more than any one person’s wishes. A woman even needed a male legal guardian to make a contract.

Historians look for the “individual” because in 1860 Jacob Burckhardt claimed that the Renaissance discovered it (see p.23). The anthropologist Clifford Geertz famously called the Western idea of a “bounded, unique” self “a rather peculiar idea within the context of the world’s cultures”. Cultural psychologists contrast two kinds of self. The independent self, common in the US, stands on its own. The interdependent self, common in East Asia and South Asia, is defined by its relationships.

McKim Marriott described South Asian persons as “dividuals”, a play on “individuals”. The Indian joint family is a good mental model here. In it you are always someone’s son or daughter, nephew or cousin, and your place in that web helps define who you are.

Remember it like this: Picture the independent self as a sealed box, complete on its own. Picture the interdependent self as a knot in a fishing net, held in place by its ties to others.

Words to know

Personhood
What a culture counts as a “person”.
Autonomous
Self-governing, independent.
Intrinsically
By its very nature.
Agency
The ability to act on your own intentions.

Reading tip: Keep separate what the historian assumes, what her research shows, and what the author concludes.

p.23Did women have a Renaissance? Burckhardt vs Joan Kelly vs recent scholarsRenaissance · periodization · Burckhardt · Joan Kelly

In short: Jacob Burckhardt said that Renaissance women “stood on a footing of perfect equality” with men. Feminist scholars cite him before they present evidence of inequality. Joan Kelly argued the opposite in 1977: the Renaissance was a period of decline for women, compared with both Renaissance men and medieval women. Recent feminist scholars reject both the rosy view and the dark one, and they stress differences among women by social status and religion. Both earlier writers had generalised from upper-class Italian women. The middle of the text is missing. The visible ending reports a scholar’s “sober” conclusion: women’s verbal opposition to the ideologies that constrained them was “highly circumscribed”.

The story

The Renaissance, meaning “rebirth”, ran from roughly 1350 to 1600. It revived Greek and Roman learning, a movement called humanism. Famous names include Leonardo, Michelangelo, Petrarch and Erasmus.

The Renaissance began in the Italian city-states, small states each run from a city, such as Florence, Venice and Rome. From there it spread north to England, in the age of Elizabeth I, who reigned 1558–1603, and of Shakespeare. Gutenberg’s printing press, from about 1450, helped it spread.

In 1860 the Swiss historian Jacob Burckhardt published The Civilization of the Renaissance in Italy. That book defined the Renaissance as the birth of the modern individual. His view of women was based on a few highly educated noblewomen of the Italian courts.

In 1977 Joan Kelly wrote the essay “Did Women Have a Renaissance?”, a founding text of women’s history. Her key point concerned periodization, the way we divide history into named eras. An age of “progress” for men, she said, may be one of loss for women. Think of the monsoon: the same rains that bless farmers can flood a city’s commuters.

In Kelly’s view, medieval courtly love, the ideal of a knight devoted to a noble lady, gave aristocratic women some autonomy. The Renaissance court, she argued, made the lady an ornament. Castiglione’s Book of the Courtier (1528) pictures that court.

The querelle des femmes, “the woman question”, was a debate about women’s nature and worth that lasted centuries. Christine de Pizan’s Book of the City of Ladies (1405) is the famous early defence.

Remember it like this: Renaissance means “rebirth”: Greek and Roman learning wakes up in Italy around 1350 and reaches Shakespeare’s England by about 1600.

Words to know

Periodization
Dividing history into named eras.
Circumscribed
Kept within limits, restricted.
Public discourse
Speech and writing that is published or made in public.
Ideology
A system of beliefs that justifies a social order.

Reading tip: There are three positions to keep apart: Burckhardt (rosy), Kelly (dark), and the recent “it depends on which women” view. For each example, ask what job it does in its paragraph.

p.24“Republican motherhood” (Linda Kerber) and Thomas WoodyAmerican Revolution · civic virtue · female education

In short: In the mid-1980s, Linda Kerber’s thesis was that after the Revolution, an ideology of “republican motherhood” led to a surge in women’s education. A republic needed virtuous citizens. Families instilled virtue, and mothers were crucial. So leaders wanted educated mothers to raise virtuous sons. The thesis transformed historiography. Before it, Thomas Woody, writing in 1929, was almost the only historian of education who discussed girls. Using newspaper advertisements for academies, he found that schooling expanded for both girls and boys around 1750. He also saw a shift in attitudes already in “An Essay on Woman” (1753).

The story

The American Revolution began with fighting at Lexington and Concord in 1775. The Declaration of Independence came on 4 July 1776, America’s 15 August. The war ended with the Treaty of Paris in 1783, and the Constitution followed in 1787.

Republicanism, with a small r and no link to the Republican Party, was an 18th-century set of ideas drawn from ancient Rome. It held that a government without a king survives only if its citizens have civic virtue, putting the public good above private interest. Think of a cricket team that wins only if players bat for the side, not for their own records. In this view, luxury and corruption had destroyed Rome and could destroy America too. So raising virtuous children became a political task.

Linda Kerber named this idea republican motherhood. She developed it from the late 1970s into the 1980s, as in her 1980 book Women of the Republic. In this idea, women had no vote but a civic role at home, which justified educating them. Examples include Benjamin Rush’s 1787 Thoughts upon Female Education and Judith Sargent Murray’s 1790 essay “On the Equality of the Sexes”. Two schools are examples too: the Young Ladies’ Academy of Philadelphia, founded in 1787, and Litchfield Female Academy, in 1792.

An academy was a private, fee-paying school. Academies existed long before free public high schools spread after the 1830s, the time of Horace Mann’s movement for free, tax-funded common schools.

In APUSH, the Advanced Placement US History course, students memorise “Republican Motherhood” as a vocabulary term. They often pair it with Abigail Adams, who wrote “Remember the Ladies” in a 1776 letter.

Remember it like this: “Republican” with a small r means “no king”, not the party. Republican motherhood: the mother at home raises the virtuous citizens that a country without a king needs.

Words to know

Republicanism
An 18th-century set of ideas: a republic survives only if its citizens have civic virtue.
Civic virtue
Putting the public good above private interest.
Republican motherhood
Linda Kerber’s term for the idea, after the Revolution, that mothers served the nation by raising virtuous citizens. The idea justified educating women.
Academy
A private school that charged fees. Academies were common before free public high schools spread.
Common school
A free, tax-funded public elementary school. Horace Mann’s movement promoted them in the 1830s–40s.

Reading tip: When a text sets two scholars side by side, note each one’s claim and evidence separately before you compare them.

p.2517th-century English women and the English Civil WarsEnglish Civil Wars · Filmer · patriarchalism · Astell

In short: It is an “odd but indisputable fact” that nearly all 17th-century English women seen as forerunners of feminism sided with the Royalists in the English Civil Wars. Royalism is linked to Robert Filmer’s patriarchalism. In that theory the king rules like a father by divine right, and the father rules the household like a king. This makes it puzzling that Royalist women wrote the first sustained criticisms of wives’ subordination. They also made the first systematic claims of women’s rational and moral equality. Some historians question the “facile” equation of Royalism with Filmer’s patriarchalism. The text adds that the two sides may not have differed consistently on family organisation. The excerpt breaks off there.

The story

The English Civil Wars ran from 1642 to 1651. On one side were the Royalists, nicknamed “Cavaliers”, who supported King Charles I. On the other were the Parliamentarians, nicknamed “Roundheads” and often Puritans, strict English Christians. They fought over taxes, religion and royal power.

Charles I was beheaded in 1649. England then became a republic under Oliver Cromwell. In 1660 the Restoration brought back a king, Charles II. Later, the Glorious Revolution of 1688 and the Bill of Rights of 1689 made England a constitutional monarchy, with the king’s power limited.

Robert Filmer’s book Patriarcha, published in 1680, argued that kings inherit Adam’s fatherly authority from God. This idea is called the divine right of kings.

John Locke replied in 1689 with Two Treatises of Government. The First Treatise attacks Filmer. The Second sets out natural rights and government by consent, ideas that later became the basis of the US Declaration of Independence. Yet Locke still accepted a husband’s rule in marriage as “natural”.

Mary Astell, who lived from 1666 to 1731, was a High-Church Tory in the Royalist tradition. Her best-known lines come from Some Reflections upon Marriage, published in 1700 and again in 1706. One asks: “If absolute Sovereignty be not necessary in a State, how comes it to be so in a Family?” Sovereignty means supreme power. The other asks: “If all Men are born free, how is it that all Women are born Slaves?” Astell is often called the first English feminist.

Remember it like this: 1642–1660 is a round trip: civil war, a beheaded king, Cromwell’s republic, and then a king again.

Words to know

Patriarchalism
The political theory that a ruler’s power is like a father’s.
Absolute monarchy
Rule by a king who is not limited by parliament or law.
Facile
Too easy, shallow.
Forerunner
Someone who came earlier and led the way, a precursor.

Reading tip: Track which views the author merely reports (“historians have been … puzzled”, “Some historians have questioned”) and which the author endorses (“indeed”). When the author brings in an example, reread the sentences around it and name the job it does there.

p.26Progressive Era reformers and child labourProgressive Era · child labour · family economy · working-class families

In short: Women’s grassroots activism drove Progressive Era social reform. White middle-class women were largely unable to vote except in school elections. Even so, they won better working conditions, especially for women and children. Ironically, child-labour laws set women of different classes against each other. Reformers saw child labour and industrial home work as inhumane. But working-class families pooled every member’s wages. They saw strict child-labour laws as a personal economic disaster, so they resisted them.

The story

American textbooks usually date the Progressive Era to about 1890–1920. It was a reform response to industrialisation, growing cities, immigration and corruption. A broader definition runs from the Panic of 1893, a severe depression with mass unemployment, to US entry into World War II. For this reading, keep that broader span in mind.

Journalists called muckrakers exposed wrongdoing. Jacob Riis wrote How the Other Half Lives in 1890, and Upton Sinclair wrote The Jungle in 1906. Other reforms included food-safety laws, four amendments to the Constitution between 1913 and 1920, and Theodore Roosevelt’s trust-busting, the breaking up of giant companies.

Jane Addams ran Hull House, a settlement house in Chicago where reformers lived among poor immigrants and offered them services. Florence Kelley worked with the National Consumers League, from 1899, and the National Child Labor Committee, from 1904. Lewis Hine’s photos of child workers in mills and mines shocked the public. Many states let women vote in school-board elections long before 1920, because schooling was seen as women’s sphere.

In 1900 roughly 1 in 6 children aged 10 to 15 worked for pay. Congress passed a first ban in 1916, but it was struck down in the 1918 case Hammer v. Dagenhart. The Fair Labor Standards Act of 1938 finally prevailed.

In industrial home work, families in crowded tenements finished garments, made artificial flowers or rolled cigars at home. They were paid by the piece, and children helped. In the family wage economy, picture one pot on the kitchen shelf that every member’s earnings go into. In poor households, children’s earnings were a vital share of that pot.

Americans learn the Progressive Era as a major reform period. They usually meet it through the muckrakers, Theodore Roosevelt and Jane Addams.

Remember it like this: Muckrakers rake up the muck. Like investigative reporters today, they dig up the dirt that the powerful want hidden.

Words to know

Progressive Era
A US reform period, usually dated about 1890–1920. It responded to industrialisation, poverty in cities and corruption.
Muckraker
A journalist who exposes corruption and social wrongs.
Settlement house
A community centre in a poor immigrant neighbourhood. Middle-class reformers lived there and offered services. An example is Hull House in Chicago, opened in 1889.
Industrial home work (outwork)
Manufacturing work done at home, paid by the piece, often with children helping.
Family wage economy
A household plan in which every member, children included, earns money for a shared budget.
Grassroots activism
Political action organised by ordinary people at the local level, not by party leaders.

Reading tip: “Ironically, though” marks a turn in the argument. Be clear about whose view each sentence gives (reformers, working-class mothers, or recent historians).

p.27English farmhouse cheese-making & the “decline thesis”Industrial & agricultural revolutions · Pinchbeck · dairying · factors

In short: Historians are moving away from the picture of a steady, unequivocal decline in women’s status under capitalism and industrialisation. They are moving toward accounts of change and continuity that vary by place and occupation. The case is English farmhouse cheese-making, 1800–1930. In Women Workers and the Industrial Revolution (1930), Ivy Pinchbeck argued that the agricultural revolution’s specialisation and larger scale shrank women’s role. In her account, women had once fed cows, reared calves and sold cheese at markets and fairs. Later they only processed the milk, while male “dairymen” and male factors handled the rest.

The story

Two revolutions remade Britain. The Agricultural Revolution, about 1700–1850, brought enclosure: open fields and commons were fenced off as private land. It also brought new crop rotations, selective livestock breeding and larger commercial farms. Wage labourers replaced small family farmers, and the extra food fed the growing industrial cities.

The Industrial Revolution, about 1760–1840, brought textile machines, Watt’s steam engine, factories and, from 1830, railways. It moved production out of the home and into the factory.

Farmhouse cheeses such as Cheddar, Cheshire, Stilton and Gloucester were traditionally made by farmers’ wives and dairymaids. It was skilled, heavy work. Factory cheese-making, copied from New York State, reached England around 1870.

Early women’s historians, Alice Clark in 1919 and Pinchbeck in 1930, told a story of decline. Before industry, they said, wives were working partners in a “family economy”, and capitalism ended it. Later historians, such as Judith Bennett, stressed continuity: women’s work was low-status both before and after.

Remember it like this: A factor is like an arhtiya, the commission agent in an Indian mandi who sells the farmer’s produce for a cut.

Words to know

Factor
A commission agent, a middleman who sells goods for producers.
Unequivocal
Clear, with no doubt and no exception.
Historiography
The writing of history, and the study of how historians’ views change.
Curtail
Cut short, reduce.

Reading tip: Separate the older historian’s claims from the newer picture. To sum up the text’s overall aim, choose a verb and scope that fit what the text as a whole actually does.

p.28Antonia Castañeda: Californianas, property law & Anglo novelsMexican California · 1848 · Spanish civil law · Chicana history

In short: Antonia Castañeda combines women’s studies and Mexican-American history to read 19th-century novels about Mexican women. US male novelists of the era defined Anglo women only as wives and mothers. This was at a time when industrialisation was eroding women’s farm-based economic role. Meanwhile, Hispanic law in territorial California gave Californianas property and inheritance rights equal to men’s. Castañeda argues that these laws explain a stock plot by male, non-Hispanic novelists: an ambitious Anglo merchant seeks to marry an elite Californiana.

The story

Picture California in 1769. Spanish missions began there under the friar Junípero Serra, and in time there were 21 of them. Spain also built presidios, or forts, and pueblos, or towns. Los Angeles began as a pueblo in 1781.

Mexico won independence from Spain in 1821, so California became a Mexican territory. In the 1830s the mission lands were secularised, that is, taken out of church hands. They were granted as huge ranchos, or cattle ranches, to a Californio elite. These families traded cattle hides and tallow with ships from New England. Richard Henry Dana’s Two Years Before the Mast (1840) describes this world.

Some Anglo-American and British merchants settled there. They converted to Catholicism, became Mexican citizens and married daughters of elite Californio families. They gained land, status and connections.

In 1845 Americans spoke of Manifest Destiny, the belief that the US was destined to spread across the continent. The Mexican–American War came in 1846–48. In the Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo of 1848, the US gained California and the Southwest, roughly half of Mexico’s territory. Gold was found in 1848, the Gold Rush followed in 1849, and California became a state in 1850.

The treaty promised to respect property. But the Land Act of 1851 forced Californios to prove their land titles in costly lawsuits, and many lost their land.

Spain and Mexico used civil law, based on written codes, while England used common law, built up from judges’ past decisions. Under Hispanic law a married woman kept her own property and could inherit equally with her brothers. She also shared community property with her husband, a bit like a joint bank account. Under common law, a rule called coverture passed a wife’s property to her husband. The Hispanic rule is why California, Texas, Arizona, New Mexico and some other states are still “community property” states.

Remember it like this: California changed hands twice: Spanish until 1821, Mexican until 1848, then American, and a US state from 1850.

Words to know

Californio / Californiana
A Spanish-speaking resident of Spanish or Mexican California. Californio is the male form and Californiana the female form.
Territorial
Before statehood.
Chicano / Chicana
Mexican American. It is a political self-name from the Chicano Movement of the 1960s.
Stereotype
A fixed, oversimplified image of a group.

Reading tip: This text is about an interdisciplinary scholar. Identify what Castañeda explains and the evidence she uses. For a quoted phrase such as “apparent contradiction”, reread the lines around it before deciding what it means.

p.29Women and the “new political history” (Paula Baker)new political history · election returns · women’s history

In short: The “new political history” of the 1960s–70s studied the political behaviour of ordinary citizens rather than leaders. But it still left out women. Its main tool was quantitative analysis of election returns, and that tool was useless for people who could not vote before 1920. Historian Paula Baker redefined “political activity” to write a political history that includes women. She concludes that, among ordinary citizens, women’s political activism in the 19th century prefigured trends in 20th-century politics. She defines “politics” as “any action taken to affect the course of behavior of government or of the community”. The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence: “Baker concludes that, while voting and holding office were restricted …”.

The story

The new political history was part of a broader “new social history”, history written from the bottom up. It used computers and statistics, such as vote tallies and census data, to study how masses of people voted. It found that religion and ethnicity strongly predicted 19th-century voting.

Men’s politics in the 1800s was intensely partisan, that is, loyal to a party. There were parades, party newspapers and patronage jobs given to loyal supporters. Voter turnout was often above 75%. Think of election season in an Indian town, with rallies and party flags everywhere.

Petitions were one part of women’s public life before the vote. Petitioning is a right protected by the First Amendment. Voluntary associations were another part. The largest women’s group of the century was the Woman’s Christian Temperance Union, founded in 1874. Temperance meant the campaign against alcohol.

Remember it like this: Picture 1800s American politics as a festival season: parades, party newspapers and often more than three in four voters turning out.

Words to know

Quantitative
Based on numbers and statistics.
Election returns
Official vote counts.
Prefigure
To hint at something before it happens, to foreshadow.

Reading tip: When a text quotes a definition, note its exact wording: much depends on what it includes and what it leaves out.

p.30“Bourgeois” vs “socialist” feminism in late-tsarist RussiaRussia 1905–1917 · Marxism · 1908 Women’s Congress · Kollontai

In short: Late-tsarist Russia had two rival women’s movements. “Bourgeois” feminists sought education, careers and legal equality. That label was given to them by radical opponents. Socialist feminists blamed class, not gender, and saw revolution as the only path. Despite their hostility, both saw paid labour as the key to emancipation. Both recognised how hard it was to combine work and motherhood. At the 1908 First All-Russian Women’s Congress, most delegates backed maternity insurance and paid leave. And both put women’s emancipation below liberating all Russians from oppression, backwardness and injustice.

The story

Tsarist Russia was an autocracy, an absolute monarchy under the Romanov tsars. Serfdom, which bound peasants to their lords’ land, was abolished only in 1861. Most Russians were peasants, and industry came late, from the 1890s. So Russia looked “backward” next to Western Europe.

In 1905, after “Bloody Sunday”, mass strikes forced Tsar Nicholas II to create an elected parliament, the Duma. But women could not vote for it. That spurred women to organise, for example in the Union for Women’s Equality, founded in 1905. In 1908 about 1,000 delegates met at a women’s congress in St Petersburg. There, socialist women organised by Alexandra Kollontai clashed with the liberal majority.

Marxists had their own view of “the woman question”. In this view, women’s oppression came from private property and from the family as an economic unit. Engels set this out in The Origin of the Family, Private Property and the State (1884). So, Marxists said, socialism would free women automatically. They condemned cross-class “women’s” alliances for dividing the working class.

In February 1917, on International Women’s Day, women textile workers went on strike over bread shortages. The revolution began, and the Tsar abdicated. The new Provisional Government gave women the vote that year. In October the Bolsheviks, led by Lenin, took power. Kollontai became People’s Commissar for Social Welfare, one of the first women anywhere to hold a cabinet post.

Americans learn about 1917 and the Cold War, but rarely about these movements. The “bourgeois” goals resemble the individualist strand of feminism (compare p.8).

Remember it like this: 1917 had two acts. February brought down the Tsar, and October brought the Bolsheviks to power.

Words to know

Bourgeois
The Marxist word for the property-owning middle class. It was often used as an insult.
Tsarist
Under the Russian emperors, called tsars, before 1917.
Emancipation
Freedom from legal or social limits.
Maternity insurance
State-funded support for mothers around the time of childbirth.

Reading tip: When a text lists shared beliefs, count them and keep each one distinct.

p.31Ewha’s 1977 women’s studies program & the Korean critique of FreudSouth Korea · Confucian family · Freud · individualism vs interdependence

In short: In 1977, Ewha Women’s University in Seoul opened Asia’s first women’s studies program. Critics called it a betrayal of national identity, a Western import and a distraction from unification and economic development. Supporters saw it as just another useful Western import, like electricity or the assembly line. The founders took a third view: test Western feminist theory against Asian experience. For example, like Western feminists, they found Freud culture-bound, but for different reasons. Freud assumes the Western nuclear, male-headed family and a competitive, individualistic self. The Korean self, by contrast, is group-centred and interdependent. Dependency is not weakness, and men show emotion openly. Since four generations may live under one roof, people can be sons and daughters all their lives.

The story

Under the Joseon dynasty, from 1392 to 1910, Korea made Neo-Confucianism its state ideology. It stressed filial piety, a child’s duty to honour parents, along with hierarchy, the patrilineal family and ancestor rites. Women owed the “three obediences”, to father, husband and son. Korea’s male-headed family registry, the hoju, was ruled unconstitutional in 2005.

In 1886 Mary Scranton, an American Methodist missionary, founded Ewha as Korea’s first modern school for women. Today it is Ewha Womans University, its official spelling, and one of the world’s largest women’s universities.

Picture Korea in 1977. It had been divided in 1945 at the 38th parallel, with a Soviet-backed North and a US-backed South. The Korean War of 1950–53 ended in an armistice, a ceasefire, not a peace treaty. So “national unification” was a sacred goal.

In the South, the authoritarian Park Chung-hee held power from 1961 to 1979. He drove rapid industrialisation based on exports, the “Miracle on the Han River”. It relied heavily on low-paid young women in textile and electronics factories. So, to critics, feminism looked like a “distraction”.

Freud, who lived from 1856 to 1939, founded psychoanalysis, a method of exploring the unconscious mind. His Oedipus complex is a son’s rivalry with his father for his mother. He also stressed rivalry between brothers and sisters. Western feminists such as Betty Friedan and Kate Millett attacked his sexism, for example his idea of “penis envy”. The Korean critique targets something else: his individualism and his assumption of the nuclear family.

A “Darwinian struggle” is competition for survival, as in “survival of the fittest”. Applied to a family, it turns family members into competitors. Cultural psychologists contrast the independent self of the West with the interdependent self of East Asia. The Japanese psychiatrist Takeo Doi made a parallel argument in 1971 about amae, healthy dependence. Indian readers will recognise the logic of the joint family.

Remember it like this: Three obediences for three stages of life: a woman obeys her father, then her husband, then her son.

Words to know

Culture-bound
True only within the culture that produced it.
Psyche
The mind or personality.
Nuclear family
Parents and their children only, unlike an extended or joint family.
Equilibrium
Balance or harmony.

Reading tip: Keep each group’s view separate: who held it and what it claimed. Signal phrases such as “Neither view was correct” mark a turn in the argument, so note what follows them.

Topic 2 · Native Americans 12 readings · pp.33–44

These readings expect you to know how relations between the US government and the tribes changed over time. First came treaties, and then removal, reservations, allotment, reorganisation, termination and, finally, self-determination. You also need two ideas from anthropology. Native property is often held by the whole group or based on use, and most of the written record comes from non-Native observers. The five short chapters below tell the story in order.

First peoples and first contact (up to 1492)

The first Americans walked in from Asia. More than 20,000 years ago, peoples from Siberia entered the Americas across Beringia, a land bridge that once joined Siberia to Alaska.

Their stone spear points tell part of the story. Spear points of the Clovis culture date to about 13,000 years ago. Some sites are even older, and these “pre-Clovis” sites are now accepted too (see p.43).

Then, in 1492, Europeans arrived. Contact brought epidemics of diseases such as smallpox and measles, and they killed a large share of Native people. In many regions, estimates often put the loss at 50 to 90 per cent. Imagine a village where, out of every ten people, between five and nine die.

Remember it like this: First a land bridge from Siberia. Then Clovis spear points, about 13,000 years ago. Then 1492: Europeans arrive, and epidemics follow.

Nations, treaties and removal (1787–1840s)

When the Constitution was written in 1787, it gave Congress power over commerce “with the Indian Tribes”. The tribes were treated as sovereign nations: peoples with the right to rule themselves. The United States made treaties with them, and about 370 treaties were ratified, or approved by the Senate, by 1871.

Between 1823 and 1832, three Supreme Court decisions known as the Marshall Trilogy defined the tribes’ place. They called the tribes “domestic dependent nations”: nations, but inside the United States and dependent on it. The tribes stood in a trust relationship with the federal government, described as that of a “ward to guardian”, like a child in a guardian’s care. And state laws did not apply in tribal territory.

In 1824 the government created an office for Indian affairs, the Bureau of Indian Affairs or BIA. It sat first in the War Department, and from 1849 in the Interior Department (see p.36).

Then came removal. In 1830, under President Andrew Jackson, Congress passed the Indian Removal Act. Through the 1830s and 1840s, the Cherokee and other nations were forced west to “Indian Territory”, now the state of Oklahoma. This forced journey is remembered as the Trail of Tears.

Remember it like this: A “domestic dependent nation” is a bit like a princely state in British India. It runs its own house, but a stronger power stands over it.

Reservations, allotment and the courts (1850s–1908)

The 1850s opened the era of reservations: areas of land set aside for the tribes. On the Plains, in the middle of the country, these years also brought war. Two names stand out: the battle of Little Bighorn in 1876 and the massacre at Wounded Knee in 1890. Meanwhile, in 1871, Congress ended treaty-making with the tribes (see p.35).

Next came allotment. The Dawes Act of 1887, also called the General Allotment Act, carved reservations into plots for individual owners. The land left over, the “surplus”, was sold to settlers. By 1934, tribal land had fallen from about 138 million acres to about 48 million, a loss of roughly two acres in every three.

Boarding schools for Native children followed the motto “Kill the Indian, save the man.” In other words: erase the Native way of life, but keep the person.

The courts shaped this era too. In Lone Wolf (1903), the Supreme Court gave Congress “plenary power”, meaning full power, over Indian affairs (see p.35). In Winters (1908), it set out reserved water rights: a reservation comes with the water it needs (see p.33).

Remember it like this: Allotment is like splitting a joint family’s shared land into small plots, then selling the “spare” land to strangers. By 1934, about two acres in every three were gone.

From the Indian New Deal to Red Power (1924–1975)

In 1924 the Indian Citizenship Act made Native Americans citizens of the United States. Ten years later, in 1934, the Indian Reorganization Act ended allotment and created tribal governments. It was nicknamed the “Indian New Deal”, after the New Deal reforms of President Roosevelt in the 1930s.

In the 1950s, policy turned the other way. Termination meant ending federal recognition of a tribe, so that in US law it no longer counted as a tribe. Relocation moved Native people to cities.

Then came “Red Power”. The American Indian Movement, or AIM, was founded in 1968. Activists occupied Alcatraz from 1969 to 1971, and Wounded Knee, the site of the 1890 massacre, was occupied in 1973. In 1975 Congress passed the Indian Self-Determination Act (see p.44). Self-determination means that a people decides its own affairs.

Remember it like this: Think of a pendulum. In 1934 it swung towards tribal governments, in the 1950s towards termination, and by 1975 back towards self-determination.

Tribes today, and what to call them

Today the federal government recognises 574 tribes. In the 2020 census, about 9.7 million people identified as American Indian or Alaska Native, either alone or in combination with another group. Tribes govern themselves on reservations and on trust land, land that the federal government holds in trust for them.

The names can confuse. “Native American”, “American Indian” and “Indian” are all used in US law and in these readings. In this topic, “Indian” means the first peoples of America, not people from India. “First Nations” is the Canadian term.

One more word has two meanings. A pueblo is a village in the Southwest, and it is also the people who live in it.

Remember it like this: “Pueblo” works like “village” in “the whole village came out”: one word for both the place and its people.

p.33Indian water rights: the Winters doctrine & the Rio Grande pueblosSupreme Court · Western water law · 1848 · reservations

In short: Winters v. United States (1908) dealt with the treaty that created Montana’s Fort Belknap Reservation. Although the treaty never mentioned water, the court held that it had silently reserved the land’s water for the tribes, because land without water would be useless. Later cases set three tests for such “reserved” rights. The tests are that the land is a federal enclave, that it was formally withdrawn from public lands, and that the government intended to reserve water. The Rio Grande pueblos fail the formal tests, since they were never public land and were never officially named reservations. The text argues that the doctrine still applies: what counts as a “reservation” is a matter of practice, not definition. It adds that Arizona v. California (1963) says the way a reservation was created does not matter. So the pueblos’ water rights date from 1848.

The story

Picture a dry valley in the American West, with one river and many thirsty farms. The West shares out such water by a rule called prior appropriation: “first in time, first in right”. Whoever first put the water to use holds the senior right. In a drought, senior rights are served first, even if junior users get nothing.

It works like a queue at a ration shop: when stock runs out, those at the back go home empty-handed. So in the West, a water right’s date, its priority date, is crucial. A priority date of 1848 puts its holder ahead of almost all later settlers. The wetter East is different: there, under riparian rights, landowners along a stream share its water.

In 1888 the Fort Belknap Reservation was created in Montana. It is home to the Gros Ventre and Assiniboine tribes. Upstream, settlers had diverted the Milk River, and the quarrel led to the Winters case of 1908. From it comes the reserved-rights doctrine: land that the federal government sets aside silently comes with enough water for its purpose. This holds for a reservation, a national park or a military base, and the water’s priority date is the date of the reservation.
