Far to the south, along the Rio Grande in New Mexico, live the peoples called pueblos. The Spanish named them after their compact adobe towns, built of sun-dried mud brick. They farmed with irrigation long before Spain arrived, and they held Spanish land grants. In 1848 the Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo ended a war with Mexico, and the United States gained New Mexico.

In US law, the pueblos’ position was truly unclear. In US v. Joseph (1876), the Supreme Court said they were not an “Indian tribe” under one federal law. Then US v. Sandoval (1913) reversed course and placed them under federal guardianship.

Arizona v. California (1963) was a long fight over Colorado River water. It also confirmed reserved rights for five tribes, including reservations created by executive order of the President rather than by treaty. Colorado River shortages are regular news, and Western Americans grow up with “water wars”. India has its own, such as the long Cauvery dispute between Karnataka and Tamil Nadu. As a Western saying goes, “Whiskey’s for drinking, water’s for fighting.”

Remember it like this: Western water is a ration-shop queue: first in time, first in right. Under Winters, a reservation comes with the water it needs, even if its treaty never mentions water.

Words to know

Reserved right
A right kept by, or set aside for, someone when land was granted or reserved.
Public lands / withdrawal
Public lands are land owned by the federal government and open to private claims. A withdrawal takes land out of that pool for a particular purpose.
Enclave
A piece of territory surrounded by an area under a different authority.
Sovereignty
The highest power to govern a territory.
Buttressed
Supported, strengthened.
Diversion
Channelling water out of a river to use it.

Reading tip: Legal writing often applies a rule to a case. List the rule’s conditions, then check how the text handles each one for the case at hand. Often the argument turns on a single link.

p.34Recording Native American life stories (Boas, Radin)ethnology · “salvage” anthropology · oral vs written · autobiography

In short: In the late 19th century, ethnologists recorded the life stories of Native Americans. They wanted to add to their field observations and to preserve cultures they believed were vanishing. Critics raised problems. Boas called autobiographies useful “chiefly for the study of the perversion of truth by memory”. Radin said investigators spent too little time with tribes and coloured the results with their own emotions. Meaning was lost in the move from spoken telling to written English. Editors chose what mattered. And narrators had to break taboos, such as naming dead relatives. Still, the author concludes that autobiography is valuable, because it reveals minds and emotions better than outsiders’ guesswork.

The story

Picture the US in 1900. Its Native population has fallen to its lowest point, about 250,000 people. The 1890 census has already declared the frontier, the moving edge of settlement, “closed”. This is the time of “the vanishing Indian”.

Believing these cultures were about to disappear, anthropologists rushed to record them. This is called salvage ethnography: like a salvage crew at a shipwreck, they saved what they could. Edward Curtis’s photographs, published from 1907 to 1930, came from the same impulse.

Ethnography is the detailed description of one culture, based on fieldwork. Ethnology compares many cultures. In the US, anthropology has four fields: cultural, linguistic, archaeological and biological.

Franz Boas, a German-born professor at Columbia, is known as the “father of American anthropology”. He championed cultural relativism, the idea that each culture should be understood on its own terms, and he fought scientific racism. His students included Margaret Mead, Ruth Benedict, Zora Neale Hurston and Paul Radin. Ironically, Radin himself published a famous Native autobiography, Crashing Thunder (1926), the story of a Winnebago, or Ho-Chunk, man.

Some life stories were “as-told-to”: a Native narrator spoke, and a white editor wrote the story down. In Black Elk Speaks (1932), John Neihardt wrote down the life of a Lakota holy man, and people still debate how much he shaped it. Many Native cultures also avoid speaking the names of the dead. A ban like this, set by custom, is a taboo. In India, too, some traditional wives avoid saying their husband’s name.

Remember it like this: Ethnography is a photograph of one culture, taken in the field. Ethnology lays many such photographs side by side to compare them.

Words to know

From without
As an outsider, from the outside.
Posterity
Future generations.
Perversion (of truth)
Distortion: a twisting of the truth.
Taboo
A ban set by a culture’s customs.

Reading tip: Track the author’s attitude through signal words such as “however”, “Even more importantly”, “Indeed” and “Despite”, and read the sentence each one introduces closely.

p.35Lone Wolf v. Hitchcock (1903) and federal Indian policyplenary power · 1871 Indian Appropriations Act · Kiowa · allotment

In short: In Lone Wolf, the Supreme Court let Congress open Kiowa, Comanche and Apache lands to settlers without the tribes’ consent. Blue Clark rightly stresses the Court’s claim that Congress has almost unlimited power to act alone. But Clark misses a bigger effect. Soon after Lone Wolf, the government stopped negotiating formal written agreements with tribes at all. Many commentators date that change to 1871, when Congress abolished treaty-making after a dispute between the House and the Senate. In reality, formal agreements continued past 1900. They were treated not as treaties with sovereign nations, but in a different form.

The story

As Foundation 2 explains, the President makes treaties “with the Advice and Consent of the Senate”, which takes a two-thirds vote. The House, the lower chamber of Congress, has no role. Yet Indian treaties committed federal money, and spending bills start in the House, much as money bills in India start in the Lok Sabha.

In 1867 the Medicine Lodge Treaty with the Kiowa, Comanche and Apache, in Indian Territory, now Oklahoma, set a rule. No reservation land could be ceded, or handed over, without the consent of three-fourths of the adult men.

The House resented being left out of treaties. In 1871 it attached a rider, an extra clause, to that year’s Indian Appropriations Act, a spending law. No tribe would again be recognised as a nation “with whom the United States may contract by treaty”. Existing treaties stayed valid.

This was the era of the Dawes Act and allotment. Reservations were divided into family plots, and the “surplus” was opened to whites. For the Kiowa, Comanche and Apache, an allotment agreement of 1892 lacked valid consent. Congress enacted it anyway in 1900, and in 1901 their lands were opened by lottery.

Kiowa leader Lone Wolf sued the Interior Secretary, Ethan A. Hitchcock. In 1903 the Supreme Court ruled that Congress has plenary power over Indian affairs and may override treaty terms on its own. Courts will not second-guess Congress, presuming that it acts in “perfect good faith”. Scholars sometimes call the ruling the “Indians’ Dred Scott decision”, after the case that said Black people could not be citizens.

In 1994 Blue Clark, a Muscogee (Creek) historian, published a book on the case.

Remember it like this: A rider is a clause that hitches a ride on a bill about something else. “Plenary” means full, as in a plenary session, where all the members meet together.

Words to know

Plenary power
Complete authority: full power, with no conditions attached.
Unilateral
Done by one side alone, without the other side’s consent.
Abrogate
To cancel or repeal something, such as a treaty.
Primacy
Being first or highest in authority.
Prerequisite
Something that must be in place before something else can happen.

Reading tip: Keep the 1871 change and the post-1903 change strictly separate, and note which claims the author accepts and which the author rejects.

p.36Maps of Native American lands & the BIA’s recordcartography · land tenure · trust land · bias of sources

In short: Almost from first contact, non-Natives mapped Native lands, resources and populations. There were missionaries’ sketches, explorers’ drawings, surveyors’ maps and treaty maps. Most maps that survive are reconstructions, pieced together from archaeology, oral reports and the writings of observers. So their accuracy depends on the mapmaker’s skill in interpreting that evidence. Many maps also reflect the Bureau of Indian Affairs’ roughly 150 years of managing tribal lands. Native map-making rarely made it into that official record. The maps that resulted reflect where their data came from, the mixed purposes they served, and changes in US policy and in non-Native attitudes.

The story

Picture a map drawn on birchbark or deerskin, or traced in sand or snow. Native peoples made such maps and gave some sketches to Europeans. In the 1720s, colonial officials received a Catawba map on deerskin. Like a metro map, these often showed relationships and routes, not surveyed boundaries.

Ideas about land differed too. Native systems of land tenure, the rules for holding land, were often communal, shared by the whole group. Or they rested on use, such as rights to hunt, fish or farm. Europeans pictured absolute ownership of a plot with fixed edges. So a Native “territory” is hard to map.

In government hands, maps were tools of power. Treaty maps marked land that tribes had ceded, or given up. The federal survey grid of the 1785 Land Ordinance cut land into square-mile “sections”. It turned Native homelands into property for sale.

With trust land, the federal government holds the legal title “in trust” for a tribe or an individual Indian. Such land can’t be freely sold, and states generally can’t tax it. This follows from the trust relationship, a federal duty to protect tribes, set by the Marshall Court (see Foundation 3). In India, too, many states limit the sale of tribal land to non-tribal buyers.

In 1824 the US set up the Bureau of Indian Affairs, or BIA. It ran reservations, schools, leases and allotments, plots carved out of reservations for individual Indians. Critics have long called it paternalistic, meaning it treats adults like children. Since the 1970s, its staff have been mainly Native people.

Remember it like this: Native maps often worked like a metro map, showing links and routes. The federal survey grid was a chessboard of square-mile plots, ready to be sold.

Words to know

Cartography
Map-making.
Land tenure
The system of rights that decides who holds land, and how.
Territoriality
A group’s claim to an area, and its defence of that area.
Reconstruction
A map made later, pieced together from indirect evidence.

Reading tip: Note each source of evidence the text names and what the text says about it.

p.37Ethnocentric textbook portrayals of Native Americansethnocentrism · cultural relativism · US history textbooks

In short: Writing in the first person, as “I”, the author argues that US history textbooks distort Native American history to fit the values of one culture. The books show settlers as more humane, complex, skilful and wise than Native Americans. They stereotype and belittle many Native cultures. And they imply that the European conquest proved Europeans were superior. Even when they judge Native architecture, politics and ways of running a home, they do so from an ethnocentric European point of view. The author then addresses a counter-argument: all textbooks are culturally biased, and children can see through such bias.

The story

Picture a traditional American history textbook. Columbus “discovered” America. Settlers tamed a “wilderness” or “virgin land”. In 1893 the historian Frederick Jackson Turner described the frontier as the line between “savagery and civilization”. There is Manifest Destiny, the belief that the US was meant to spread across the continent. And there are the Pilgrims, sharing a friendly Thanksgiving in 1621.

Here is what that story left out. Near today’s St Louis stood Cahokia, a city of perhaps 10,000–20,000 people between about 1050 and 1350, with huge earthen mounds. Ancestral Puebloan people built cliff cities at Mesa Verde. The Hohokam dug irrigation canals. Native farmers grew the “Three Sisters” together: maize, beans and squash. And the Haudenosaunee, or Iroquois, Confederacy, a league of Native nations, lived by its Great Law of Peace. In 1988 Congress formally recognised its influence on the US system of government, though historians debate how much.

Scholars have names for two ways of seeing another culture. Ethnocentrism, a term W. G. Sumner wrote about in 1906, means judging it by your own culture’s standards. Its opposite, cultural relativism, is linked to the anthropologist Boas. It means understanding a culture on its own terms.

In 1995 James Loewen’s bestseller Lies My Teacher Told Me attacked exactly these biases in textbooks. Native mascots, the symbols that sports teams use, were contested too. In 2020–22, Washington’s American football team and Cleveland’s baseball team dropped their Native names.

Americans grow up with Columbus Day, now often renamed Indigenous Peoples’ Day. Children act in Thanksgiving pageants, school plays about that friendly feast. And “textbook wars” over how history is taught break out again and again. India has its own arguments over what school history books should say.

Remember it like this: Ethnocentrism is judging a cricket match by baseball rules and deciding the players have it all wrong. Cultural relativism is learning cricket’s own rules before you judge.

Words to know

Deprecate
To belittle something, or to say you disapprove of it.
Stereotype
A fixed and oversimplified image.
Denote
To show or to mean.
Ethnocentric
Seeing everything through the values of your own ethnic group.

Reading tip: Note exactly what any opponent is said to claim and how the author responds.

p.38The earliest fur trade: Newfoundland fishing crewsfur trade · cod fisheries · archaeology · 1501

In short: Nobody knows exactly when Native Americans began trading furs with Europeans in the early modern era. Even without written records, it is fairly certain that the first traders were fishing crews off Newfoundland. Archaeologists found iron bolts and metal pins at Native sites from the 1500s. Only after reading Nicolas Denys’s account of 1672 did they realise that crews had dismantled, or taken apart, parts of their ships to trade for furs. By the time anyone recorded the trade in writing, it was apparently already well under way. The first record, linked to a Portuguese ship from Newfoundland in 1501, mentions a Native American wearing Venetian silver earrings.

The story

Picture Norse Vikings reaching Newfoundland, an island off the east coast of Canada, about 1000 CE. They landed at a place now called L’Anse aux Meadows. Historians call the years from about 1500 to 1800 the early modern era. So that label leaves out the much earlier Viking contact.

In 1497 John Cabot sailed to Newfoundland, a year before Vasco da Gama reached India. Its fishing grounds, the Grand Banks, were one of the world’s richest cod fisheries. After Cabot’s voyage, Basque, Portuguese, French and English crews came every summer. Many landed to dry and salt their cod on shore, much as fishing families on the coasts of India dry fish in the sun. That brought them into contact with the Beothuk and Mi’kmaq peoples. Fishermen kept few written records.

In 1501 the Portuguese Corte-Real voyages explored Newfoundland and seized Native captives. Reports from Lisbon noted that the captives already had European-made objects. Later, in 1534–35, the French explorer Jacques Cartier explored the Gulf of St Lawrence and claimed it for France.

Why furs? Europe had a craze for hats made of felt, a thick cloth of pressed fibres, and this felt came from beaver fur. The fur trade powered French Canada, where Quebec was founded in 1608. It also powered Dutch New Netherland and the Hudson’s Bay Company, set up in 1670. And it reshaped Native economies and wars, as in the “Beaver Wars” of the 1600s.

Native peoples valued metal goods highly, such as iron tools and copper kettles, which they cut into ornaments. Nicolas Denys was a French colonist and merchant in Acadia, now Nova Scotia.

Remember it like this: Think “hats and dates”. Europe wanted beaver fur for felt hats. And the early modern era runs from about 1500 to 1800, long after the Vikings’ visit around 1000 CE.

Words to know

Early modern
The years from about 1500 to 1800.
Artifact
Something made by people and later found by archaeologists.
Strewn
Scattered about.

Reading tip: Keep straight which evidence (artefacts, Denys’s 1672 account, the 1501 report) supports which claim.

p.39Hopi cultural persistence, 1680–1880Pueblo Revolt · Hopi · matrilineal clans

In short: Anthropologists see Hopi society from 1680 to 1880 as surprisingly stable, despite a shrinking population and pressure from outside. The usual explanations are the determination of the religious elite and the greater geographic isolation of the Hopi, which limited contact and disease. The author adds that the social system’s built-in flexibility may have been equally important. For example, matrilineal clans survived even as some merged and others split. And control of ceremonies, traditionally held by particular clans, could change.

The story

Picture three mesas, flat-topped ridges shaped like giant tables, in north-eastern Arizona. On top sit the villages of the Hopi, a Pueblo people. One village, Old Oraibi, has been lived in since about 1100 CE. It is among the oldest continuously occupied towns in the US.

Hopi clans are matrilineal: you belong to your mother’s clan. Houses and fields pass down the female line, and a husband joins his wife’s household. The Khasi of Meghalaya, in north-east India, follow a similar pattern. Clans also “own” particular ceremonies and ritual knowledge, such as katsina rituals and the Snake Dance.

In 1680 the Pueblo peoples rose up. In this Pueblo Revolt, led by Popé, they drove the Spanish out of New Mexico for 12 years. The remote Hopi killed their missionaries. Unlike the pueblos on the Rio Grande, they were never brought back under Spanish rule.

The Hopi also faced other pressures. Droughts and smallpox epidemics struck, notably in the late 1700s. Navajo and Ute raiders attacked. And there was contact with Spanish and Mexican outsiders.

Around 1880, US influence grew stronger. In 1882 an executive order, a direct order from the president, created a Hopi reservation. Railways and government schools arrived too. Today the Hopi Reservation is surrounded by the much larger Navajo Nation.

Remember it like this: Two book-ends: in 1680 the Pueblo Revolt pushes Spain out, and around 1880 US railways, schools and a reservation push in.

Words to know

Matrilineal
Tracing family descent through the mother’s line.
Clan / descent group
A group of relatives who claim the same ancestor.
Diminution
A decrease, or shrinking.
Accommodate
To adapt or adjust to something.
Socio-cultural system
The way a society is organised, taken together with its culture.

Reading tip: Examples carry this argument. For each example, note which claim it illustrates.

p.40The Inca “vertical archipelago”Andes · altitude zones · Inca Empire · Murra

In short: By the 1500s the Inca Empire ran from Ecuador to central Chile. Highlanders living above 9,000 feet could grow few crops. Of the main Andean food crops, 95% grow below 3,000 feet, but only 20% thrive above 9,000 feet. The highlanders needed products from the lowlands. Most preindustrial societies solved this with long-distance trade networks, over which consumers had little control. Andean highlanders took part in such networks. But they relied mainly on running their own production in as many ecological zones as possible. In this system, goods were extracted, processed and transported entirely by members of one group.

The story

Picture the Inca Empire, also called Tawantinsuyu. From its capital, Cusco in Peru, it grew fast between about 1438 and 1533. It reached roughly 10 million people, the largest state in the Americas before the European conquest.

Its road network stretched 40,000 km, about the distance around the Earth. It recorded data on quipus, knotted cords, instead of writing. Machu Picchu dates from about 1450. In 1532–33, Pizarro conquered the empire.

The land rose through several altitude zones. Along the coast lay desert, while warm valleys grew maize, cotton, chili, coca and fruit. Temperate slopes grew maize. On the puna, the high cold grassland, people grew potatoes and quinoa and herded llamas and alpacas. Potatoes were freeze-dried into chuño so they would keep.

For scale, 9,000 feet is about 2,750 metres, higher than Shimla. And 3,000 feet is about 900 metres, roughly the height of Bengaluru.

In 1972 the anthropologist John Murra gave a name to an Andean pattern: the vertical archipelago. An archipelago is a group of islands. Under this pattern, one group’s fields and herds were scattered across several altitude zones, like islands in a sea.

Remember it like this: Think of an Andean mountain as a tall building. Fruit and coca sit on the warm lower floors, maize in the middle, and potatoes and llamas on the top floor.

Words to know

Ecological zone
An area with its own climate, plants and animals. Here the zones are set by altitude.
Autonomous
Independent, running its own affairs.
Preindustrial
From the time before factories.
Commodity
A raw or basic product.

Reading tip: Read the percentages precisely: they are shares of the principal crops that grow at an altitude, not shares of land. Details also hinge on qualifiers such as “most”, “primarily”, and “entirely”.

p.41Algonquian family hunting territoriesproperty rights · fur trade · reciprocity · Speck vs Leacock

In short: What kind of property are Algonquian family hunting territories, and where did they come from? The dominant recent view says that, like other private ownership of land, they did not exist before contact with Europeans. In this view, they grew out of the fur trade, together with changes in the environment. A rival view says they existed before contact and are true private property of individuals and families. Newer fieldwork shows that family rights sit inside a larger system of hunting groups made up of several families. It shows that rights at the community level take precedence, or come first. And it shows that the system rests on spiritual and social reciprocity, or give-and-take, which clashes with European private property.

The story

Picture the cold subarctic forests of Canada, just south of the Arctic. Here the Cree, Innu (or Montagnais) and Ojibwe (or Anishinaabe) hunt beaver, moose and caribou. Scholars study family hunting territories mainly among these forest peoples.

“Algonquian” is not one tribe but a language family, like the family that links Tamil and Telugu. Besides the Cree, Innu and Ojibwe, it includes the Mi’kmaq, Lenape, Powhatan, Wampanoag, Cheyenne and others.

The English thinker John Locke said property comes from mixing your labour with land, by farming it and enclosing it, or fencing it in. Colonists used his idea to argue that hunting peoples did not truly “own” land. To them, it was “vacant land”. So the question of Native property rights has always carried political weight.

In 1915 Frank Speck argued that family territories were aboriginal private property, meaning they existed before Europeans arrived. He did so partly to fight the claim that Native people lacked property and so could be dispossessed, or pushed off their land. In 1954 Eleanor Leacock disagreed. She said they arose after contact with Europeans. In her view, trapping furs for sale encouraged each family to keep its own trapping grounds.

In the 1970s–80s, fieldwork, or on-the-spot research, among the James Bay Cree found a layered system centred on the community. It was tied to land-claims talks over a giant hydro-electric project in Quebec. Cree belief also has give-and-take with animals: they “give themselves” to hunters who show them respect. A steward, called a tallyman, manages each territory for the group and must share.

Remember it like this: Locke’s rule in one line: plough the land and fence it, and it is yours. By that rule, land used only for hunting looked “vacant” to colonists.

Words to know

Private property (fee simple)
Exclusive ownership: the owner can keep others out. The owner can also sell it or pass it on to heirs.
Usufruct
The right to use land or what it produces, without owning it outright.
Reciprocity
Give-and-take: duties that run both ways.
Take precedence
To rank above something else, or override it.

Reading tip: There are three views to map: post-contact origin (dominant), pre-contact private property (rival), and the recent fieldwork (layered, communal, reciprocal). Be clear about which view holds which belief.

p.42Hierarchy in 14th–15th-century Western Pueblo settlementsarchaeology · Great Kivas · intensive agriculture · elites

In short: In the 1300s and 1400s, many Western Pueblo settlements may have been hierarchical, or ranked. Labour-intensive farming may have produced leaders who managed labour and food. Large storage spaces around Great Kivas hint that food was managed in a formal, organised way. There is no direct proof that these spaces held food. But communities with little farmland could have kept extra food and imported food there. Specialised production of raw materials, ceramics and textiles also suggests an elite who made decisions. The text ends with a claim about how wealth was distributed.

The story

Picture the Four Corners region of the American Southwest. Here the Ancestral Puebloans built the great houses of Chaco Canyon, in about 850–1150 CE, and the cliff dwellings of Mesa Verde in the 1200s. Their older name, “Anasazi”, is now avoided.

After a great drought in the late 1200s, people left the Four Corners and gathered in large villages to the south and east. The Western Pueblos, such as the Hopi, Zuni and Acoma, grew in the 1300s–1400s. A kiva is a ceremonial room, fully or partly underground. A Great Kiva is a large one for the whole community.

Why do archaeologists debate hierarchy, a ranking of people from top to bottom? Historic and modern Pueblo societies are often called egalitarian, treating people as equals, and run by consensus, or general agreement. So evidence of past chiefs or elites is controversial.

With no written records, archaeologists use clues: settlement size, storage space, imported goods and craft specialisation, where certain people focus on one craft. They also use burial analysis, comparing the objects buried with different people, called grave goods.

Farming comes in two kinds. Intensive farming puts more labour into each acre, through irrigation, terraces and check dams, low walls that slow running water. Extensive farming uses more land, with less labour on each acre.

Remember it like this: Intensive farming is many hands on a small plot, like terraced hill fields. Extensive farming is few hands spread over a big one.

Words to know

Hierarchical
Ranked, with some people holding more authority than others.
Arable
Good for growing crops.
Differentiation of labour
Different people doing different, specialised jobs.
Elite
A small group with power or special advantages.

Reading tip: The text is full of hedges (“may have”, “suggested”, “could have”, “strengthen the possibility”): inference from indirect evidence. To judge how strong the case is, ask what other explanation could account for the evidence.

p.43Did Paleo-Indians kill off the Ice Age megafauna? (Martin vs Krech vs White)Pleistocene extinctions · overkill hypothesis · Clovis · dating

In short: Paul Martin argues that the arrival of Paleo-Indians about 11,000 years ago directly caused the late-Pleistocene extinctions in North America. Shepard Krech objects. Large animals vanished even where there is no evidence of hunting. Small animals, plants and insects vanished too. And he asserts that climate change did occur. Yet Krech still gives humans secondary, if not primary, blame. The historian Richard White says even that may be unsupported. Martin’s claim depends on humans arriving at the same time as the extinctions. And new finds suggest humans arrived much earlier.

The story

Picture North America in the Pleistocene, the long age of repeated ice ages. It lasted from about 2.6 million to 11,700 years ago, and our own age, the Holocene, followed it (see Foundation 6). Woolly mammoths, mastodons, giant ground sloths, sabre-toothed cats, dire wolves, native horses and camels, and giant beavers lived here. Such large animals are called megafauna. North America lost about 35 genera of them, each genus a group of related species.

The earliest people in the Americas are called Paleo-Indians. Migrants reached the Americas by crossing Beringia, a land bridge between Asia and North America that appeared when sea levels fell. Clovis spear points, found with mammoth bones, are named after Clovis, New Mexico, much as the Harappan civilisation is named after Harappa. For a long time these points were thought to mark the first Americans, a view called “Clovis-first”.

Today, sites older than Clovis are widely accepted. They include Monte Verde in Chile, from about 14,500 years ago, and fossil footprints at White Sands, New Mexico, reported as about 21,000–23,000 years old. Older texts give “11,000 years ago” in radiocarbon years, the uncorrected result of carbon-14 dating. Calibrated, or converted, to calendar years, Clovis is about 13,000 years ago. So when sources quote different numbers, both are often right.

Paul S. Martin’s idea is called the overkill hypothesis, or “blitzkrieg”, meaning lightning war. He argued for it from the 1960s to 2005. He proposed that skilled hunters met animals with no fear of humans. The rival explanations are climate change, disease and a disputed comet impact. The climate change in question is a sudden cold spell called the Younger Dryas.

In 1999 Shepard Krech wrote The Ecological Indian. It challenged the romantic stereotype, or idealised picture, of Native Americans as natural conservationists, people who protect nature. Richard White is a leading historian of the American West.

Americans grew up with the “ecological Indian” image, as in the famous 1971 “Crying Indian” TV advert against litter. That is why this debate stirs strong political feelings in the US.

Remember it like this: Radiocarbon years and calendar years are like a price in dollars and in rupees: “11,000” and “13,000” can name the same moment.

Words to know

Paleo-Indian
The earliest people to live in the Americas.
Megafauna
Large animals, weighing over about 45 kg.
Vindicated
Shown to be right.
Primary vs secondary responsibility
Primary means the main cause. Secondary means a contributing cause.

Reading tip: With several scholars, note each one’s claim, how strongly it is put, and whose claim it responds to.

p.44What is ethnicity? Primordial vs interest-group vs processsociology · Native political participation · Red Power · pan-Indian identity

In short: Sociologists use two definitions of ethnicity. In the primordial definition, ethnicity is unchanging. In this view, it meets a basic need to belong, through shared ancestry and culture. In the interest-group definition, ethnicity is a way to mobilise, or organise, people around shared economic interests. Both are useful, but neither captures how ethnicity changes in the US. The author prefers a process view. In this view, existing community bonds and cultural traits are adapted for practical, or instrumental, purposes as circumstances change. The example is the rise in Native American participation in mainstream US politics since the civil rights era of the 1960s.

The story

A 1908 play gave Americans the image of the “melting pot”: assimilation, with every group melting into one American identity. The rival image is the “salad bowl”, or multiculturalism, where groups keep their distinct identities.

Primordialism, linked to Edward Shils and Clifford Geertz, sees ethnicity as a deep attachment you are born into, rooted in blood, language, religion and custom. Instrumentalism, linked to the 1963 book Beyond the Melting Pot by Glazer and Moynihan, says ethnic groups act as interest groups competing for resources.

In 1969 Fredrik Barth offered a third idea, constructivism: ethnic identity is made and remade at the boundaries between groups. The “process” view of ethnicity is close to this.

The 1950s Relocation programme moved many Native people to cities. There, members of different tribes forged a shared “American Indian” political identity. This is pan-Indian identity, where “pan” means “all”, as in “pan-India”. Examples include the National Congress of American Indians, from 1944, and the American Indian Movement, from 1968.

The civil rights movement of 1954–68 inspired Red Power, the Native American movement, and also the Chicano, Asian American and women’s movements (see Foundation 1). Native activists occupied Alcatraz, a former prison island, in 1969–71. In 1972 they seized the headquarters of the BIA, the government office for Indian affairs.

From 1960 to 1990, the number of Americans calling themselves Native in the census grew far faster than births could explain. Sociologist Joane Nagel called this “ethnic renewal”, a textbook case of ethnicity as a process.

Remember it like this: Think “roots or rupees”: primordialism ties ethnicity to roots like blood, language and custom, while instrumentalism ties it to rupees, meaning groups competing for resources.

Words to know

Primordial
Existing from the very start, and basic.
Instrumental
Used as a tool to get something done.
Mobilise
To organise people to act together.
Interest group
An organised group that pushes for shared aims, often economic ones.

Reading tip: Keep straight which definition stresses culture and which stresses economics. The two are easy to swap.

↑ Back to top

Topic 3 · African Americans 8 readings · pp.46–53

All eight readings in this topic are set between the Civil War and the civil rights era. The big story runs like this: slavery, then freedom, then the gains of Reconstruction, then Jim Crow. After that come the Great Migration and the “New Negro”, the unions and the New Deal, World War II and the civil rights movement. One debate keeps coming back: accommodation or agitation. Should Black Americans work within the system, or confront it? The chapters below tell the timeline that American students memorise.

From slavery to freedom (1619–1877)

The story begins in 1619, when the first enslaved Africans arrived in Virginia, an English colony. When the new country wrote its Constitution in 1787, it protected slavery. The three-fifths clause counted each enslaved person as three-fifths of a person when seats in Congress were shared out among the states. The fugitive slave clause said that people who escaped from slavery had to be handed back to their owners.

In 1857, in the Dred Scott case, the Supreme Court ruled that Black Americans could not be citizens. Four years later the Civil War began (1861–65). It was fought between the North, called the Union, and the Southern slave states.

In 1863 President Lincoln issued the Emancipation Proclamation. “Emancipation” means setting people free. The proclamation also let Black men join the Union army, and about 180,000 served (see p.53).

The North won, and Reconstruction followed (1865–77). The South was rebuilt under the watch of the national government.

Three amendments, or additions to the Constitution, belong to this period. The 13th ended slavery. The 14th made everyone born in the country a citizen, Black Americans included, and promised “equal protection” of the laws. The 15th protected Black men’s right to vote.

For a while, Black men voted and held office. But Reconstruction ended with the Compromise of 1877, when federal troops, the national government’s soldiers, left the South.

Remember it like this: Count up from 13. The 13th Amendment sets people free, the 14th makes them citizens and the 15th gives Black men the vote: freedom, citizenship, vote.

Jim Crow and the first answers (1877–1909)

The years from 1877 to 1965 are the era of Jim Crow, the name for the South’s system of racial rules. It had four parts, and the first was legal segregation, which means keeping Black and white people apart by law. The second was disfranchisement, or taking away the vote. A fee to vote, the poll tax, and a reading test for voters, the literacy test, kept Black citizens from voting.

The third part was lynching: murder by a mob, with no trial. The fourth was sharecropping. Families who owned no land farmed someone else’s fields for a share of the crop, and they were often trapped in debt to the landowner.

In 1896, in Plessy v. Ferguson, the Supreme Court upheld “separate but equal” (see p.47). This meant segregation was allowed, as long as the separate facilities were equal.

Black leaders answered in different ways. In 1895 Booker T. Washington gave a speech in Atlanta that became known as the “Atlanta Compromise”. In 1903 W. E. B. Du Bois published The Souls of Black Folk. Du Bois then helped found the Niagara Movement in 1905, and in 1909 the NAACP, the National Association for the Advancement of Colored People (see p.48).

Remember it like this: Picture Jim Crow as a cage with four bars: separate places, no vote, mob violence and farm debt.

Moving north and organising (1910–1941)

From 1910 to 1970, about 6 million Black Southerners moved to cities in the North and the West. This was the Great Migration (see p.46). Meanwhile, the US joined World War I in 1917. About 380,000 Black soldiers served in that war, in segregated units.

In 1919 a wave of race riots became known as the “Red Summer”. This was also the time of Marcus Garvey’s movement, the Universal Negro Improvement Association, or UNIA. It was the time of the Harlem Renaissance too: a flowering of Black writing, music and art centred on Harlem, in New York (see p.50).

Black workers also organised, and in 1925 came the Brotherhood of Sleeping Car Porters. It was a union of Black porters, the attendants who looked after passengers in sleeping cars on overnight trains (see p.49). In the 1930s, during the New Deal, the national government’s answer to the Great Depression, unions grew fast. The CIO, a federation of unions, organised interracial unions, with Black and white members together (see p.51).

In 1941 the Brotherhood’s leader, A. Philip Randolph, threatened a March on Washington. The threat won a ban on discrimination in defence plants.

Remember it like this: Picture a river of about 6 million people flowing out of the South between 1910 and 1970. Then picture a march that never had to happen: in 1941 Randolph’s threat was enough.

War and the civil rights movement (1941–1968)

During World War II, the “Double V” campaign called for two victories. One was over fascism abroad, and the other was over racism at home (see p.52). In 1948 President Truman ordered the desegregation of the military: Black and white troops would no longer be kept apart.

Then came the civil rights movement. In 1954, in Brown v. Board of Education, the Supreme Court overturned “separate but equal” in schools. In 1955 Black citizens of Montgomery, a Southern city, refused to ride its buses in the Montgomery bus boycott. A boycott means refusing to buy or use something as a protest, like the boycott of British cloth during the Indian freedom struggle.

In 1963 came the March on Washington, where Martin Luther King Jr. gave his “I Have a Dream” speech. Its senior organiser was A. Philip Randolph, the man who had threatened a march back in 1941. Congress then passed the Civil Rights Act in 1964 and the Voting Rights Act in 1965. In 1968 King was assassinated.

Remember it like this: Climb the civil rights staircase: schools in 1954, buses in 1955, the March in 1963, the Civil Rights Act in 1964 and the Voting Rights Act in 1965.

A note on words

Older writings and quotations use the words “Negro” or “colored”. You can see this in the NAACP’s own name.

These were the standard words of their time. “Black” became the standard word in the late 1960s, and “African American” from the late 1980s.

Remember it like this: Words have birthdays. “Black” became standard in the late 1960s and “African American” in the late 1980s. Before that, texts said “Negro” or “colored”.

p.46Who joined the Great Migration?Great Migration · boll weevil · WWI labour demand · census data

In short: Between 1910 and 1930, more than 10% of Black Americans left the South. It is commonly assumed, but never proven, that the migrants were rural people. On this view, they were pushed to leave by the cotton collapse that the boll weevil caused, and drawn by Northern jobs once WWI halted immigration from Europe. That assumption is then used to blame their later lack of economic mobility on their rural inexperience. The author argues that the migrants may have been urban, skilled, employed Southern workers. In 1910 more than 600,000 Black workers were in “manufacturing and mechanical pursuits”. The author says this group, with their families, could easily have made up the whole migration. Southern wages were low, old artisans were being squeezed out, and rural newcomers undercut wages. So moving north made sense even for people who had jobs.

The story

Between 1910 and 1970, about 6 million Black Americans left the South. This was the Great Migration. They moved to Chicago, Detroit, New York’s Harlem, Philadelphia and Cleveland, and later to the West Coast. Popular retellings include Isabel Wilkerson’s book The Warmth of Other Suns and Jacob Lawrence’s Migration Series paintings.

Push factors drive people out of a place, and pull factors draw them to a new one. The South’s push factors included Jim Crow, lynching, the loss of the vote, sharecropping debt, the boll weevil and floods.

A sharecropping family owned no land, farmed a landowner’s fields for a share of the crop, and was often trapped in debt. The boll weevil is a beetle from Mexico whose larvae destroy cotton bolls, the pods that hold the fibre. It spread from Texas across the Cotton Belt, the South’s cotton country, over several decades. Indian farmers know a similar pest, the pink bollworm.

The pull came from the North. World War I cut immigration from Europe from over a million a year to a trickle, and laws in the 1920s restricted it further. So Northern factories in steel, meatpacking and cars needed workers. Wages were higher, and Black people could vote.

The Chicago Defender, a Black newspaper founded in 1905, openly urged migration. Pullman porters, the Black attendants on overnight sleeping-car trains, often smuggled it south (see p.49). Northern labour recruiters also travelled south.

Remember it like this: A push factor is a broom sweeping people out of a place. A pull factor is a magnet drawing them to a new one.

Words to know

Preponderance
The greater part, or the majority.
Concurrent
Happening at the same time.
Exodus
A mass departure, when many people leave a place.
Artisan
A skilled worker who works with their hands, such as a blacksmith, mason or carpenter.
Obsolescence
Becoming out of date.
Economic mobility
Moving up or down the ladder of income and jobs.

Reading tip: Note how strongly each claim is put: hedges such as “it is claimed”, “perhaps” and “could” mark possibilities, not proven facts.

p.47C. Vann Woodward’s The Strange Career of Jim Crowhistoriography · Brown v. Board · Reconstruction · Paine’s Common Sense · MLK

In short: Writing history can change history. In autumn 1954, months after Brown v. Board, C. Vann Woodward gave lectures. They challenged the dogma, or unquestioned belief, that Southern segregation was old, continuous and the same everywhere. He argued that Jim Crow laws were a deliberate effort to erase the gains Black Americans had made in Reconstruction. The research behind this grew partly from his work for the NAACP’s Brown campaign. The book, The Strange Career of Jim Crow, had an impact comparable to Paine’s Common Sense, although Paine was not a historian. Martin Luther King Jr. praised and quoted it often.

The story

Start with a name. “Jim Crow” began in the 1830s as a character in blackface minstrel shows, where white performers darkened their faces to mock Black people. Later the name came to mean the whole system of segregation laws and customs, from about 1877 to 1965. It meant separate schools, rail cars and water fountains, poll taxes and literacy tests, and lynching.

Before Jim Crow came Reconstruction (1865–77). Under the 13th, 14th and 15th Amendments, Black men voted, and around 2,000 held office, including US senators. After federal troops withdrew in 1877, white Southern leaders called “Redeemers” took back control.

The “prevailing dogma” said that segregation was a timeless Southern “folkway”, a custom that no law could change. W. G. Sumner summed it up: “stateways cannot change folkways”.

Woodward’s thesis, or main claim, was that race relations had been more fluid, or less fixed, in the 1870s and 1880s. In his account, rigid segregation laws came mainly in the 1890s and 1900s. If segregation was recent and man-made, it could be unmade. Later historians debated this “Woodward thesis”.

In May 1954, in Brown v. Board of Education, the Supreme Court overturned “separate but equal” in schools. Every judge agreed. Thurgood Marshall argued the case for the NAACP, and historians helped research what the people who wrote the 14th Amendment had intended. Virginia, where Woodward lectured, would soon lead “Massive Resistance” against desegregation.

In January 1776, Thomas Paine’s best-selling pamphlet Common Sense rallied the colonists to independence, before the Declaration of Independence. Hence Woodward’s joke that his first edition was like “a history of the American Revolution published in 1776”. He was writing while the civil rights revolution was still unfolding, like a cricket match report written during the first innings. Martin Luther King Jr. cited Woodward’s account of how segregation was created, most notably in his 1965 speech at the end of the Selma-to-Montgomery march.

Remember it like this: Folkways are the folk’s ways, meaning the people’s customs. Stateways are the state’s ways, meaning its laws. Sumner’s slogan said that the second cannot change the first.

Words to know

Dogma
A belief that people accept without question.
Codify
To write something into law.
Revisionist
Giving a new reading of accepted history.
Epochal
So important that it defines an era.
Anachronism
Something placed in the wrong period of time.
Impinge
To have an effect on something, or to intrude on it.

Reading tip: For the Paine comparison, note what the text says is alike and what it says is different: an analogy rests on both.

p.48Du Bois’s 1918 “Close Ranks” editorialDu Bois · Booker T. Washington · Trotter · accommodation vs agitation · WWI

In short: In a 1918 editorial, W. E. B. Du Bois urged African Americans to stop agitating for equality and to stand with white Americans during WWI. This surprised those who saw him as uncompromising and as the main opponent of Booker T. Washington’s accommodationism. In fact, Du Bois often moved along the spectrum, or range of positions, between Washington and militants such as William Monroe Trotter. In 1895 he praised Washington’s Atlanta speech. In 1903 he sided with Trotter. This was less for reasons of ideology, or political belief, than because Trotter told him about Washington’s efforts to silence critics in the Black press.

The story

W. E. B. Du Bois (1868–1963) was a sociologist. In 1895 he became the first Black American to earn a Harvard PhD. His 1903 book The Souls of Black Folk introduced “double consciousness”, the sense of always seeing yourself through others’ eyes. It declared that “the problem of the twentieth century is the problem of the color-line”, and one chapter criticised Booker T. Washington.

Du Bois co-founded the Niagara Movement in 1905 and the NAACP in 1909. He edited its magazine, The Crisis, and his editorial “Close Ranks” appeared there in July 1918. To “close ranks” is to stand closer together, like soldiers in a line.

Washington (1856–1915) was born enslaved, and in 1881 he founded a school, the Tuskegee Institute. His 1895 speech at the Atlanta Exposition, a large fair, became known as the “Atlanta Compromise”. It asked Black Southerners to focus on vocational education, or training for a trade, and on economic self-help. It also asked them to put off the fight over segregation and voting.

In the speech he said: “In all things that are purely social we can be as separate as the fingers, yet one as the hand…”. Washington’s network, the “Tuskegee Machine”, controlled funding and patronage, meaning jobs and favours handed out to supporters. It also quietly influenced Black newspapers or subsidised them, paying some of their costs.

William Monroe Trotter, a Harvard graduate, was Washington’s fiercest critic. From 1901 he edited the Boston Guardian. In 1903 he confronted Washington at a meeting, and he was jailed after this “Boston Riot”.

The US entered World War I in 1917, and Black soldiers served in segregated units. Wartime laws, the Espionage Act of 1917 and the Sedition Act of 1918, were used against publications that disagreed with the government. After the war, in 1919, Du Bois wrote: “We return. We return from fighting. We return fighting.” Today every APUSH student, in the high-school AP US History course, writes the standard compare-and-contrast essay on Washington and Du Bois.

Remember it like this: Match each man to his base: Washington to Tuskegee, Du Bois to The Crisis and Trotter to the Boston Guardian.

Words to know

Accommodationism
Working within an unjust system and putting off confrontation, to win small gains step by step.
Agitate
To campaign in public, again and again.
Militant
Ready to confront others aggressively.
Continuum
A spectrum, or range, between two extremes.
Solidarity
Unity behind a shared purpose.

Reading tip: Build a quick timeline of what Du Bois said or did in 1895, 1903, and 1918, and keep what the text reports separate from how it evaluates it.

p.49A. Philip Randolph & the Brotherhood of Sleeping Car PortersPullman Company · union recognition · company union · 1935

In short: A. Philip Randolph took over the Brotherhood of Sleeping Car Porters. He then fought a ten-year battle for recognition from the Pullman Company, meaning its agreement to bargain with the union. Pullman was the largest private employer of Black Americans. In 1935 the Brotherhood became the first Black union recognised by a major corporation. Randolph’s efforts in the fight helped change how Black workers saw unions and how they saw themselves as a group. Eventually, Randolph also helped weaken organised labour’s hostility to Black workers. Among the obstacles were Black workers’ understandable scepticism of unions, which had long shut them out, and Pullman’s own company union, set up by Pullman itself.

The story

Picture an overnight train journey in America. Passengers slept in luxury sleeping cars run by the Pullman Company. From the 1860s the company hired Black men, many of them formerly enslaved, as porters. A porter was like the attendant who looks after passengers in an AC coach of an Indian overnight train.

Passengers often called the porters “George”, after the company’s owner, George Pullman. The work meant long hours, low pay and dependence on tips. Yet porters were respected, well-travelled figures in Black communities.

A. Philip Randolph (1889–1979), a Harlem socialist who edited The Messenger, organised the Brotherhood of Sleeping Car Porters in 1925. Later, in 1941, his threatened March on Washington pushed President Roosevelt to ban discrimination in defence industries. His pressure helped win Truman’s 1948 order to desegregate the military, that is, to stop keeping Black and white troops apart. He was also the senior organiser of the 1963 March on Washington, working with Bayard Rustin.

Union recognition means that an employer agrees to bargain with a union as the workers’ representative. It is the gateway to collective bargaining, where workers negotiate pay and conditions as one group. A company union is a “union” created and controlled by the employer to keep an independent union out. In the 1930s, national laws banned such unions and made recognition achievable: the Railway Labor Act amendments of 1934 and the Wagner Act of 1935.

Most craft unions in the AFL, a large alliance of unions, barred Black workers (see p.14 and p.51). Craft unions grouped skilled workers by trade. Randolph spent decades fighting racism inside the AFL, and later inside the AFL-CIO, formed when the AFL merged with another alliance, the CIO. Today Pullman porters and Randolph are staples of Black History Month lessons each February, often linked to the 1963 March on Washington.

Remember it like this: A company union is a student council picked by the principal. It looks like the students’ voice, but it answers to the principal.

Words to know

Recognition
Formal acceptance of a union as the group that bargains for the workers.
Antagonism
Active hostility, or ill will.
Formidable
So difficult that it seems frightening.
Parlor car
A luxury railway carriage for daytime travel, with individual seats.

Reading tip: Distinguish the obstacles from the outcomes, and note which effects the author attributes to the struggle.

p.50Marcus Garvey & Wallace’s “revitalization movements”UNIA · WWI Black veterans · Red Summer · anthropology theory

In short: Marcus Garvey arrived from Jamaica in 1916, at the right moment in history. Black soldiers coming home from World War I in 1918 were an ideal audience for his message of unity, pride and better conditions. They had joined the army hoping to share in the American belief in individual success. But in the army they met segregation and humiliating treatment. Then they came home to a country that had not changed and was still segregated. The anthropologist Anthony F. C. Wallace argued that a visible gap between what a culture expects and how things really are can spark a revitalization movement. This is an organised, conscious effort to build a culture that meets those expectations. The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence: “Some scholars have argued that Garvey created …”.

The story

Marcus Garvey founded the Universal Negro Improvement Association, or UNIA, in Jamaica in 1914. In 1916 he arrived in Harlem, in New York City, and built it up there. By the early 1920s it was the largest mass movement in Black American history up to that point.

Garvey preached racial pride: “Up, you mighty race!” He also preached economic self-reliance, much like India’s Swadeshi idea. He called for “Africa for the Africans” and a back-to-Africa scheme. He started the Black Star Line shipping company in 1919, founded the Negro World newspaper and gave his followers the red, black and green flag.

In 1923 he was convicted of mail fraud, cheating people through the post, and in 1927 he was deported. Still, he influenced Malcolm X, the Rastafarian faith, Black Power and Pan-Africanism, the idea of unity among people of African descent everywhere. He also feuded bitterly with Du Bois, another Black leader.

Back in World War I, about 380,000 Black soldiers had served, most in segregated labour units. The 369th Infantry, the “Harlem Hellfighters”, fought under French command and won the Croix de Guerre, a French medal for bravery. The veterans came home to the Red Summer of 1919, with race riots in dozens of cities. Some men still in uniform were lynched, killed by a mob without a trial.

Anthropologists study human cultures. In 1956 one of them, Anthony F. C. Wallace, published an article called “Revitalization Movements”. To revitalize is to give new life. His examples include the Seneca prophet Handsome Lake’s religion, the Ghost Dance of 1890 and Melanesian cargo cults.

Finally, there is the American success ethos, or guiding belief. It celebrates the self-made man, like the heroes of Horatio Alger’s rags-to-riches stories. Americans also call it the “American Dream”.

Remember it like this: Revitalize means “give new life”, like watering a wilting plant. For Wallace, the spark can be a gap people can see between what they expect and what they get.

Words to know

Constituency
A group of supporters.
Ethos
The core beliefs that guide a culture.
Indignities
Treatment that humiliates someone.
Pan-Africanism
Unity among people of African descent all over the world.

Reading tip: Map each element of Wallace’s theory (expectation, reality, gap, movement) onto the Garvey case as you read.

p.51Southern Black industrial workers & unions, 1930s–40slabour unions · occupational segregation · seniority · employer power

In short: In the 1930s and 1940s, Black industrial workers in the South strongly backed unions. They made up 80% of the unskilled factory workers there. The AFL shut them out or kept them segregated. The CIO instead organised integrated unions that stood for equal rights, and Black union members were its backbone. Yet unions, CIO unions included, often used contracts negotiated by whites to keep occupational segregation in place. Black workers saw employers’ power as the central cause of their being pushed to the margins. So they allied with white workers, despite those workers’ racism. In the end, their persistence paid off.

The story

Picture the American South in the 1930s and 1940s, with its tobacco, steel, lumber, docks and meatpacking. Birmingham, Alabama, made steel, and R. J. Reynolds made tobacco goods in Winston-Salem.

There were two main groups of unions (see p.14). The AFL was craft-based, with one union per skilled trade, and often whites-only. The CIO was industrial, taking in all workers in an industry, and officially egalitarian, standing for equal rights. In 1943 workers at R. J. Reynolds formed a CIO local, or branch, that was heavily Black and female.

A union contract, the deal it strikes with an employer, could set up separate “Negro” and “white” job lines. Each line had its own seniority list, ranking workers by years of service. So Black workers could not transfer or be promoted into skilled, better-paid jobs. Title VII of the Civil Rights Act outlawed such arrangements in 1964.

The South was also hard ground for unions, with low wages and hostile employers. After the Taft–Hartley Act of 1947, “right-to-work” laws spread, letting workers stay out of unions. The CIO’s postwar drive to unionise the South, “Operation Dixie”, failed. Dixie is a nickname for the South.

Remember it like this: Separate job lines with their own seniority lists are like two ladders side by side. You climb your own ladder year by year, but you can never step across to the taller one.

Words to know

Occupational segregation
Sorting workers into different jobs because of their race or sex.
Marginalisation
Pushing a group out to the edges of power or of economic life.
Contract
Here, the agreement a union negotiates with an employer for the workers as a group.
Backbone
The main source of strength.

Reading tip: Track each “Yet” and “However” turn, and note whose actions or views each sentence describes (the AFL, the CIO, white unionists, Black workers).

p.52Unions and Black workers in WWIIWWII home front · Double V · hate strikes · Korstad & Lichtenstein · Herbert Hill

In short: Some historians say World War II created a dynamic alliance between unions and the Black community that advanced civil rights. They also say its collapse after the war was a “lost opportunity”. Other scholars say organised labour always defended white workers’ privileges. The author says reality can’t be reduced to either view. Unions faced a choice. They could keep the prewar status quo, or they could take an inclusive approach. That meant equal participation in union affairs, access to skilled, high-paying jobs, and protection from management’s arbitrary power. They often voiced support for inclusion, but more often favoured entrenched interests.

The story

On the American home front, meaning life back home during World War II, war production boomed and workers were scarce. This drew a second, larger wave of Black migration. In 1941, after Randolph threatened a march (see p.49), the president’s Executive Order 8802 banned discrimination in defence industries. A new Fair Employment Practice Committee was set up to enforce it.

In 1942 the Pittsburgh Courier newspaper launched the “Double V” campaign. Its two Vs stood for victory over fascism abroad and victory over racism at home.

Some white workers pushed back. In “hate strikes”, they walked out when Black workers were promoted. At Detroit’s Packard plant in 1943, about 25,000 struck, and the Detroit race riot of 1943 followed. In the Philadelphia transport strike of 1944, the army took over.

One reading of these years is the “lost opportunity” thesis. In 1988 Robert Korstad and Nelson Lichtenstein described what they called “civil rights unionism” in CIO unions led by the political left. They argued that postwar anti-communism killed it.

They pointed to two events. First, the Taft–Hartley Act of 1947 made union officers swear they were not communists. Then, in 1949–50, the CIO expelled left-led unions, among them the ones most committed to racial equality.

Herbert Hill was the labour director of the NAACP, a civil rights group. He is the best-known critic of unions’ record on race.

Remember it like this: Double V is two victory signs raised together: one for beating fascism abroad, one for beating racism at home.

Words to know

Status quo
The way things already are.
Entrenched interests
Groups already dug in, here senior white workers, defending their advantages.
Demise
The end or death of something.
Arbitrary authority
Power used without fair rules, such as firing workers whenever the boss likes.
Reconcilable
Able to be made to fit together.

Reading tip: Distinguish what unions said from what they did, and note which scholars hold which view.

p.53Glatthaar’s Forged in Battle: Black soldiers & white officers in the Civil WarUSCT · 54th Massachusetts · discrimination in pay · labour duty · book review

In short: Joseph Glatthaar’s Forged in Battle (1990) is not the first fine study of Black soldiers in the Civil War and their white officers. But it uses more letters and diaries, including rare ones by Black soldiers. It also looks more closely at relations between Black and white men inside Black regiments. Its thesis is in the title: shared dangers in combat forged loyalty, friendship and respect. Glatthaar accurately records discrimination in pay, promotion, medical care and assignments, and the soldiers’ campaign for the chance to fight. That chance stayed limited, because most Black units were kept in rear areas and labour battalions. The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence as the reviewer turns to their combat death rate.

The story

In the American Civil War, the Union, meaning the North, fought the Confederacy, the Southern slave states. About two-thirds of all deaths in the war came not from battle but from diseases such as dysentery, typhoid, malaria and pneumonia. At first, Black men were barred from the Union army. Then the Emancipation Proclamation, a declaration of freedom issued on 1 January 1863, allowed them to enlist.

About 179,000 Black men served in the United States Colored Troops, or USCT. That was roughly 10% of the Union army, and about 19,000 more served in the navy. Their officers were almost all white, much as Indian sepoys in the British Indian Army served under British officers.

Two episodes became famous. In 1863 the 54th Massachusetts regiment attacked Fort Wagner, an assault later dramatised in the 1989 film Glory. In 1864, at the Fort Pillow massacre, Confederates killed Black troops who were trying to surrender. The Confederacy also threatened to enslave or execute captured Black soldiers.

Pay was unequal too: a Black soldier got $10 a month, minus $3 for clothing. A white private, the lowest rank of soldier, got $13 a month plus clothing. In protest, the 54th Massachusetts refused any pay at all for 18 months. In 1864 Congress made the pay equal.

Armies also need labour. Fatigue duty meant digging trenches, building fortifications and guarding supply depots, and labour battalions did such work. The rear echelon means the area behind the front lines.

Americans meet this history at school. The film Glory is shown in many US classrooms, and the USCT is a standard textbook topic.

Remember it like this: Ten dollars minus three for clothes, against thirteen plus clothes. The 54th Massachusetts chose no pay at all over unequal pay.

Words to know

Regiment
An army unit of about 1,000 soldiers.
Officer vs enlisted
Officers are the commanders. Enlisted men are the ordinary soldiers.
Mortality rate
How many deaths there are for a given number of people.
Thesis
The main argument.

Reading tip: Book reviews mix the reviewer’s judgments with a summary of the book. Separate the two, and when statistics appear, note which point each one supports.

↑ Back to top

Topic 4 · Minorities (Black, Native, Asian, Hispanic) & Latin America 3 readings · pp.55–57

This topic has only three readings. But readings about minorities often expect you to know some background on Asian Americans, Hispanic Americans and affirmative action. The chapters below give you that background.

Who’s who: the census and Hispanic Americans

Like India, the United States counts all its people in a census. The 2020 Census gives a rough picture of who is who. About 58% of Americans are white and not Hispanic. About 19% are Hispanic or Latino, about 12–14% are Black and about 6–7% are Asian. American Indians and Alaska Natives make up about 1–3%. About 10% are multiracial, meaning they belong to more than one race.

Two of these labels need care. Hispanic is a census term from the 1970s for people of Spanish-speaking origin. Latino means someone of Latin American origin, and “Latinx” is a newer form that people argue about. In US statistics, Hispanic is an ethnicity, a matter of origin and culture, not a race. So a Hispanic American can be of any race.

The groups under this label have very different stories. Mexican Americans go back to 1848. In that year the Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo (p.28) ended the Mexican–American War, and Mexico handed California and the Southwest to the United States. So the Mexican people living on that land found themselves inside the US.

Later came the Bracero programme (1942–64), which brought in Mexican guest workers, people allowed in to work for a time. Then came the Chicano Movement, in which Mexican Americans campaigned for their rights. In 1962 César Chávez and Dolores Huerta founded the United Farm Workers, a union for farm labourers.

Two other groups have their own stories. Puerto Rico is an island that belongs to the United States, and Puerto Ricans have been US citizens since 1917. Cuban Americans came mainly after Castro’s revolution of 1959, and they settled in Miami.

Remember it like this: “Hispanic” is an umbrella, not a race. Under it stand Mexican Americans since 1848, Puerto Ricans as citizens since 1917 and Cuban Americans in Miami after 1959.

Asian Americans: from the railroad to the campus

The story starts with a railroad. Chinese workers built the Transcontinental Railroad, the line across the continent finished in 1869. Yet in 1882 the Chinese Exclusion Act shut Chinese immigrants out. It was the first US law to bar immigrants because of their nationality, and it lasted until 1943.

Indians enter the story in 1923. In US v. Bhagat Singh Thind, the Supreme Court looked at the case of an Indian Sikh and former US soldier. It ruled that he was not “white”, so he could not become a citizen. Then in 1956 Dalip Singh Saund became the first Asian American in Congress, the US parliament.

In 1942, during World War II, about 120,000 Japanese Americans were locked up. This is called the Japanese American incarceration, or internment. In Korematsu (1944), the Supreme Court upheld it. In 1988 the government made an official apology. It also paid each survivor $20,000 in reparations, money paid to make up for a wrong.

The 1965 Immigration Act ended national-origin quotas, the limits on how many people could come from each country. This led to large immigration from Asia and Latin America. Today Indian Americans are the second-largest Asian group in the country.

The 1960s also gave this group two labels. The “model minority” stereotype, which pictures Asian Americans as an especially successful minority, dates from 1966. The term “Asian American” was coined at Berkeley in 1968. Then the student strikes of 1968–69 at San Francisco State and Berkeley gave birth to Asian American studies (p.56).

Remember it like this: Four dates, four doors: 1882 slams shut on the Chinese, 1923 shuts citizenship to Thind, 1942 locks Japanese Americans up, and 1965 swings wide open.

Affirmative action and help for minority businesses

Affirmative action means active efforts to widen chances for minority groups in jobs, education and business. The term comes from a 1961 executive order, a direct order from the President, issued by John F. Kennedy (JFK).

In 1969 President Nixon’s “Philadelphia Plan” set goals for hiring minority workers. Nixon also promoted “Black capitalism”, the idea of helping Black Americans own businesses, along with programmes for minority businesses (p.55).

The Supreme Court then made four key rulings. In Bakke (1978), it banned racial quotas, fixed numbers of places kept for one group, but allowed race to count as one factor. In Croson (1989) and Adarand (1995), it applied “strict scrutiny” to racial set-asides, which are shares of government contracts kept for minority firms.

Strict scrutiny is the toughest test a court uses. The government must show a very strong reason for any rule based on race. Finally, in SFFA v. Harvard (2023), the Court ended race-conscious college admissions, meaning admissions that take race into account.

Remember it like this: India’s reservations keep a fixed share of seats for certain groups. In America, Bakke (1978) banned fixed quotas but let race count as one factor. Then SFFA (2023) ended race-conscious college admissions.

p.55Aiding minority businesses: SBA loans and a newer approachSBA · small business · Black capitalism · policy comparison

In short: Federal help for minority businesses began in the 1960s. It started with the Small Business Administration’s guaranteed loans and its help with management and technical matters. Many firms were founded. But inexperienced managers, poor locations and shortages of capital led to high failure rates. Fifteen years later, minority firms’ receipts were not quite 2% of the national total. Recently, policymakers adopted an approach meant to speed up the growth of this sector by “moving away from directly …”. The excerpt breaks off there.

The story

The Small Business Administration, or SBA, is a US government agency founded in 1953. It guarantees bank loans to small firms: if a firm cannot repay, the SBA pays the bank. It also gives advice, and through its “8(a)” programme it keeps a share of government contracts for disadvantaged firms.

In 1964 President Lyndon Johnson (LBJ) began a War on Poverty that included small-business loans. Then riots broke out in Watts in 1965 and in Detroit in 1967. In 1968 a government panel, the Kerner Commission, warned of “two societies, one black, one white—separate and unequal”.

Next, President Nixon promoted “Black capitalism”, which meant helping Black Americans own businesses. In 1969 his government set up the Office of Minority Business Enterprise.

Firms raise money in two basic ways. A loan is debt: it must be repaid with interest, whatever happens. Venture capital is equity: it buys a share of ownership, so the investor shares the risk and usually brings expertise and contacts. Think of the difference between a moneylender and a business partner.

Why do small firms fail? Some are undercapitalised, with too little start-up money. Others face weak markets, such as low-income neighbourhoods, or suffer from inexperienced management.

Remember it like this: An SBA guaranteed loan works like a parent who stands guarantor for a student’s education loan. If the borrower cannot pay, the guarantor pays the bank.

Words to know

Receipts
All the money that businesses take in.
Guaranteed loan
A loan that a third party, here the government, promises to repay if the borrower cannot.
Capitalise
To supply with capital, meaning money.
Intermediary
A go-between organisation.

Reading tip: Note each problem the text names precisely (causes and statistics). It is easy to swap one cause for another.

p.56Teaching introductory Asian American studiesethnic studies · academic publishing · reference works

In short: Teachers of introductory Asian American studies face a dilemma that did not exist decades ago, when there were almost no texts. Now there are excellent anthologies, and new books appear almost every week. So even experts struggle to choose, and non-experts who teach survey courses struggle more. A complicating factor is that there are still no one-volume reference works, such as biographical dictionaries or desk encyclopedias. In such books, students could look up basic facts on Asian American people, institutions, history and culture.

The story

Picture San Francisco in 1968. Students at San Francisco State strike under the banner of the Third World Liberation Front. Their strike of 1968–69 is the longest student strike in US history. Students at UC Berkeley strike too.

The San Francisco strike created the first College of Ethnic Studies. Ethnic studies, the study of ethnic and racial groups in America, was born in these strikes. Asian American, Black, Chicano and Native American studies programmes followed across the country.

“Asian American” is a pan-ethnic umbrella, one label over many groups. It covers Chinese, Indian, Filipino, Vietnamese, Korean, Japanese and many other Americans, with very different histories, languages and religions. In the same way, the one word “Indian” covers people of many languages and faiths.

Three words from teaching are worth knowing. An anthology is one book that collects pieces by many different writers. A survey course is a broad introductory course. A reference work, such as an encyclopedia or a dictionary, is for looking up facts, not for reading straight through.

Remember it like this: An anthology is a thali: many dishes on one plate. A reference work is a dictionary on the shelf: you dip in for one fact, then put it back.

Words to know

Dilemma
A situation that forces a hard choice between options.
Nonexpert
A teacher who comes from a neighbouring field.
Complicating factor
Something that makes a problem worse.

Reading tip: Watch how the text itself uses a familiar word like “dilemma”. What matters is the text’s precise sense, not the dictionary’s general one.

p.57The Great Depression in Latin Americaeconomic history · Latin America · 1930s · import substitution

In short: In the 1980s most economic historians argued that the Depression hit Latin America earlier than the US but was less severe there. They also argued that it did not significantly slow industrial growth. To work out total manufacturing output and profits, they relied on government tax and export records and on industrial censuses. The author objects that early-20th-century Latin American statistics are unreliable and inconsistent, especially the manufacturing data. In the author’s view, those data were gathered from factory owners for tax purposes and so may be distorted. The author adds that output and profit often move in opposite directions, so you cannot work out one from the other.

The story

The Great Depression began with the US stock market crash of October 1929. It was at its worst in 1932–33. US output fell by about a quarter, unemployment hit about 25% and thousands of banks failed. World trade collapsed too, as the US raised import taxes with the Smoot–Hawley tariff of 1930 and other countries hit back.

Latin America lived on commodity exports, raw goods sold abroad. Brazil and Colombia sold coffee, Cuba sold sugar, Chile sold nitrates and copper, and Argentina sold beef and wheat. Then commodity prices crashed, and export earnings and government revenues collapsed.

Political upheaval followed. In 1930 a wave of revolutions and coups, sudden seizures of power, swept the region. Argentina had one, and in Brazil Getúlio Vargas took power. Populism, politics built on leaders who claim to speak for ordinary people, was on the rise.

Unable to afford imports, countries began making goods at home, such as textiles, processed food and cement. This is called import-substitution industrialisation, or ISI. Many historians therefore saw the Depression as a spur to Latin American industry. ISI later became official policy in the 1950s, linked with the economist Raúl Prebisch and the UN’s Economic Commission for Latin America.

Finally, keep two simple ideas in mind. Output is the quantity a firm produces, and profit is its revenue minus its costs. A firm can produce more while earning less, for example by cutting prices to keep its factories running. The reverse can happen too.

Figures reported to tax officials give owners a motive to under-report, which means to report less than the truth. This is a classic source of bias, a steady tilt in the numbers.

Remember it like this: ISI means “can’t buy it, so make it”: when imports cost too much, a country starts making its own cloth, food and cement. India followed a similar path for decades after independence.

Words to know

Industrial census
A government survey of what factories produce.
Correlation
When two quantities tend to move together.
Impede
To hold back or get in the way of.
Tax revenues
The money a government collects from taxes.

Reading tip: Separate the scholars’ claims from the author’s objections, and note what each objection targets: the data or the reasoning.

↑ Back to top

Topic 5 · Brain 5 readings · pp.59–63

Brain readings explain their own science. But they use words that American students learn in high-school biology and in “Psych 101”, the introductory college course in psychology. Examples are neurotransmitter, receptor, hypothalamus and episodic memory. Learn the three short chapters below once, and all five readings will go much faster.

How neurons talk

Follow one message on its journey through the brain. A brain cell, or neuron, collects signals from other cells on its branching dendrites. If it is excited enough, it “fires”. An electrical pulse, called an action potential, then travels down its axon, the cell’s output cable.

The axon meets the next cell at a synapse, a tiny gap between the two cells. There the neuron releases chemical messengers called neurotransmitters. They cross the gap and attach to receptors on the next cell, like keys fitting into locks.

Some signals are excitatory: they make the next neuron more likely to fire. Others are inhibitory: they make it less likely to fire. Adenosine is one example of an inhibitory signal (p.60).
