The body has another way to send messages. Hormones, such as insulin, melatonin and cortisol, are carried in the blood. Neurotransmitters, by contrast, act locally, across synapses. So a hormone is like a radio broadcast, while a neurotransmitter is a whisper to the person next to you.

The brain is also careful about what gets in. Its tiny blood vessels, the capillaries, are tightly sealed, and this seal is called the blood–brain barrier. As a result, many molecules can enter only through specific carrier proteins, called transporters, which act like guarded gates (p.59).

All this happens on a huge scale. The human brain has about 86 billion neurons and around 100 trillion synapses.

Remember it like this: Electricity runs along the axon, and chemistry crosses the gap. Neurotransmitters are keys and receptors are locks. A hormone is a radio broadcast, but a neurotransmitter is a whisper.

Brain map & the main chemicals

Now take a tour of the brain itself. The wrinkled outer layer is the cerebral cortex, which handles thought, language and perception. It is divided into four lobes. The frontal lobe plans, the parietal lobe handles touch and space, the temporal lobe handles hearing and memory, and the occipital lobe handles vision.

Other parts have more specific jobs. The hippocampus forms new memories (p.63). The amygdala handles fear and emotion. The cerebellum handles coordination, and the brainstem runs breathing and the heartbeat.

The hypothalamus looks after hunger, thirst, body temperature and hormones. It also looks after the body clock (SCN, p.62).

Neurons pass on their messages with several main chemicals. Serotonin affects mood, sleep and appetite, and antidepressants such as Prozac boost it. Dopamine is linked to reward and movement, and to Parkinson’s disease. Norepinephrine keeps us alert, and acetylcholine works in muscles and memory.

Two chemicals are the brain’s main “go” and “stop” signals. Glutamate says “go” and GABA says “stop”, like the accelerator and the brake of a car.

Remember it like this: Glutamate is the brain’s accelerator and GABA its brake. Serotonin goes with mood, dopamine with reward, and the hippocampus is where new memories are formed.

Why rats and mice?

Why do so many brain experiments use rats and mice? Their brain chemistry resembles ours. Scientists can also tightly control their diet, the light they live in and the drugs they receive.

The typical design changes one thing, the variable. It then compares the result against a control, a group in which that thing was not changed. Brain readings usually turn on what an experiment shows, or on how an apparent exception is explained.

Remember it like this: A fair test is two identical pots of dal with extra salt added to only one. Whatever tastes different must come from the salt.

p.59Food → tryptophan → serotonin: diet changes brain chemistryneurotransmitters · amino acids · insulin · experiment sequence

In short: The brain was once thought to be independent of the body’s metabolism. Rat studies showed that the brain’s production of serotonin depends directly on food. After a meal, tryptophan in the blood, tryptophan in the brain and serotonin in the brain all rose together. Injected insulin did the same, and so did the rat’s own insulin, released after a carbohydrate meal. Surprise: adding lots of protein to the meal made brain tryptophan and serotonin fall, even though protein contains tryptophan. The reason is that tryptophan enters the brain by the same route as other amino acids, such as tyrosine and leucine. Protein raises those competitors by a much larger proportion. So the ratio of tryptophan to its competitors drops, and less tryptophan reaches the brain.

The story

Picture a laboratory at MIT, a leading American university, in the 1970s. There, Richard Wurtman and John Fernstrom carried out classic research on food and the brain. Their work helped launch a whole new field, “nutrition and the brain”.

To follow their work, start with protein. Proteins are chains built from 20 kinds of small building blocks called amino acids, like necklaces strung from 20 kinds of beads. Nine amino acids are “essential”, which means the body cannot make them. Tryptophan is one of the nine, so the brain’s supply of it depends on diet.

The brain turns tryptophan into serotonin in steps. Tryptophan becomes 5-HTP, then serotonin, and serotonin can later become melatonin. The enzyme, or helper protein, that runs the first step normally works below full capacity. So when more tryptophan arrives, more serotonin is made, just as a half-busy factory makes more when more raw material comes in.

To reach the brain, tryptophan must pass through a transporter, a gate that it shares with the other “large neutral amino acids”. These are tyrosine, phenylalanine, leucine, isoleucine and valine. Picture several queues sharing one turnstile at a metro station. In most proteins, tryptophan is the scarcest of this group.

The pancreas releases insulin when carbohydrates raise blood sugar. Besides moving glucose into cells, insulin pushes many of the competing amino acids into muscle. Tryptophan, much of which rides in the blood on a protein called albumin, is less affected, so it faces less competition at the gate. This is the standard textbook account.

Many Americans “know” a piece of Thanksgiving folklore: the tryptophan in the holiday turkey makes you sleepy. Scientists call this a myth. Turkey has no more tryptophan than chicken, and in a protein-heavy meal the competing amino acids crowd tryptophan out. The drowsiness comes from the sheer size of the meal, much like the sleepiness after a big wedding lunch in India.

Remember it like this: Like milk that becomes curd, then butter, then ghee, tryptophan becomes 5-HTP, then serotonin, then melatonin. The body cannot make the “milk” itself, so it must come from food.

Words to know

Metabolic processes
The body’s chemistry for breaking down food and building tissue.
Precursor
A substance from which another is made. Tryptophan is a precursor of serotonin.
Coupled
Linked, so that when one changes, the other does too.
Elicit
To draw out or bring about. A meal elicits insulin.

Reading tip: With a sequence of experiments, jot a quick table (what was given → what rose or fell) as you read.

p.60How caffeine works: blocking adenosine receptorsreceptors · antagonist · methylxanthines · adenosine

In short: Snyder, Daly and Bruns proposed that caffeine affects behaviour by working against adenosine. Adenosine is a natural brain chemical that reduces the firing of neurons, apparently by holding back the release of neurotransmitters. Adenosine must first bind to receptors, of which there are at least two classes, A1 and A2. Caffeine is similar in structure to adenosine. It binds to both classes and blocks adenosine, so neurons fire more readily. Across related compounds, binding to the receptors generally went with stimulation. An apparent exception, IBMX, bound very well but reduced how much mice moved around. The authors don’t see this as a major problem, because the compound has mixed effects, which is common for psychoactive drugs. Even caffeine reduces mouse movement at very low concentrations and increases it at higher ones.

The story

The longer you stay awake, the more adenosine builds up in your brain, and sleep clears it away. It is a by-product of using ATP, the cell’s energy molecule, and its build-up is called “sleep pressure”. Caffeine does not really give you energy. It hides the tiredness signal, like a strip of tape over a warning light on a car’s dashboard.

Caffeine belongs to a chemical family called the methylxanthines. Its relatives include theophylline, found in tea, and theobromine, found in chocolate. IBMX is a man-made methylxanthine used in laboratories.

Why did scientists need a new theory of caffeine? The older explanation said that caffeine blocks an enzyme inside cells, called phosphodiesterase. But that would take concentrations far higher than a coffee drinker’s brain reaches. Blocking receptors works at normal doses.

Pharmacology, the science of drugs, follows a standard logic. If a drug acts through a receptor, compounds that bind to it more tightly should generally act more strongly. So the key evidence is a correlation, a link between how well compounds bind and how strong their effect is. Later research found four kinds of adenosine receptor: A1, A2A, A2B and A3.

One drug can also do opposite things at different doses. Such effects are called biphasic, or dose-dependent. Alcohol is a familiar example: at low doses it is mildly stimulating, but at high doses it makes people sleepy.

Solomon Snyder of Johns Hopkins, a top American university, is one of the best-known American brain scientists. In 1973 he and Candace Pert discovered the brain’s opiate receptor. Coffee itself is central to American working life, from the office “coffee break” to Starbucks. The FDA, the US food and drug regulator, calls about 400 mg of caffeine a day, roughly four cups of brewed coffee, generally safe for healthy adults.

Remember it like this: Coffee, tea and chocolate are cousins. Caffeine, theophylline and theobromine all belong to one family, the methylxanthines, and IBMX is their lab-made relative.

Words to know

Psychoactive
Affecting the mind or behaviour.
Bind
To attach to a receptor.
Locomotion
Movement. How much mice run around is the standard lab measure of stimulation.
Stumbling block
An obstacle, here one that stands in the way of accepting a hypothesis.

Reading tip: When the author mentions something, ask why: what role it plays in the argument, not just what it says.

p.61Searle vs the brain-as-computer metaphorphilosophy of mind · Chinese Room · syntax vs semantics · AI

In short: The idea of the brain as an information processor dominates neuroscience. The philosopher John Searle objects. In his view, computers only follow algorithms. They handle symbols by their form, or syntax, without grasping meaning, or semantics. People understand meaning because of what Searle calls the brain’s “causal powers”. The author then asks how a brain could work except by turning what it learns into a code passed between neurons. If that code can be cracked, a computer should in principle be able to simulate it. Searle would reply that even a perfect simulation wouldn’t really be thinking. It would only be acting as if it were.

The story

In 1950 Alan Turing proposed a simple test for machines, now called the Turing Test. Suppose you chat by typing with someone you cannot see. If you cannot tell whether a machine or a person is typing, you should call the machine intelligent.

In 1980 the philosopher John Searle offered a famous thought experiment, the Chinese Room. Picture a man who knows no Chinese, sitting in a room with a rulebook. Questions in Chinese come in through a slot, and by matching symbols to rules he passes out perfect Chinese answers. Outsiders think the room understands Chinese, yet nobody inside understands a word.

Searle’s moral is that running a program means following rules of form, or syntax, and that is never enough for understanding meaning, or semantics. So the Chinese Room aims to show that passing the Turing Test does not prove understanding. Searle’s target is “strong AI”, the claim that the right program literally is a mind. “Weak AI” says only that computers are useful tools for studying minds.

Searle’s favourite point is about simulation. A computer simulation of a rainstorm leaves nobody wet, and a simulated fire burns nothing. So, he argues, simulating a mind is not the same as having one.

Where does understanding come from, then? Searle holds that consciousness is a biological product of brains, just as photosynthesis is a product of plant cells. Brains, in his phrase, have “causal powers”. Critics complain that he never says which brain properties do the work.

The debate is alive again today. People argue over whether ChatGPT-style AI really “understands”, or merely predicts the next word, and that argument is a modern rerun of the Chinese Room. In America the Chinese Room is a staple of introductory college courses in philosophy and cognitive science. Films such as Her and Ex Machina have also made “can machines think?” a familiar American debate.

Remember it like this: The Chinese Room is like reciting a shloka perfectly without understanding a word. The form is flawless, and that is syntax. The meaning never arrives, and meaning is semantics.

Words to know

Algorithm
A step-by-step set of rules a machine can follow.
Syntax
The rules of form: how symbols are arranged.
Semantics
Meaning.
Metaphor
Describing one thing as if it were another, such as calling the brain a computer.
Fathomable
Able to be understood.
Simulate
To imitate a process with a model.

Reading tip: Separate three voices: the dominant view, Searle’s objection, and the author’s own comments. Rhetorical questions in a text usually carry the author’s position. To judge what fits Searle’s view, use Searle’s stated reasoning, not the author’s.

p.62Body clocks and the suprachiasmatic nuclei (SCNs)circadian rhythms · hypothalamus · clock genes · melatonin

In short: For a long time, scientists believed that two clusters of nerve cells in the hypothalamus, the suprachiasmatic nuclei (SCNs), controlled our circadian rhythms. These are the roughly 24-hour cycles that keep in step with day and night. Here is the supporting evidence. In some animals, the SCNs control daily swings in blood pressure, body temperature, activity and alertness. They also control the release of melatonin at night. Cells in the retina that report light levels to the SCNs were also found recently. However, four key clock genes are active in every tissue of flies, mice and humans, not just in the SCNs. And when rats that normally ate whenever they liked were fed once a day, a clock gene in their livers shifted its peak by 12 hours. Meanwhile, the same gene in the SCNs stayed in step with light. The excerpt breaks off as the next sentence begins: “While …”.

The story

The word “circadian” comes from the Latin circa diem, “about a day”. Even in constant darkness, human rhythms keep running on their own, which is called “free-running”. But each cycle then lasts slightly longer than 24 hours, like a wall clock that runs a little slow.

Every day, cues called zeitgebers, German for “time-givers”, reset the rhythms. The main cue is light, but food and temperature count too.

The SCN is about 20,000 neurons. It sits just above the optic chiasm, where the two optic nerves cross, and that gives it its name: “supra-chiasmatic” means “above the crossing”. In the 1970s, rats whose SCNs were destroyed lost their daily rhythms. Later, transplanted SCN tissue restored rhythms in hamsters, and so the SCN earned the label “master clock”.

At night, on the SCN’s signal, the pineal gland releases melatonin, the “darkness hormone”. Around 2000–02, scientists identified light-sensing cells in the retina that contain a pigment called melanopsin. These cells don’t form images. They just tell the SCN how bright it is, which is why bright screens at night can disturb sleep.

In 2017 the Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine went to Jeffrey Hall, Michael Rosbash and Michael Young. They showed how clock genes, such as one called period, work in fruit flies. A protein builds up and then switches off its own gene, in a loop that takes about 24 hours. Think of the float valve in a rooftop water tank, which shuts off the inflow when the tank is full and opens again when the level falls.

Twice a year, Americans change their clocks for daylight saving time, unlike India, which keeps one clock time all year. Their rule of thumb is “spring forward, fall back”: clocks go forward in spring and back in the fall, the American word for autumn. In Congress, the American parliament, proposals to end the switch are often argued in body-clock terms. Melatonin is also sold over the counter in American pharmacies as a sleep aid.

Remember it like this: Your body’s rhythm is a wall clock that runs a little slow. Every day, light, the chief “time-giver”, sets it right again.

Words to know

Nuclei (in the brain)
Clusters of neuron cell bodies. This is not the nucleus of a cell or of an atom.
Hypothalamus
A small region at the base of the brain. It controls hunger, temperature, hormones and the body clock.
Synchronised
Kept in step.
Peak activity
The time of day when a gene is most active.

Reading tip: Note scope words (“in some animals”, “every tissue”). When the text gives a list, keep to its own items.

p.63Episodic memory and Clayton’s scrub-jay experimentmemory types · animal cognition · corvids · food caching

In short: Endel Tulving coined the term “episodic memory” for what he considered a uniquely human ability. It means recalling specific past events, travelling back into the past in one’s own mind. He set it apart from simply using information gained through experience. Later, Nicola Clayton and colleagues set criteria to test for it in animals. By their criteria, an episodic memory binds several parts of a single event together: “what”, “where” and “when”. Scrub jays hide food so that they can retrieve it when food is scarce. In Clayton’s experiment, crickets, which the birds prefer but which spoil quickly, were stored in one place, and peanuts in another. Once the food had been stored for a certain time, the birds switched their preference from crickets to peanuts, “showing that they retain information …”. The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

In psychology, memory comes in several kinds. Declarative, or explicit, memory is what you can consciously put into words. In 1972 Endel Tulving split it in two. Episodic memory holds personal events, such as “my tenth birthday”, while semantic memory holds facts, such as “Delhi is India’s capital”.

Other kinds of memory work differently. Non-declarative, or implicit, memory covers skills such as riding a bike, along with habits and conditioning. You may not be able to explain how you balance on a bike, yet you still can. Working memory, or short-term memory, is also separate from long-term memory.

Tulving was born in Estonia and worked at the University of Toronto. He tied episodic memory to “autonoetic”, or self-knowing, consciousness: the feeling of re-living an event. He also tied it to “mental time travel”, which includes imagining the future. Recalling your tenth birthday can feel like stepping back into that day for a moment.

In 1953 surgeons removed much of the hippocampus of Henry Molaison, known as patient H.M. Afterwards he could no longer form new episodic memories. Yet he could still learn new motor skills, meaning physical skills. This was proof that memory has separate systems.

Crows, ravens, jays and magpies belong to a family called the corvids, which are among the most intelligent birds. Some nutcrackers hide thousands of seeds and find them months later. Scrub jays even re-hide their food if another jay watched them hide it. In 1998 Clayton and Dickinson published their scrub-jay “what-where-when” experiment in the journal Nature.

Intro psychology is one of the courses American college students take most often. So “episodic” and “semantic” memory, and the case of H.M., are familiar to many American readers.

Remember it like this: Semantic memory knows that Delhi is India’s capital. Episodic memory takes you back to your tenth birthday, like a time machine.

Words to know

Recollect
To consciously remember.
Cache
To store food in a hidden place. As a noun, it means the hidden store itself.
Degrade
To spoil or decay.
Criteria
The standards a test checks. The singular is criterion.
Bound
Linked into a single memory.

Reading tip: For experiments, note what was manipulated, what was measured, and what conclusion is drawn. Map the design onto the criteria: which feature of the setup tests “what”, which tests “where”, and which tests “when”?

↑ Back to top

Topic 6 · Astronomy / Stars / Galaxies / Black Holes / Meteors / Asteroids 13 readings · pp.65–77

Astronomy readings are detective stories. Nobody can visit a galaxy, so astronomers work out everything from two kinds of clue: light and gravity. Learn the toolkit and the map of objects in the chapters below. Then every reading in this topic will follow one pattern: the evidence, a conclusion drawn from it and a rival explanation.

How astronomers know things: clues in the light

Picture a detective who can never visit the scene of the crime. Every clue reaches the detective as light, often after a very long journey. That is the astronomer’s position, and luckily light carries a lot of information.

The simplest clues are a star’s brightness and colour. Redder stars are cooler, and bluer stars are hotter. A blacksmith’s iron bar follows the same rule: it glows dull red at first, then yellow and white as it heats up. The hottest stars go one step further and look blue.

The richest clue is the spectrum, the band of rainbow colours you get when light is spread out. Dark and bright lines cross this band. Each chemical makes its own pattern of lines, so the lines work like fingerprints at a crime scene.

Movement leaves a clue too, called the Doppler shift. Light from something moving away is stretched and looks redder, which is called “redshift”. Light from something coming closer looks bluer. By measuring these shifts, astronomers find the speeds of stars, gas and galaxies (pp.68, 73).

You can hear the same effect on any busy road. An ambulance siren sounds higher as it comes towards you and lower as it moves away.

Distances in space are measured in light-years. A light-year is the distance light travels in one year, about 9.5 trillion km. Because light takes time to travel, looking far away also means looking back in time.

Remember it like this: Light is the astronomer’s star witness. Its colour gives the temperature, its lines give the chemicals and its shift gives the speed.

More clues: gravity, craters and computers

The second great clue is gravity, which lets astronomers weigh things they can never touch. The method comes from Newton’s law of gravity. If you know how fast something orbits and how big its orbit is, you can work out the mass inside that orbit.

This one trick weighs many very different things. It weighs pairs of stars, black holes, whole galaxies and even unseen dark matter (pp.65, 68, 73).

Mass also bends light. A heavy object bends the light passing close to it, a little like a glass lens. This is called gravitational lensing (p.76).

Solid surfaces keep a record of collisions. When asteroids and comets crash into them, they leave craters, and the biggest impacts leave wide basins (pp.74–75). These marks are like hail dents on a car roof, which show what fell long after the storm has passed.

Some changes are far too slow to watch, because they take millions of years. So astronomers run computer simulations, which show what a theory predicts over such long stretches of time (pp.69, 70, 72).

Remember it like this: Gravity is the astronomer’s weighing scale, and craters are a planet’s hail dents. A computer simulation fast-forwards millions of years.

The object map, part 1: from dust to planets

Start with the smallest objects. A grain of rock or dust drifting in space is a meteoroid. When one enters Earth’s air, it makes a bright streak across the sky, a “shooting star”, and that streak is a meteor. Any piece that lands on the ground is a meteorite.

Comets are “dirty snowballs”, and they shed streams of dust as they travel (p.69). Asteroids are rocky leftovers, and most of them circle the Sun between Mars and Jupiter (p.72).

Next come the planets. The inner planets, from Mercury to Mars, are rocky. The outer planets, from Jupiter to Neptune, are giants. When the Sun was young, ice could freeze only beyond a boundary called the “frost line”.

Pluto was long counted as a planet. In 2006, however, it was reclassified as a “dwarf planet”.

Remember it like this: A meteoroid drifts in space, a meteor is the streak of light in the sky, and a meteorite is the piece that lands on the ground.

The object map, part 2: stars, clusters and galaxies

Stars are born in clouds of gas called nebulae. They shine by fusion, which joins hydrogen together to make helium. How a star ends depends on how big it is. Small stars end as white dwarfs, while big ones explode as supernovae and leave behind neutron stars or black holes (p.73).

Stars are often found together, and many come in pairs (p.65). Bigger groups are called clusters. Open clusters are young and loose, while globular clusters are ancient, dense balls of stars (p.70).

Stars also gather in huge systems called galaxies. Spiral galaxies, such as our Milky Way and Andromeda, have curving arms. Elliptical galaxies, such as M87, are round or oval balls of stars. Dwarf or irregular galaxies, such as the Magellanic Clouds, are small or have no clear shape.

The Milky Way has three parts. At its centre sits a bulge. Around the bulge spreads a flat disc with spiral arms, and the Sun lies in this disc, about 26,000 light-years from the centre. A round halo of old stars and dark matter surrounds the whole galaxy.

Remember it like this: Picture the Milky Way as a fried egg seen from the side. The yolk is the bulge, the white is the flat disc, and the halo is a round cloud around the whole egg.

A century of discoveries, to date the readings (1915–2021)

This timeline helps you work out when a reading was written, and so what its writer could have known. In 1915 Einstein published general relativity, his new theory of gravity. In 1929 Hubble showed that the universe is expanding. In 1933 Zwicky proposed the idea of “dark matter”.

In 1965 scientists detected the afterglow of the Big Bang, the birth of the universe. The 1970s brought Vera Rubin’s results on how galaxies rotate (p.68). In 1990 the Hubble Space Telescope was launched. Through the 1990s astronomers ran microlensing searches, which use gravitational lensing (p.76).

Then, in 1998, astronomers found that the expansion is accelerating, driven by “dark energy”. This discovery won a Nobel in 2011. In 2015 gravitational waves, ripples in space and time, were detected. In 2019 came the first image of a black hole (M87, p.73), and in 2021 the James Webb Space Telescope was launched.

Today’s recipe for the universe is about 5 per cent ordinary matter, 27 per cent dark matter and 68 per cent dark energy. Articles written before 1998 ask whether the expansion will “stop”. Read them on their own terms.

Remember it like this: Before 1998, astronomers asked whether the cosmic car would brake to a stop. In 1998 they found it pressing the accelerator instead.

p.65“Weighing” double starsNewton’s gravity · mass vs weight · binary stars · free fall

In short: In a spaceship in free fall, where things are weightless, you can’t find an object’s mass by balancing it against a known weight. But you can put the object on a spring scale and whirl the scale in a circle on a string. The tension that the scale reads depends on the speed of revolution and on the object’s mass. So from the tension and the speed, you can work out the mass. Astronomers “weigh” double-star systems in the same way. How fast the two stars circle each other depends on the gravitational force holding them together. That force plays the role of the string’s tension. By Newton’s law of gravitation, it is proportional to the stars’ combined mass.

The story

Mass is the amount of matter in an object, measured in kilograms. Weight is the pull of gravity on that mass. A shopkeeper’s two-pan balance compares weights, since gravity pulls down on both pans. So when everything floats, the balance is useless.

Astronauts look “weightless”, but the word is misleading. At the height of the space station, gravity is still about 90 per cent as strong as on the ground. Astronauts float because they and their ship are in free fall, falling around Earth together. You get a brief taste of this feeling when a lift suddenly drops or a fairground ride plunges.

Anything moving in a circle needs an inward pull, called centripetal force. For a ball whirled on a string, the pull is the string’s tension. For stars or planets in orbit, the pull is gravity. More mass or more speed needs a stronger pull.

Binary stars, pairs of stars that orbit each other, are very common, and many Sun-like stars have a companion. These pairs are the main way astronomers measure the masses of stars directly. Newton’s version of Kepler’s third law turns the size of the orbit and the time for one orbit into the pair’s total mass.

To split that total between the two stars, astronomers find each star’s distance from their shared balance point, the centre of mass. It works like a see-saw, where a heavier child sits nearer the pivot. The same logic gives the Sun’s mass from Earth’s orbit, and Jupiter’s mass from its moons.

Whirling a ball on a string is a standard physics demonstration in American high schools. Every American student has also seen videos from NASA, the American space agency, of astronauts floating inside their ship.

Remember it like this: Mass is how much stuff an object has, and weight is how hard gravity pulls on it. In orbit, the weight seems to vanish, but the mass stays the same.

Words to know

Free fall
Moving under gravity alone, so that things feel weightless.
Tension
The pulling force carried by a string or rope.
Revolution vs rotation
Revolution is going around something, as in an orbit. Rotation is spinning on one’s own axis.
Analogous
Similar in structure or role.

Reading tip: Build your own two-column mapping (spaceship procedure ↔ double-star procedure) as you read. The whole argument rests on that mapping.

p.66The Mars meteorite ALH84001Mars meteorite · organic molecules · contamination

In short: In 1994 David McKay’s team began studying the meteorite ALH84001. It was found in Antarctica in 1984 and is generally agreed to come from Mars. In 1996 the team announced compelling evidence that life once existed on Mars. This included organic molecules called PAHs, the first found in Martian rock. When microbes die, their organic matter often decays into PAHs. Sceptics note that the rock has been on Earth for 13,000 years, so the PAHs might be contamination from Earth. McKay’s team replied that the concentration of PAHs increases deeper inside the rock. That is the opposite of what contamination from outside would produce. The text continues the debate.

The story

Picture a rock being blasted off Mars. Roughly 17 million years ago, an asteroid struck Mars and threw the rock into space. It drifted through space for a long time and finally fell on Antarctica.

Its name is a code. ALlan Hills is the place in Antarctica where it was found, 84 stands for 1984, and 001 is the specimen number. Its chemistry matches a family of meteorites known to come from Mars. Some of them hold trapped gas identical to the Martian atmosphere measured by NASA’s Viking landers in 1976.

Why are so many meteorites found in Antarctica? Dark rocks stand out on white ice, and the cold preserves them. Moving ice also gathers them in certain spots, like a very slow conveyor belt.

In chemistry, “organic” means carbon-based. PAHs, short for polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons, are molecules built from several linked rings of carbon atoms.

In 1996 McKay’s team offered several lines of evidence. Besides the PAHs, there were carbonate globules, tiny magnetite crystals and worm-like microscopic shapes. “Today, rock 84001 speaks to us across all those billions of years and millions of miles,” President Clinton said on TV.

The announcement was huge news in America, and it boosted NASA funding for astrobiology, the search for life beyond Earth. Americans often quote Carl Sagan’s maxim “extraordinary claims require extraordinary evidence” about it. Since then, NASA rovers have kept searching. Perseverance, which landed in 2021, has found rocks with possible, but unconfirmed, signs of past microbial life, and it is collecting samples for future return to Earth.

Remember it like this: Read ALH84001 like a train ticket. ALlan Hills is the station, 84 is the year 1984, and 001 is the seat number.

Words to know

Meteorite
A space rock that reaches the ground.
Terrestrial
Of or from Earth.
Contamination
Unwanted material that gets in from outside.
Compelling
Convincing and forceful.

Reading tip: Track each objection and the specific reply to it, and keep the author’s own stance separate from the McKay team’s.

p.67Milankovitch: did Earth’s orbit cause the ice ages?ice ages · oxygen isotopes · Earth’s orbit · climate

In short: Early in the 20th century, Milankovitch proposed that the ice ages were caused by changes in Earth’s orbit around the Sun. For a long time the theory was considered untestable. The reason was that there was no precise chronology, or dated record, of the ice ages to match against the orbital changes. A recent discovery made such a chronology possible. The ratio of two oxygen isotopes, 16 and 18, in ocean sediments reveals how much land ice existed at a given time. Almost all oxygen in water is O-16, but a few molecules per thousand carry the heavier O-18. When an ice age begins, growing ice sheets keep evaporated ocean water from returning to the sea. The record shows an ice age roughly every 100,000 years. This establishes a strong link between the orbital changes and the periodicity, or regular repetition, of the ice ages. The author then adds a caution. Other factors, such as volcanic particulates or changes in the sunlight Earth receives, could also have affected climate. The theory’s advantage is that it is testable, since orbital changes can be calculated backwards with Newton’s laws. But a lack of information about the other factors doesn’t make them unimportant.

The story

Picture Milutin Milanković, a Serbian engineer and mathematician whose name is also spelled Milankovitch. For decades he calculated by hand how changes in Earth’s orbit alter the summer sunlight reaching the far north. Earth’s orbit and axis change in three slow cycles. The first is eccentricity, how stretched the orbit is, on a cycle of about 100,000 years.

The second is obliquity, the tilt of the axis, which moves between about 22 and 24.5 degrees on a cycle of about 41,000 years. The third is precession, the wobble of the axis, like a spinning top, on a cycle of about 23,000 years. The key idea is simple. If northern summers are too cool to melt the winter snow, ice sheets grow year after year.

The record of past ice comes from isotopes, forms of the same element with different weights. Water containing light O-16 evaporates more easily, so the snow that builds ice sheets is rich in O-16. During ice ages, the oceans are left relatively richer in O-18. Tiny shelled plankton called foraminifera record the ocean’s ratio in their shells, which pile up on the seafloor layer by layer like the pages of a diary.

In 1976 a landmark paper by Hays, Imbrie & Shackleton, “Variations in the Earth’s Orbit: Pacemaker of the Ice Ages”, found the predicted cycles in deep-sea cores. The last ice age peaked about 20,000 years ago. Ice covered Canada and the northern US, sea level was about 120 m lower, and a land bridge called Beringia joined Siberia to Alaska.

Big volcanic eruptions throw sunlight-blocking particulates, or fine particles, high into the atmosphere. Tambora in Indonesia erupted in 1815 and caused 1816’s “Year Without a Summer”. Pinatubo in the Philippines erupted in 1991 and cooled the globe by about 0.5 °C for a year or so.

Orbital cycles act over tens of thousands of years. So they can’t explain the rapid warming since 1900, which is driven by greenhouse gases. In America, climate change is a politically polarised subject, and “it’s just natural cycles” is a common sceptic argument. That is why many Americans have heard of orbital cycles in that debate.

Remember it like this: Remember the three cycles as stretch, tilt and wobble. The orbit stretches, the axis tilts, and the axis wobbles like a spinning top.

Words to know

Chronology
A dated sequence of events.
Periodicity
Regular repetition at fixed intervals.
Particulates
Fine particles in the air, such as ash or sulphate droplets.
Configuration
An arrangement, here of the planets’ positions.
Testable
Able to be checked against evidence. See “falsifiable” in Foundation 6.

Reading tip: When a final paragraph turns with “however … yet …”, it often carries the author’s own attitude, so read it slowly.

p.68The “missing matter”: will the universe stop expanding?critical density · dark matter · galaxy rotation · Vera Rubin

In short: Evidence from the 1920s showed that the universe is expanding. A question followed: will it expand forever, or is there enough mass for gravity to halt the expansion? The critical density needed to “close” the universe equals about three hydrogen atoms per cubic metre. Luminous matter (galaxies) supplies only a fraction of that. So for the expansion to stop, invisible matter would have to outweigh luminous matter by a factor of about 70. The authors’ contribution to the search for this “missing matter” is to measure how fast galaxies rotate at different distances from their centres. The excerpt breaks off just as the text begins to say what “has been known for some time”.

The story

In 1929 Edwin Hubble, building on theory by Georges Lemaître, showed that farther galaxies are moving away faster. The universe is expanding, and this is the basis of the Big Bang theory. Think of raisin bread rising in the oven. As the dough swells, every raisin moves away from every other, and the farthest ones move apart fastest.

Before 1998, astronomers pictured three classic fates for the universe, like a ball thrown upward that may fall back or escape. In a closed universe, the density is above critical, so gravity halts the expansion and reverses it into a “Big Crunch”. In an open universe, the density is below critical, and the universe expands forever. In a flat universe, the density is exactly critical, so the expansion slows forever but never quite stops.

How can astronomers weigh things they cannot touch? By Newton’s laws, how fast something orbits depends on how much mass lies inside its orbit. In other words, speed reveals mass. That is why astronomers can “weigh” matter they cannot see, by measuring the speeds of matter they can see.

The idea of unseen dark matter goes back to Fritz Zwicky in 1933. In the 1970s Vera Rubin and Kent Ford made galaxy-rotation measurements of this kind. Rubin never won a Nobel, an omission that is often cited. The Vera C. Rubin Observatory in Chile is named after her.

Then came the 1998 twist. The expansion turned out to be accelerating, because of something called “dark energy”. Overall, the universe is very close to critical density, but mostly in the form of dark energy rather than matter.

In America, Rubin is a celebrated woman scientist, often cited in debates about women in science (see Topic 1). In 2019 Congress, the American parliament, named the observatory after her. It was the first US national observatory named for a woman.

Remember it like this: Think of the universe as a ball thrown upward. Falling back means closed, escaping means open, and having exactly the escape speed, slowing forever but never stopping, means flat.

Words to know

Critical density
The density that divides endless expansion from eventual collapse.
Luminous matter
Matter that gives off light, such as stars and glowing gas.
Rotational velocity
How fast stars and gas orbit a galaxy’s centre.
Constituents
Components, the parts that make something up.

Reading tip: Keep two quantities separate as you read: how fast matter rotates and how bright it is, at each distance from the centre. A quick sketch of each against distance helps, and any comparison should be checked against the exact lines.

p.69Meteor streams as hollow pipes: the Geminid modelcomets · meteor showers · computer model · orbits

In short: A meteor stream is dust ejected, or thrown out, from a parent comet at varying speeds. The grains share the comet’s orbit but slowly drift ahead or behind, until dust surrounds the whole orbit. Astronomers hypothesised that streams broaden over time as the gravity of planets perturbs, or disturbs, the grains. A computer model tracked simulated particles in the orbit of the Geminid stream for 5,000 years. The stream did broaden, as expected. But instead of growing denser toward the centre, as conventional theories predicted, it became a thick-walled hollow pipe. Earth would take just over a day to cross such a stream. So a shower should show two peaks, as Earth enters and leaves the wall. The gap between them should vary from year to year, because Earth needn’t pass through the exact centre. The text then asks whether the real Geminid shower shows these twin peaks.

The story

Comets are icy bodies from the outer solar system. In 1950 Fred Whipple called them “dirty snowballs”. Near the Sun, their ice turns to gas and frees dust, which forms the comet’s tails and, over many orbits, a stream of dust. Halley’s Comet, which comes back about every 76 years and is next due in 2061, is the most famous.

A meteor shower happens when Earth crosses such a stream, on the same dates each year. The meteors seem to fly out from one spot in the sky, like spokes from the hub of a wheel. Each shower is named after the constellation at that spot. The Perseids come in August, the Leonids in November and the Geminids in mid-December, one of the year’s richest showers.

The Geminids’ parent, identified in 1983, is 3200 Phaethon. It is an unusual rocky body that behaves like a burnt-out comet. Small gravitational tugs, mostly from Jupiter, slowly alter orbits. This is called perturbation.

Earth moves at about 30 km per second, or roughly 1.6 million miles a day. Americans use miles, and a mile is about 1.6 km.

To study such slow changes, astronomers run a simulation. A computer applies the laws of gravity to thousands of test particles over a long span of time.

In America, the news and NASA publicise each year’s showers. Watching the August Perseids is a summer camping tradition, so “meteor shower” is an everyday American phrase.

Remember it like this: A comet leaves dust along its path, like crumbs from a leaking bag. Each year Earth drives through the same trail on the same dates, and we see a meteor shower.

Words to know

Ejected
Thrown out.
Perturbed
Disturbed or pushed off a regular path.
Shroud
A covering that wraps around something.
Disintegrating
Breaking apart.

Reading tip: To work out what the model predicts, draw the pipe and Earth’s path through it, and reason from the geometry.

p.70Globular cluster ages & how the Milky Way formedgalaxy formation · globular clusters · Milky Way halo

In short: New age measurements of some globular clusters cast doubt on a long-held theory of how the Milky Way formed. The galaxy has about 125 globular clusters. Each is a compact group of tens of thousands to perhaps a million stars. Together they sit in a roughly round halo around the galaxy’s centre. Their stars are thought to have formed with the galaxy, so they may be considered relics of the original gas cloud, the galactic nebula. The conventional theory says that about 12–13 billion years ago a round cloud of gas collapsed under its own gravity. Within about 200 million years it became a disc surrounded by a halo. If so, all halo stars should be nearly the same age. But Michael Bolte found quite large differences in age. One cluster is 2 billion years older than most, and another is 2 billion years younger. A colleague says that Palomar 12 is 5 billion years younger. So astronomers are taking a fresh look at “renegade” theories. In the early 1970s Richard Larson argued that the halo formed over a billion years or more. In his view, hundreds of small gas clouds drifted, collided, lost orbital energy and collapsed into an elliptical system, densest at its centre. Computer modelling by a mathematician in the 1970s goes well with his “lumpy and turbulent” picture.

The story

From most of India, you can see Omega Centauri with the naked eye, low in the southern sky. It is the Milky Way’s biggest globular cluster: a dense, ancient ball of stars orbiting in the galaxy’s halo, the roughly round region around its disc. Such clusters are among the oldest objects in the galaxy. Open clusters, such as the Pleiades, are the opposite: young, loose and lying in the disc.

How can anyone tell a cluster’s age? All its stars were born together, but heavy stars burn out faster. So the heaviest stars that are still burning hydrogen act as a clock. The older the cluster, the lighter its heaviest survivors.

On how the galaxy formed, there are two schools. The top-down school says it formed in a rapid collapse, like a ball of dough flattening into a roti in one go. Eggen, Lynden-Bell and Sandage set out this view in 1962.

The bottom-up school says it was assembled from smaller pieces. Larson argued for this, and so did Searle and Zinn in 1978.

The name Palomar 12 comes from California’s Palomar Observatory. Its 200-inch telescope was the world’s largest from 1948 to the mid-1970s and became an icon of American astronomy.

Remember it like this: Heavy stars are sparklers and light stars are candles, all lit at the same moment. The sparklers die first, so seeing which ones still burn tells you how long ago the match was struck.

Words to know

Relic
Something that survives from an earlier time.
Halo
The roughly round region around a galaxy’s disc.
Nebula
A cloud of gas and dust.
Renegade
Going against the usual view, outside the mainstream.
Protogalaxy
A galaxy that is still forming.
Centrally condensed
Densest at the centre.

Reading tip: For each theory, note what it predicts about cluster ages, then check which prediction the new data fit.

p.71Low-surface-brightness galaxies & the missing baryonsgalaxies · baryons · Big Bang helium · selection bias

In short: Besides conventional galaxies, the universe contains very dim galaxies that went unnoticed until recently. There may be as many of these low-surface-brightness (LSB) galaxies as conventional ones. They have the same general shape as spiral galaxies and about the same number of stars. But they tend to be much larger, so they have far fewer stars in each unit of volume. They apparently take much longer to turn their primordial gas into stars, which means they evolve much more slowly. They may help solve a long-standing puzzle, the missing baryonic mass. Baryons, generally protons and neutrons, are the source of the light from stars and galaxies. So their number can be estimated from how luminous, or bright, galaxies are. The excerpt breaks off in the middle of a sentence: “However, the amount of helium in the universe, as measured by …”.

The story

Spread the same butter over a bigger slice of toast, and the layer gets thinner. Surface brightness, or brightness per patch of sky, works the same way. Spread the same stars over a bigger area, and each patch looks fainter. Many LSB galaxies are fainter than the natural glow of the night sky itself.

Sky surveys have a weakness: they find what is easiest to see. It is like the old joke about the drunk man who looks for his lost keys under a streetlight, because that is where the light is. This is called selection bias.

For a long time, galaxy catalogues favoured bright galaxies. The classic LSB example, Malin 1, was found in 1986. It is a spiral several times wider than the Milky Way.

Now a word that sounds harder than it is. Baryons are ordinary matter: the protons and neutrons inside atoms. Electrons are too light to matter here. “Non-baryonic” dark matter, made of unknown particles, is a separate issue.

Helium can act as a baryon counter. In the first minutes after the Big Bang, roughly a quarter of ordinary matter fused into helium. Traces of deuterium and lithium were made too. How much of each was made depends on the density of baryons, so measuring these amounts gives a count that does not rely on starlight.

Remember it like this: Low surface brightness is the same butter spread over a bigger slice of toast. Selection bias is hunting for lost keys only under the streetlight.

Words to know

Primordial
Existing from the very earliest times.
Luminosity
The total amount of light something gives out.
Diffuse
Spread out, not packed together.
Condense
To gather into a denser form, as gas does when it forms stars.

Reading tip: Comparisons hinge on more/fewer, larger/smaller, faster/slower, so keep a two-column list (conventional vs LSB) as you read.

p.72Are asteroids solid rocks or rubble piles?asteroids · rotation rates · escape velocity · collisions

In short: Collisions make asteroids spin faster or slower. Suppose all asteroids were monoliths, single solid rocks, hit by random collisions. Then their spin rates would form a bell-shaped curve, with “tails” of very fast and very slow spinners. If they are rubble piles, the fast tail would be missing. That is because a loose heap spinning faster than about once every few hours would fly apart. The exact limit depends on its bulk density. Researchers found that all but five of the observed asteroids obey a strict spin limit. The exceptions are all smaller than 200 metres, and there is a sharp cutoff above that size. The obvious conclusion is that asteroids larger than 200 metres are rubble piles. This agrees with computer models of collisions, which find a switch-over at the same diameter. A collision can blast a large asteroid to bits, but the bits usually move slower than their mutual escape velocity.

The story

About 66 million years ago, an asteroid roughly 10 km across ended the age of the dinosaurs. Asteroids are rocky leftovers from the time the planets formed. Most of them lie in the belt between Mars and Jupiter. They range in size from boulders up to Ceres, about 940 km across, which is now classed as a dwarf planet.

What is an asteroid like inside? A monolith is held together by the strength of solid rock. A rubble pile is a heap of boulders, gravel and dust, held together only by its own weak gravity. Spin a rubble pile too fast, and it flies apart.

A common pattern in data is the bell-shaped, or normal, distribution, in which values bunch around the average, with fewer and fewer towards the extremes. Think of the heights of adults in a big crowd. Most are close to the average, and very few are very tall or very short. Those thin ends of the curve are its “tails”.

Another key idea is escape velocity, the lowest speed needed to break free of a body’s gravity. For Earth it is 11.2 km per second. For an asteroid 1 km wide, it is well under 1 metre per second, slower than walking. Pieces moving slower than this cannot get away from each other.

Spacecraft have now visited some asteroids. Japan’s Hayabusa photographed Itokawa, a rubble pile, in 2005, and Hayabusa2 collected samples from Ryugu in 2018–19. NASA’s OSIRIS-REx found Bennu’s surface so loose that the craft’s sampling arm sank in, as if into a ball pit. It brought a sample back to Earth in 2023.

All this matters for planetary defence: in 2022 NASA’s DART spacecraft deliberately rammed a small asteroid, Dimorphos, and changed its orbit. Whether a threatening asteroid is solid or rubble changes how you would push it off course. Hollywood films made the threat of a strike from space familiar to Americans: Armageddon and Deep Impact in 1998, and Don’t Look Up in 2021. DART itself was widely covered news across the US.

Remember it like this: A monolith is a solid brick. A rubble pile is a loose boondi laddoo that barely holds together: spin it too fast and the pieces fly off.

Words to know

Monolith
One single large block of stone.
Aggregate
A mass made up of many loose pieces.
Bulk density
The average density of the whole body, counting the gaps between pieces.
Cutoff
A sharp limit.

Reading tip: For each hypothesis, note what it predicts about rotation rates, then check which prediction the data fit. To see what could overturn the conclusion, look for an observation the conclusion rules out.

p.73Black holes in M87 and NGC 4258: proof by densityblack holes · inference from gravity · skeptics’ alternative · galaxy cores

In short: Astronomers theorise that a black hole forms when a massive object shrinks catastrophically under its own gravity. This leaves a gravitational field so strong that nothing escapes. Black holes are invisible, so their existence must be inferred from their gravitational pull on visible bodies nearby. In the galaxy M87, gas clouds whirl unusually fast around the centre. Most astronomers think the large concentration of mass at the centre is a black hole. A few sceptics argue that it could instead be a cluster of a billion or so dim stars. The same star-cluster idea might have been applied to the galaxy NGC 4258. But it was severely undermined when astronomers measured the speed of a ring of dust and gas spinning close to the centre. From that speed they calculated an extraordinarily high density for the core, more than 40 times other estimates. The text goes on to explain why this undermines the star-cluster idea.

The story

In 1915 Einstein published general relativity, his theory of gravity, and it predicted black holes. In 1916 Karl Schwarzschild worked out the first black-hole solution to its equations. The name “black hole” came much later, when John Wheeler made it popular in 1967. Around every black hole lies its event horizon, the point of no return: once past it, nothing can pass back out, not even light.

Black holes come in two main kinds. Stellar black holes have a few to tens of times the Sun’s mass, and they are left behind when giant stars collapse. Supermassive black holes have millions to billions of times the Sun’s mass, and they sit at the centres of most large galaxies. Astronomers have a simple weighing trick (p.65): the speed and distance of gas in orbit give the mass inside the orbit. Divide that mass by the volume it takes up, and you get a density.

M87 is a giant elliptical galaxy about 55 million light-years away. In April 2019 the Event Horizon Telescope, a planet-wide network of radio dishes, released the first-ever image of a black hole. It showed M87’s black hole, about 6.5 billion times the Sun’s mass, as a dark shadow inside a glowing ring. In 2022 the same network imaged our own galaxy’s black hole, Sagittarius A*, at about 4 million Suns.

NGC 4258 is a spiral galaxy about 24 million light-years away. In 1995 radio astronomers tracked natural microwave “lasers”, called masers, in a thin disc orbiting its centre. They revealed tens of millions of Suns’ worth of mass packed within a fraction of a light-year. It became one of the strongest early cases for a supermassive black hole.

The odd names come from old catalogues. “M” numbers come from the list of Charles Messier, an 18th-century French comet-hunter. He listed fuzzy objects that people should not confuse with comets. “NGC” stands for the New General Catalogue of 1888.

The 2020 Nobel Prize in Physics went to Roger Penrose for black-hole theory. It also went to Reinhard Genzel and Andrea Ghez for the supermassive object at our galaxy’s centre. In the US, the 2019 image was front-page news. A photo of Katie Bouman, a young American computer scientist on the team, reacting to it went viral. And the film Interstellar, from 2014, had already made black holes part of pop culture.

Remember it like this: An event horizon is the edge of a waterfall: once a boat goes over, no amount of rowing brings it back. Past a black hole’s edge, nothing climbs back out, not even light.

Words to know

Catastrophically
Suddenly and drastically, like a disaster.
Infer
To work something out from evidence, without seeing it directly.
Undermine
To weaken the base or support of something.
Density
How much mass is packed into a given space: mass per unit volume.

Reading tip: Separate the premises the two sides share from the conclusions they dispute.

p.74Moon basins with “wrong” gravity: Clementine datalunar geology · gravity anomalies · impact basins · 1994 mission

In short: This article dates from 1996. When a large body hits a planet or moon, it throws out material. This leaves a hole and a local mass deficit, a shortfall of mass. The deficit shows up as a gravity anomaly: gravity there is slightly lower than in the areas around it. So the Moon’s large multi-ring impact basins, which are essentially huge holes, should all show negative gravity anomalies. Yet 1994 data from the Clementine spacecraft show that many of them have no unusually low gravity. Some even have unusually high gravity. Scientists speculate about what happened early in the Moon’s history, when large impactors blasted out millions of cubic kilometres of crust. In their view, denser mantle material rose up beneath the holes almost immediately, making up for the lost mass. The text then works out the consequences of this idea.

The story

About 4.5 billion years ago, the Moon probably formed from debris after a Mars-sized body hit the early Earth. Like the Earth, it is built in layers. It has a light crust, a denser mantle underneath and a small core.

The crust “floats” on the denser mantle, which flows very slowly, rather like ice floating on water. Take weight off the top, and the material below rises to make up for it. This balance is called isostasy. The same principle gives mountains deep “roots”.

Look at the full Moon and you will see dark patches called “seas”, or maria, though they hold no water. They are plains of lava that later flooded many of the Moon’s impact basins. Impact basins are the biggest craters, hundreds of kilometres across, and many are ringed by mountain chains, one circle inside another. That is why they are called “multi-ring” basins.

Gravity can be measured from orbit. Extra or missing mass below a spot slightly speeds up or slows down a spacecraft passing overhead, and tracking its radio signal reveals the change. In 1968 NASA’s Lunar Orbiter craft were unexpectedly tugged over certain basins. These spots were named mascons, short for “mass concentrations”, and they were a navigation headache for the Apollo landings.

Those Apollo landings, in 1969–72, are still a point of national pride in America. The next US Moon mission was Clementine, in 1994, a joint mission of NASA and the Pentagon, America’s defence headquarters. It mapped the Moon’s surface and gravity, and it found hints of ice in polar craters that are always in shadow.

Later, in 2011–12, NASA’s twin GRAIL probes made the most detailed gravity map of the Moon. Today NASA’s Artemis programme aims to send astronauts back to the Moon, partly for that polar ice.

Remember it like this: Mascon is short for “mass concentration”: a hidden lump of extra mass that tugs on passing spacecraft. And the Moon’s dark “seas” hold no water: they are lava plains.

Words to know

Impactor
A body that hits another body.
Deficit
A shortfall: less than there should be.
Anomaly (anomalous)
Something different from what is expected. “Anomalous” means unexpected or unusual.
Mantle
The dense rocky layer under the crust.
Compensate
To make up for something, or balance it out.

Reading tip: To apply the scientists’ explanation to another case, run the stated mechanism step by step and stay within what the text says.

p.75Jupiter’s four largest moonsGalilean moons · composition gradient · geological activity · craters
In short: Io and Europa are the inner two of Jupiter’s four largest moons. They are about the size of Earth’s moon and are made mostly or entirely of rock and metal. Ganymede and Callisto are larger and roughly half ice. So the four moons are somewhat like the solar system’s planets. There, the rocky inner planets differ from the much larger outer ones, which are rich in gas and ice. But Jupiter’s moons are more “systematic”: many of their properties change gradually with distance from Jupiter.
  • Ice: Io has no ice. Europa has a shell of ice on its surface. Ganymede and Callisto are rich in ice, and Callisto, the outermost, is the iciest.
  • Geological activity: Io is extremely active and Europa modestly so. Ganymede had bouts of activity in its past, and Callisto shows none.
  • Cratering: Callisto is very heavily cratered. Ganymede, like Earth’s moon, is heavily cratered in parts. Europa is very lightly cratered. The text then completes the pattern.

The story

In January 1610, Galileo turned one of the first telescopes on Jupiter and discovered these four moons. That is why they are called the Galilean moons. Moons circling something other than Earth supported Copernicus’s Sun-centred system, in which the Earth goes round the Sun. This was part of the clash behind Galileo’s trial in 1633 (see Topic 8.2).

Io is the most volcanically active body in the solar system, with about 400 active volcanoes. Voyager 1 first saw its eruptions in 1979. Its heat comes from tidal flexing: Jupiter’s gravity kneads Io, squeezing and stretching it. A rubber ball warms up in the same way when you squeeze it again and again. Regular tugs from Europa and Ganymede keep Io’s orbit slightly oval.

Europa has a cracked shell of ice over a global ocean of liquid water. That makes it one of the best places to look for life beyond Earth. NASA’s Europa Clipper launched in 2024 and is due to arrive in 2030.

Ganymede is the largest moon in the solar system, bigger even than the planet Mercury, and it has its own magnetic field. Europe’s space agency, ESA, launched its JUICE probe in 2023, and it will orbit Ganymede in the 2030s. Callisto has one of the most heavily cratered surfaces in the solar system.

Why are the Sun’s inner planets rocky? Near the young Sun it was too hot for ice to freeze, so only rock and metal condensed into solids. Beyond a boundary called the frost line, ice was plentiful.

Pictures from NASA missions fill American textbooks: Voyager in 1979, then the Galileo spacecraft from 1995 to 2003, then Juno from 2016. They show Io’s volcanoes and Europa’s ice.

Remember it like this: From Jupiter outwards the order is Io, Europa, Ganymede, Callisto: “I Eat Green Carrots”. Galileo found all four in 1610, so they are the Galilean moons.

Words to know

Analogous
Similar in structure or pattern, so the two can be compared.
Systematic
Following a regular, orderly pattern.
Gradient
A gradual change across a distance.
Geologically active
Still changing from the inside, through things such as volcanoes and quakes.

Reading tip: Make a four-column table (Io → Europa → Ganymede → Callisto) with one row for each property the text lists. Most details then become easy look-ups.

p.76Hunting MACHOs with microlensingdark matter · gravitational lensing · variable stars · test criterion

In short: In the 1980s the astronomer Bohdan Paczyński proposed a way to test whether the Milky Way’s enormous dark halo is made of MACHOs (massive compact halo objects). These are bodies too dim to see. If the halo is made of them, a MACHO would now and then drift in front of a star in the Large Magellanic Cloud. This is a bright galaxy near the Milky Way. The MACHO’s gravity would bend together the star’s light rays, which would otherwise spread apart. So the star would briefly look brighter from Earth. This is microlensing. Some astronomers objected that many stars change in brightness on their own, and such brightening could be mistaken for microlensing. But when a star that is variable by its own nature brightens, its different colours are affected differently. Microlensing, by contrast, affects all of a star’s colours equally. The text illustrates this with an example.

The story

Picture an eclipse of the Sun in 1919. An expedition led by Arthur Eddington measured starlight bending as it passed the Sun, and the result made Einstein world-famous. In Einstein’s general relativity, mass curves space, so light passing a massive object is bent, as if by a lens. This is gravitational lensing.

Huge lenses, such as clusters of galaxies, bend light into arcs and multiple images. A small lens, such as a MACHO, makes a star behind it brighten for a while. A MACHO could be a brown dwarf, a “failed star” too small to fuse hydrogen. It could also be a faint dwarf star, a neutron star or a black hole.

Colours matter here. A glass prism splits white light into a rainbow, because it bends each colour by a different amount. Gravity, by contrast, is achromatic: it bends every colour by the same amount. A pulsating variable star is different: it changes temperature as it brightens, and hotter means bluer, so its colours change unequally.

Stars that change in brightness on their own are called variable stars. In 1912 Henrietta Leavitt found that the pulse rate of one kind, the Cepheid variables, reveals their true brightness. She was one of Harvard Observatory’s women “computers”, people who did calculations by hand, so her story also links to Topic 1. Her discovery turned Cepheid variables into cosmic yardsticks, which Hubble later used.

The Large Magellanic Cloud is a small satellite galaxy, a companion of our own, about 160,000 light-years away. It is visible to the naked eye from the Southern Hemisphere and is named after Magellan’s voyage. Looking at it means looking through our galaxy’s halo at millions of stars in the background. In the 1990s several surveys went hunting for MACHOs: the US–Australian MACHO Project, France’s EROS and Poland’s OGLE.

The surveys found some events, but too few for MACHOs to make up most of the halo. Most dark matter is now thought to be exotic matter that is not made of atoms. Microlensing later became a way to discover planets. The name “MACHO” is a pun on American slang. The rival dark-matter candidates are called WIMPs, weakly interacting massive particles, so the debate is “macho”, the tough guy, against “wimp”, the weakling.

Remember it like this: Picture a wrestling match over dark matter: in one corner stands MACHO, the tough guy, made of big dim bodies. In the other stands WIMP, the weakling, made of unseen particles.

Words to know

Intrinsically
By its own nature, from within.
Diverge
To spread apart.
Magnify
To make bigger or brighter.
Halo
The huge, roughly round outer region of a galaxy.

Reading tip: The strongest evidence is an observation that only one explanation predicts.

p.77Quantum tunnelling: faster than light?quantum mechanics · relativity · Wigner & Eisenbud · tunnelling time

In short: Most attempts to send particles faster than light involve quantum tunnelling. In tunnelling, particles pass through barriers that seem impossible to get through. A ball thrown at a wall bounces back, but subatomic particles do the equivalent of going straight through. Quantum theory gives a small but real chance, or probability, that a particle will tunnel through a barrier. That probability falls exponentially as the barrier gets thicker. Tunnelling’s extreme speed was noted as early as 1932. But only in 1955 did Wigner and Eisenbud propose that tunnelling particles sometimes travel faster than light. Their grounds were calculations suggesting that tunnelling time grows with barrier thickness only up to a maximum. After that, the calculations suggested, the time stays the same however thick the barrier gets. The excerpt breaks off in the middle of the argument, so work out for yourself what that calculation implies. For help, see the school formula “speed equals distance divided by time” in the story below.

The story

In 1905 Einstein set a speed limit in his theory of special relativity. No object, signal or piece of information can travel faster than light in a vacuum, about 300,000 km per second. If signals could, an effect could come before its cause. So physicists treat superluminal, or faster-than-light, claims with great caution.

In the 1920s physicists such as Heisenberg, Schrödinger and Bohr built quantum mechanics, the physics of the very small. At the scale of atoms, particles behave like waves of probability. When such a wave hits a barrier, it does not stop dead but fades inside it. If the barrier is thin enough, a little of the wave leaks out the other side, and that leak is the chance of tunnelling.

That chance “declines exponentially” as the barrier gets thicker: each extra slice of barrier multiplies it by the same small fraction. Picture a row of gates, each letting only one person in ten through. After three gates, only one person in a thousand has got through. In the same way, a slightly thicker barrier makes tunnelling vastly rarer, and that is why balls never pass through walls.

Tunnelling is real and everyday: in 1928 George Gamow showed that it explains a kind of radioactivity called alpha decay. It also lets protons in the Sun fuse even though they repel each other, and it makes flash memory in USB drives and SSDs work. The scanning tunnelling microscope, whose invention won a Nobel Prize in 1986, depends on it too. In the 1980s John Clarke, Michel Devoret and John Martinis showed that a whole electric circuit, big enough to hold in your hand, can tunnel. This large-scale, or “macroscopic”, quantum tunnelling won them the 2025 Nobel Prize in Physics and is groundwork for today’s quantum computers.

How do physicists test a faster-than-light claim? They time particles, often photons (particles of light), as they cross a barrier, and compare that with the same trip without the barrier. Reading these timings is notoriously tricky, so treat any “faster than light” result as a claim, not a settled fact. Remember the school formula, “speed equals distance divided by time”, and keep track of which quantity is growing and which is fixed.

Americans have an everyday phrase for faster-than-light travel: “warp speed”, from Star Trek. The US government even named its 2020 COVID-vaccine drive “Operation Warp Speed”.

Remember it like this: Tunnelling is a particle walking through a wall like a ghost. A ball never manages it, and every extra slice of wall makes the trick far rarer.

Words to know

Impenetrable
Impossible to get through.
Albeit
Although, even though.
Exponentially
By the same factor at every step, so the change builds up very fast.
Superluminal
Faster than light.
Photon
A particle of light.

Reading tip: When the author mentions something, reread the sentences around it and name the job it does there. To see what happens if a step fails, rebuild the chain of reasoning and find the link that breaks.

↑ Back to top

Topic 7 · Biological Evolution / Animal & Human Evolution / Animal Behaviour / Fossils 22 readings · pp.79–100

Biology readings follow a familiar pattern. Usually there is a puzzle, such as a fossil, a behaviour or a sudden fall in an animal population. Then one or more explanations are offered and weighed against the evidence. You don’t need specialist biology. But a few core ideas will stop the jargon from slowing you down: natural selection, adaptation, what fossils can and cannot show, and how ecologists test explanations. The chapters below tell them as short stories.

Darwin’s big idea: natural selection

In 1859 Darwin published On the Origin of Species. Its central idea, natural selection, works in three steps. First, individuals of the same kind vary, so no two are quite alike. Second, some of these variants survive and reproduce better than others. Third, they pass their traits on, so over many generations those traits become common.

An adaptation is a trait that selection has shaped to do a particular job. Be careful with the word “fitness”. In biology it does not mean strength. It means reproductive success: how well an animal manages to breed and leave young.

Evolution also leaves two patterns worth knowing. Sometimes unrelated groups face similar pressures and end up with similar features. This is convergent evolution. Bats, birds and insects, for example, all have wings, but they are not close relatives. At other times one ancestor branches out into many different forms. This is adaptive radiation, and Darwin’s finches are an example: many kinds of finch from a single ancestor.

Biologists also have to sort living things into groups. They must decide what counts as a separate species (p.86). They also sort all of life into basic categories (p.89).

Remember it like this: Natural selection is a sieve shaken over many generations: the variants that breed best get through. In biology, fitness is about babies, not biceps.

Deep time: the history of Earth as one calendar year

The history of life is told in deep time, spans of millions of years. Scientists shorten “million years ago” to “Ma”, so 150 Ma means 150 million years ago. Earth itself is about 4.5 billion years old.

Animals with hard parts appear about 540–520 Ma, in a burst called the Cambrian explosion (p.84). Dinosaurs lived from about 230 to 66 Ma. Then an asteroid wiped them out, except for the birds, which most scientists now count as dinosaurs.

The human line split from the chimpanzee line about 6–7 Ma (p.83). Our own species, Homo sapiens, appeared only about 300,000 years ago.

Remember it like this: Squeeze the whole history of Earth into one calendar year. Animals with hard parts arrive in November, dinosaurs roam from mid-December until the last week, and our species turns up in the last hour of the last day.

Birds, dinosaurs and the gaps in the rocks

In 1861, just two years after Darwin’s book, a fossil turned up in Germany. It was Archaeopteryx, about 150 million years old, and it mixed bird-like and dinosaur-like parts. It had feathers and wings, but also teeth, clawed fingers and a bony tail.

Since 1996, many fossils of feathered dinosaurs have been found in Liaoning, in China. These finds have made one view mainstream: birds evolved from small, meat-eating dinosaurs called theropods (pp.79–80).

Fossils must be read with care, because the fossil record, the set of all fossils known so far, is patchy. Soft parts such as lungs, muscle and skin rarely turn into fossils. And a line of animals can exist long before its oldest known fossil. Put simply, absence of evidence is not evidence of absence.

Still, bones and teeth are real data. The fine structure inside a bone hints at how fast the animal grew (p.81). Wear and breakage on teeth hint at what an animal ate and how it behaved (pp.82–83).

Remember it like this: Archaeopteryx is a mosaic made of mixed tiles: bird-like feathers and wings set beside dinosaur-like teeth, clawed fingers and a bony tail.

Watching animals: how, why and how clever?

Ethology is the study of animal behaviour in natural settings. Its founders, Konrad Lorenz, Niko Tinbergen and Karl von Frisch, shared the Nobel Prize in 1973. Lorenz studied imprinting in goslings: a newly hatched gosling bonds with the first moving figure it sees and follows it. Von Frisch studied the honeybee’s “waggle dance”, which a bee uses to tell its hive-mates where food is.

Biologists ask two kinds of question about any behaviour. A proximate explanation says how it works: which genes, hormones or learning produce it. An ultimate explanation says why it evolved: what payoff it brought in survival or breeding. Biology readings often move between these two levels.

Scientists must also guard against anthropomorphism, reading human motives into animals. Their brake is Morgan’s Canon, a rule from 1894. It says: don’t credit an animal with a higher mental ability if a simpler one explains the behaviour.

Darwin added a second idea in 1871. Sexual selection favours traits that win mates, such as a peacock’s tail, even when those traits cost the animal in survival. Honest signals are ornaments too costly to fake. Zahavi named this idea the “handicap principle” in 1975.

Remember it like this: Proximate means “nearest”: the machinery inside, such as genes, hormones and learning. Ultimate means “final”: the payoff in survival or breeding that made the behaviour evolve.

Who eats whom: ecology

Ecology is the study of how living things affect one another. It maps food webs, the networks of who eats whom. It also studies predators, their prey and the rivals that compete with them.

A keystone species has an effect out of proportion to its numbers (p.85). The term comes from Paine, in 1969. Removing a top predator can set off a trophic cascade. “Trophic” means to do with feeding, and a cascade here is a chain reaction that runs down the feeding levels of a food web. Populations can also rise and crash in repeated cycles of boom and bust (p.96).

Ecology is also put to practical use, and this is called applied ecology. Its fields include disease ecology (p.90) and conservation (pp.91, 98). They also include biological pest control, which turns a pest’s natural enemies against it (p.97), and wildlife management (p.99).

Remember it like this: A keystone species is like hing in a pot of dal: you add only a pinch, yet it changes the taste of the whole dish.

A biology timeline, to date the readings (1859–2003)

These dates help you place each reading in time. The story opens with Darwin’s Origin in 1859, and Archaeopteryx was found two years later, in 1861. In 1866 Mendel set out his laws of heredity, the rules by which traits pass from parents to their young. In 1871 Darwin’s Descent of Man described sexual selection.

In 1924 the Taung Child was found, the first hominin fossil from Africa. A hominin is a member of the human line, after it split from the chimpanzees. In 1925 the Scopes “Monkey Trial”, a court case in Tennessee, was fought over the teaching of evolution (see Topic 8.2). In 1942 Mayr set out his definition of a species.

In 1953 scientists worked out the double helix, the twisted-ladder shape of DNA. A polio vaccine followed in 1955, and the book Silent Spring appeared in 1962. In 1969 Ostrom described Deinonychus, and Paine introduced the “keystone species”. In 1973 the US passed its Endangered Species Act, and ethology won its Nobel Prize.

In 1974 the fossil known as “Lucy” was found. In 1980 scientists proposed that an asteroid impact caused the dinosaurs’ extinction. The first feathered dinosaur was found in 1996. Finally, in 2003, the human genome, the complete set of our DNA, was sequenced, or read letter by letter.

Remember it like this: Darwin opens the story in 1859, and the human genome closes it in 2003. Indian independence falls neatly between Mayr’s species definition and the DNA double helix.

How to read these texts: Keep track of who holds which view. Often a “traditional view” is set against “newer research”. Note what evidence each side uses, and what the author finally concludes. Watch the evidence verbs closely. Consistent with means the evidence supports a claim but does not prove it. Rule out means an alternative has been eliminated. Cannot be supported or falsified means the claim can’t be tested yet.
p.79Origins of bird flight: the “trees-down” and “ground-up” hypothesesbird flight · Archaeopteryx · dinosaurs

In short: There are two traditional scenarios for how bird flight began. The arboreal, or “trees-down”, hypothesis says that bird ancestors climbed trees. They then glided down with the help of early, or incipient, feathers, since height is a good launch point. As the feathers grew larger, flapping flight evolved. The author admits that this “makes intuitive sense” but finds parts of it troubling. Archaeopteryx and its maniraptoran dinosaur cousins show no obvious adaptations for life in trees, such as feet fully adapted for perching.
