No convincing analysis shows how Archaeopteryx could both climb and fly with its forelimbs. And where its fossils are found, no plants grew taller than a few metres. Even an animal that can climb trees is not necessarily a glider. Many small animals, and even some goats and kangaroos, climb trees without gliding. The visible text breaks off while it is still criticising the arboreal view. The rival cursorial, or “ground-up”, hypothesis is the second scenario named at the start.

The story

Picture southern Germany in 1861, just two years after Darwin’s Origin. In the fine Solnhofen limestone of Bavaria, a fossil turns up. It is Archaeopteryx, “ancient wing”, about 150 million years old. It comes from the Late Jurassic, a time deep in the age of dinosaurs, and it lived in a hot, dry region of lagoons with scrubby plants.

Archaeopteryx is a mosaic, a mix of parts: bird-like feathers and wings, but dinosaur-like teeth, clawed fingers and a long bony tail. That made it the textbook “transitional fossil”. Its closest dinosaur cousins are the maniraptorans, or “hand-snatchers”. They belong to the theropods, the mostly meat-eating dinosaurs, and include the “raptors” Velociraptor and Deinonychus.

The two classic answers to how flight began take their names from Latin. Arboreal comes from arbor, “tree”, and cursorial from cursor, “runner”. In the ground-up idea, small running, leaping dinosaurs used their feathered arms for lift, balance or speed, while chasing prey or escaping. In time, they became airborne.

Feathers came before flight. Many feathered dinosaurs could not fly. So feathers probably served first for warmth, for showing off, or for covering eggs in the nest, and were borrowed for flight only later. Biologists call a re-used trait like this an exaptation, rather like an old sari that ends up as a curtain.

Gliding and flapping flight work in different ways. A glider, such as a flying squirrel or India’s own Draco “flying lizard”, trades height for distance. Powered flight needs a flight stroke, the flapping beat of the wings.

Americans grew up with the 1993 film Jurassic Park, whose “Velociraptors” were as big as a man and covered in scales. The real Velociraptor was about the size of a turkey and had feathers. Its arm bone even shows quill knobs, the bumps where feathers were anchored. Many US news stories about new feathered dinosaurs open with a nod to Jurassic Park.

Remember it like this: Arboreal comes from arbor, Latin for “tree”: flight from the trees down. Cursorial comes from cursor, “runner”, like the cursor that runs across your screen: flight from the ground up.

Words to know

Incipient
Just beginning, at an early stage.
Arboreal
Living in trees, or adapted to life in trees.
Cursorial
Adapted for running on the ground.
Forelimbs
The front limbs, such as arms or wings.
Synonymous with
The same as, or equal to.
Exaptation
A trait that evolved for one use and was later borrowed for another.

Reading tip: “Makes intuitive sense, but certain aspects are troubling” is a signal of attitude. To weigh evidence against a view, use only what the text itself offers as evidence, not what you already know about dinosaurs.

p.80Birds and theropod dinosaursbirds · theropods · Archaeopteryx · wishbone

In short: Many lines of evidence indicate that birds evolved from ground-dwelling theropod dinosaurs, but some scientists remain unconvinced. First, timing. Archaeopteryx lithographica, the oldest known bird, appears about 150 million years ago. But fossils of non-avian maniraptoran theropods, the closest relatives of birds, dated only to about 115 million years ago. The supposed ancestors seemed to come too late. But bones apparently from non-avian maniraptorans of Archaeopteryx’s age have now been found. In any case, failing to find fossils of a predicted kind does not rule out their existence in a deposit that has not yet been discovered. Second, clavicles. Birds have fused clavicles, which form the “wishbone”, while theropods were thought to have unfused ones. That objection was reasonable when only early theropod clavicles were known. But theropod clavicles that look just like Archaeopteryx’s wishbone have since been unearthed. Third, lungs. Skeptics argue that the complex lungs of birds could not have evolved from theropod lungs. The text calls this an assertion that “cannot be supported or falsified”, and the excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

The idea that birds are related to dinosaurs is older than you might think. In the 1860s T. H. Huxley, known as “Darwin’s bulldog”, pointed out similarities between birds and dinosaurs. Then in 1926 Gerhard Heilmann wrote an influential book that rejected the link, partly because theropods seemed to lack clavicles, or collarbones. In 1969 John Ostrom described Deinonychus, an agile, bird-like dinosaur, and revived the idea. Since 1996, dozens of feathered dinosaurs from China have persuaded most palaeontologists.

Birds and theropods share many features: hollow, air-filled bones, three main toes pointing forward, wishbones and a swivelling wrist. They also share a habit, nesting and brooding, which means sitting on eggs to keep them warm. In Mongolia in the 1990s, fossils of oviraptorids were found sitting on their nests in a brooding posture. The name holds an irony. Oviraptor means “egg thief”, because the first one, found in 1924, was wrongly thought to be stealing eggs.

The furcula, or wishbone, is two collarbones fused into a “V”. In flying birds it flexes like a spring during each wingbeat. Wishbones are now known in many theropods, including Tyrannosaurus and Velociraptor.

Fossils are rare. Most animals rot or are eaten by scavengers before they can be buried in sediment and mineralised, or turned to stone. So the fossil record is patchy. Archaeopteryx itself is known from only about a dozen specimens, all from Solnhofen in Bavaria, Germany.

Bird lungs work differently from ours. Our lungs fill and empty like a balloon, with air going in and out the same way. In birds, air sacs pump air one way through rigid lungs. This efficiency lets bar-headed geese fly over the Himalayas.

Falsifiability is an idea from the philosopher Karl Popper. A scientific claim must, in principle, be open to being shown false by evidence. For example, “this coin always lands heads” is falsifiable, because a single tails would show it false.

At Thanksgiving, American families dry the turkey’s wishbone, and two people pull it apart. Whoever gets the bigger piece “gets a wish”. American children see dinosaurs in museums such as the Smithsonian in Washington and Chicago’s Field Museum, home of “Sue”, a famous T. rex. Many US textbooks and museums now present birds as living dinosaurs.

Remember it like this: The wishbone is two fused collarbones, the bone American families pull apart at Thanksgiving. Bird lungs are a one-way street for air, which lets bar-headed geese fly over the Himalayas.

Words to know

Theropod
The group of mostly two-legged, mostly meat-eating dinosaurs, such as T. rex and Velociraptor. Most palaeontologists now think birds descend from them.
Non-avian
Not a bird. “Non-avian dinosaurs” means all the dinosaurs other than birds.
Clavicle
Collarbone. Fused clavicles form the furcula, or “wishbone”.
Falsify
To show, with evidence, that a claim is false.
Deposit
A layer of rock or sediment that holds fossils.
lithographica
Named after Solnhofen’s fine limestone, which was used for printing from stone, a method called lithography.

Reading tip: Ask what the author is doing in every paragraph, not just what topic it covers. For a quoted phrase, read the full sentence it sits in and the sentence before it.

p.81Dinosaur bone tissuedinosaurs · physiology · bone histology

In short: Scientists have long argued about whether dinosaurs were warm-blooded or cold-blooded. The warm-blooded camp points out that dinosaur bone is generally fibro-lamellar. This bone forms quickly, so its fibrils, or tiny filaments, are laid down haphazardly. Fast-growing warm-blooded animals, such as birds and mammals, tend to make such bone. Slow-growing, cold-blooded reptiles generally lay their fibrils parallel to each other. Dinosaur bone is also highly vascularized, or full of blood vessels, like the bone of birds and mammals. These features were first recognised in the 1930s. In the 1960s de Ricqlès recorded them in several dinosaur groups. In the 1970s Bakker cited them as evidence that dinosaurs were warm-blooded. De Ricqlès urged caution and argued for an intermediate type of dinosaur physiology, somewhere between the two. Yet a generation of palaeontologists came to see dinosaur bone as mammal-like. The text then moves on to later research, but the excerpt breaks off there.

The story

In 1842 the scientist Richard Owen coined the word “dinosaur”, which means “terrible lizard”. For the next century, people pictured dinosaurs as slow, sluggish giant lizards that dragged their tails along the ground.

From the late 1960s, that picture changed. In the Dinosaur Renaissance, Ostrom (see p.80) and his student Robert Bakker recast dinosaurs as active, bird-like animals. Bakker is an American palaeontologist, or fossil scientist. He is famously colourful and a champion of “hot-blooded” dinosaurs. His popular 1986 book, The Dinosaur Heresies, took the warm-blooded case to the public.

Warm or cold blood is a question of physiology, or how an animal’s body works. Birds and mammals are endotherms. They make their own body heat, keep a steady temperature, eat a lot and can grow fast. Most reptiles, amphibians and fish are ectotherms, which rely on outside heat, like a lizard basking in the sun. They need far less food and usually grow more slowly.

Think of an endotherm as a house with its own heater: always warm, but with a big fuel bill. An ectotherm is a house with no heater, warm only when the sun shines in. In fact, a “cold-blooded” lizard in the sun can be quite warm, so scientists prefer the words endotherm and ectotherm.

Palaeontologists study long-dead animals by comparing them with living ones. One tool is bone histology, the study of bone tissue under a microscope. Researchers cut paper-thin slices of fossil bone and examine them. The way the fibres are arranged, and how thickly the bone is packed with blood-vessel channels, show how fast it was laid down. Armand de Ricqlès is a French specialist in bone tissue.

Ordinary Americans watched the dinosaur image flip within one generation. Old films showed slow swamp monsters. Then came the fast, clever animals of Jurassic Park in 1993. The film’s scientific adviser was the Montana palaeontologist Jack Horner, and its hero, Dr Alan Grant, was partly modelled on him.

Remember it like this: Bone is like handwriting. Scribbled in a hurry, the strokes go every which way, like the fibrils of fibro-lamellar bone. Written slowly, the lines run neat and parallel.

Words to know

Physiology
How a living thing’s body works, such as how it uses energy, controls its temperature and grows.
Fibro-lamellar bone
Bone that forms fast, with its fibres laid in a haphazard, woven pattern.
Fibrils
Tiny fibres. Here, they are the collagen threads in bone.
Vascularized
Supplied with blood vessels.
Haphazardly
At random, without any order.
Intermediate
In between two types. Here it means neither fully warm-blooded nor fully cold-blooded.

Reading tip: With several named scientists, note each one’s stance and the date of each piece of work. To see how the author regards one of them, look for the author’s own evaluative words.

p.82Broken teeth at the La Brea tar pitsPleistocene · predators · fossils

In short: A recent study offers clues to predator–prey dynamics, meaning how hunters and hunted affected each other, in the late Pleistocene. Researchers compared broken teeth in present-day carnivores with broken teeth in carnivores preserved in the Rancho La Brea tar pits of Los Angeles. These lived 36,000–10,000 years ago. Breakage was strikingly more frequent in the extinct species. The researchers rejected three other explanations. One was demographic bias: older animals were not over-represented in the fossil samples. Another was preservational bias: two extinct species showed no breakage at all, so the fractures did not come from abrasion inside the pits. The last was local bias: data from other Pleistocene sites were similar to La Brea’s. They think the most plausible explanation is that extinct carnivores behaved differently from present-day ones. The text then goes on to explain this, but the excerpt breaks off there.

The story

Picture the middle of Los Angeles, a big city in California. Here, natural asphalt seeps up from oil deposits underground. It is the same kind of sticky black stuff that Indians call tar on their roads. In Spanish, brea means “tar” and la means “the”, so “the La Brea Tar Pits” literally means “the the tar tar pits”.

Animals got stuck in the sticky asphalt, which was often hidden under water or leaves. Their struggles drew predators and scavengers, and those got stuck too. So La Brea became a predator trap. Among its millions of fossils, meat-eaters, or carnivores, vastly outnumber plant-eaters.

The most common large mammal there is the dire wolf, with thousands of individuals. Next comes the sabre-toothed cat Smilodon, California’s state fossil. Diggers have also found American lions, short-faced bears, coyotes, Columbian mammoths and giant ground sloths.

The Pleistocene lasted from about 2.6 million to 11,700 years ago. It was the “Ice Age” epoch, a long era when ice sheets spread again and again and giant mammals, called megafauna, roamed. Most of North America’s megafauna disappeared around 13,000–10,000 years ago. Climate change and hunting by newly arrived humans are the leading suspects.

But a fossil sample is not a random sample of a living population. It can be skewed by the ages of the animals that died, a demographic bias. It can be skewed by what happened to the bones after death, a preservational bias studied in taphonomy. Or one unusual site can skew it, a local bias. A careful study rules these out before trusting a biological explanation.

Today the tar pits sit on Wilshire Boulevard’s “Museum Row”, next to the Los Angeles County Museum of Art. Life-size mammoth statues stand in the lake pit. Generations of Southern California schoolchildren have visited on field trips, and the digging is still going on.

Remember it like this: Think of La Brea as a giant glue-trap. One stuck animal became bait, its struggles drew in hungry predators, and they stuck fast too.

Words to know

Predator–prey dynamics
How hunters and the animals they hunt affect each other’s numbers and behaviour.
Demographic
About the make-up of a population, such as its ages and sexes.
Abrasion
Wearing or scraping away by rubbing.
Taphonomy
The study of what happens to remains between death and becoming fossils.
Plausible
Reasonable and believable.

Reading tip: Each “ruled out because …” sentence implies an unstated fact. Note which part of the reasoning each sentence serves.

p.83What did australopithecines eat? Tooth micro-wearhuman evolution · diet · teeth

In short: Tiny wear marks on teeth, seen under a microscope and called micro-wear, may reveal what the long-extinct australopithecines ate. Using micro-wear, Walker dismisses Jolly’s hypothesis that they ate hard seeds. He also disputes Szalay’s suggestion that their heavy tooth enamel was an adaptation to bone crunching, since both seed cracking and bone crunching leave distinctive micro-wear. Walker concludes that they were frugivores, or fruit eaters. His reason is that the micro-wear of East African australopithecine specimens cannot be told apart from that of chimpanzees and orangutans. These apes are commonly assumed to eat fruit. However, research on the diets of present-day primates suggests that micro-wear studies may be of limited use for telling which foods are actually eaten. For example, insect eating, which can cause distinct micro-wear… The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

Picture Africa roughly 4 to 2 million years ago. Upright-walking human relatives called australopithecines, or “southern apes”, lived there. They had chimp-sized brains and ape-like faces. Their big molars, or back teeth, had thick enamel, the hard outer coat of a tooth. Strictly speaking, they were a group of species, not one species.

Think of evolution as a family tree. About 6–7 million years ago, one branch forked: one side led to chimpanzees, the other to us. Hominins are humans and all the extinct relatives on our side of the fork, including the australopithecines. Our own group of closely related species, the genus Homo, appears roughly 2.8–2.3 million years ago.

In 1924 Raymond Dart recognised the first australopithecine, the Taung Child, in South Africa. “Lucy”, found in Ethiopia by Donald Johanson in 1974, is an A. afarensis about 3.2 million years old, named after a Beatles song. The Laetoli footprints in Tanzania, about 3.6 million years old, prove upright walking. In 1959 Mary Leakey found “Nutcracker Man”, a huge-jawed relative. Later chemical studies suggested it ate mostly grasses and grass-like sedges rather than nuts.

Under a microscope, chewing leaves scratches and pits on the enamel. Hard, brittle foods such as seeds, nuts and bone tend to leave pits. Tough foods such as leaves and grass stems leave long scratches. Researchers match the patterns on fossil teeth against living animals whose diet is known.

Three scholars are worth knowing. In 1970 Clifford Jolly put forward a “seed-eater” model, comparing early hominins to gelada baboons, which eat grass seeds in the Ethiopian highlands. Around 1980 Alan Walker pioneered micro-wear studies of hominin teeth. Frederick Szalay proposed a hunting-and-scavenging model of how hominins began.

In the US, “Lucy” is a household name, and her fossil toured American museums from 2007 to 2013. Yet teaching human evolution remains politically sensitive in some states. In 2005 a federal court case, Kitzmiller v. Dover, barred “intelligent design” from science classes in public schools, which in America are government-run schools. See Topic 8.2 for more on challenges to religion.

Remember it like this: Read a tooth like a kitchen chopping board. Small dents usually mean hard, brittle food such as seeds or nuts. Long scratches usually mean tough food such as leaves or grass stems.

Words to know

Micro-wear
Scratches and pits, too small to see without a microscope, that chewing leaves on teeth.
Enamel
The hard outer layer of a tooth.
Frugivore
A fruit eater. Compare a herbivore, which eats plants, an insectivore, which eats insects, and an omnivore, which eats a mixed diet.
Contemporary
Here it means “living today”. Elsewhere it can mean “living at the same time as”. This double meaning often trips readers up.
Utility
Usefulness.

Reading tip: Build a who-says-what map as you read. When two sides disagree, find the exact point of conflict, which is narrower than the general topic.

p.84Conodonts and the origin of the vertebrate skeletonfossils · vertebrates · defence vs predation

In short: Conodonts are spiky, phosphatic remains of tiny sea animals: bones and teeth made of calcium phosphate. The animals probably appeared about 520 million years ago. Conodonts were once among the most disputed fossils, because no one knew what animal they came from or what the parts were for. In 1981, fossils were found that preserved the soft-bodied animals themselves, which are also called conodonts. Reconstructions of their bodies are now relevant to how the vertebrate skeleton began. The traditional view was that the skeleton began as defence. On this view, vertebrates became predators only much later, when jaws evolved. The first, soft-bodied vertebrates would have been easy prey for meat-eating invertebrates. That would be especially so if they were sedentary suspension feeders, which stay in one place and filter food from the water. The rival view was that key vertebrate traits would not have evolved unless the first vertebrates were predators. Those traits are paired eyes, plus muscles and a skeleton built for an active life. On this view, teeth came before outer armour. The conodont evidence: a stiffening notochord, V-shaped muscle blocks and tail fins at the rear mark conodonts as among the most primitive vertebrates. They have no mineralised parts except the elements in the mouth. That makes them more primitive than armoured jawless fishes such as the ostracoderms. The conclusion is that hard parts first evolved in the mouth to make a better predator. So aggression, not protection, drove the origin of the vertebrate skeleton.

The story

Run a finger down your back and you can feel the bumps of your backbone. Animals with a backbone are called vertebrates: fish, amphibians, reptiles, birds and mammals. They belong to a wider group, the chordates, which all have a notochord at some stage of life. It is a stiff but bendy rod along the back, like a thin bamboo cane. Human embryos have one too, and remnants of it survive in the cores of our spinal discs.

Between about 540 and 520 million years ago, most animal groups first appear in the fossil record. This burst is called the Cambrian explosion, and its famous fossil sites include the Burgess Shale in Canada and Chengjiang in China. Arms races between predators, the hunters, and their prey are a major theme of this period. In an arms race, each side keeps answering the other: better weapons on one side, better armour on the other.

The first vertebrates had no jaws, and lampreys and hagfish are their living jawless descendants. The ostracoderms, or “shell-skins”, were extinct jawless fish covered in bony armour plates. They lived about 480–360 million years ago. Jaws evolved later still. In biology, “primitive” means close to the ancestral form, or branching off early on the family tree. It does not mean crude or inferior.

In 1856, scientists first described tiny, tooth-like fossils called conodont elements, most of them under a millimetre long. They are common and changed quickly over time, so geologists use them to date rock layers. It is like dating an old family photo by the mobile phone in someone’s hand. Their colour also changes with heat, which helps oil explorers judge how “cooked” a rock has been.

The animals themselves were eel-shaped, mostly a few centimetres long, with large eyes. The first whole-body fossils came from near Edinburgh, in Scotland.

Many educated Americans know the Cambrian story from Stephen Jay Gould’s 1989 bestseller Wonderful Life, about the strange early animals of the Burgess Shale.

Remember it like this: A notochord is a thin, bendy cane that every chordate has down its back at some stage of life, even you as an embryo. On life’s family tree, “primitive” means an early branch, not a bad one.

Words to know

Phosphatic
Made of calcium phosphate, the mineral in bones and teeth.
Invertebrate
An animal without a backbone, such as an insect, a worm or a squid.
Postulate
To suggest or assume something as a starting point for an argument.
Sedentary suspension feeder
An animal that stays in one place and filters bits of food from the water, like a clam.
Mineralized
Hardened with minerals, as bone, teeth and shell are.
Posterior
At the back end. Its opposite is anterior, at the front.

Reading tip: Track which anatomical feature supports which claim, and what each particular feature shows.

p.85Keystone speciesecology · keystone species · Pisaster

In short: The most abundant, dominant species in an ecosystem is often crucial to keeping it going. But a keystone species also plays a vital role. It is defined here as a species whose effects are much larger than you would predict from its appearance. Species interactions can be complex. So identifying a keystone by removing it and watching what changes is problematic. Certain traits might seem to define a keystone. The ochre starfish Pisaster ochraceus is often a keystone predator, because it eats and suppresses mussels. Without it, mussels can become dominant. But this kind of predation, on species that are dominant or could become dominant, happens both in systems that have keystone species and in systems that don’t. Moreover, Pisaster is clearly a keystone on wave-exposed rocky headlands. But in more sheltered habitats its effect is weak or non-existent. And at some sites, it is sand burial that wipes out the mussels. Keystone status appears to depend on context, whether of geography or of other factors … The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

Picture an old stone arch over a gateway. The single wedge-shaped stone at the very top is the keystone. Pull it out and the whole arch collapses.

In 1963, the ecologist Robert Paine of the University of Washington began one of ecology’s most famous experiments. His site was a stretch of rocky shore on the coast of Washington State, in the US north-west. There he kept removing Pisaster sea stars, a kind of starfish. Mussels took over the space, and the number of species in the plot dropped sharply. In 1969, Paine coined the term keystone species.

A keystone is not the same as a dominant species. A dominant species matters because there is so much of it, like the main tree species of a forest. A keystone matters far more than its numbers suggest. Ecology’s standard test is the removal experiment: take a species away and watch what changes. But the results can be indirect, slow to show and tied to one particular site.

Textbooks name other keystones too. Sea otters eat sea urchins, which would otherwise devour kelp forests, the underwater forests of giant seaweed. Beavers are “ecosystem engineers”, whose dams create wetlands. Fig trees feed tropical animals when little else is fruiting.

Wolves are another textbook case. In 1995, they were brought back to Yellowstone. They changed how elk behaved and how many elk there were, with knock-on effects on plants along the streams. Such a chain of effects down the food chain is called a trophic cascade, though how strong this one is remains debated.

Yellowstone, set up in 1872, was America’s first national park. The wolves’ return there is the country’s best-known ecology story, retold in a viral 2014 video, “How Wolves Change Rivers”. Sea otters, for their part, are beloved mascots of the California coast, for example at Monterey Bay Aquarium.

Remember it like this: A dominant species is the crowd: it matters because there is so much of it. A keystone is the single stone at the top of an arch: it matters far more than its numbers suggest.

Words to know

Keystone species
A species whose effect on its community is far bigger than its numbers would suggest.
Perpetuate
To keep something going.
Suppress
To hold down or keep in check.
Headland
A rocky point of land that sticks out into the sea and takes the full force of the waves.
Unambiguous
Clear, with only one possible meaning.

Reading tip: To judge what would support the argument, first restate the exact claim being supported. Support must bear on that claim, not just on the general topic.

p.86Species, interbreeding and hybridsbiology · reproduction · hybrids

In short: It is hard to decide whether a population is a distinct species, because there is no single accepted definition. The biological species concept uses reproductive compatibility. On this concept, a species is a group whose members can mate to produce fertile offspring, but cannot mate successfully with members of other groups. “Yet this idea can be too restrictive.” (1) Mating between groups labelled as different species, called hybridization, is quite common in nature, as in the canine, or dog, family. (2) Two populations may be able to interbreed even though they differ in traits unrelated to reproduction. Some biologists question whether such disparate groups should count as one species. (3) Investigators cannot always tell whether two groups living in different places are able to interbreed. The text then continues, but the excerpt breaks off there.

The story

In 1942, the German-American biologist Ernst Mayr formalised the biological species concept, the definition most school textbooks teach. One word in it matters a lot: fertile, meaning able to have young. A horse and a donkey can mate and produce a mule, a hybrid, or cross, of the two. But a mule is almost always sterile, so horses and donkeys remain separate species. Lion–tiger crosses, called “ligers”, exist only in captivity.

Real hybrids give biologists headaches. Wolves, coyotes, dogs and jackals can all produce fertile hybrids. In eastern North America, “coywolves” carry wolf, coyote and dog DNA, and whether the endangered red wolf is a separate species is disputed. In the Canadian Arctic, wild hybrids of polar bears and grizzly bears, nicknamed “pizzly” bears, have turned up.

Even humans and Neanderthals, our extinct cousins, interbred. Most people of non-African descent carry about 1–2% Neanderthal DNA. Svante Pääbo won the 2022 Nobel Prize for this genetic work.

Over the years, biologists have proposed more than 20 species concepts. It is a bit like defining “middle class” in India: everyone uses the term, but people draw the line in different ways. The morphological concept is based on body form, and it is the only option for fossils. The phylogenetic concept takes the smallest group with a shared ancestry, often judged by DNA. The ecological concept is based on a distinct way of life.

The lines matter in law too. The US Endangered Species Act of 1973 protects species, subspecies and “distinct population segments”. So where biologists draw the lines decides what the law protects. India has its own example. The Asiatic lions of Gir were long treated as a separate subspecies, but a 2017 revision grouped them with West and Central African lions.

In America, fights over the Endangered Species Act make national news. In 1978, in the Supreme Court case TVA v. Hill, a small fish called the snail darter halted a dam. In the 1990s, the northern spotted owl was pitted against logging. Today, coyotes live in almost every US city, including New York and Chicago.

Remember it like this: A horse and a donkey make a mule, and the line almost always ends there. The biological species concept asks not just “can they mate?” but “can their young have young?”

Words to know

Constitute
To make up, or to count as.
Reproductive compatibility
The ability to mate and have young together.
Fertile
Able to reproduce, or have young.
Hybridization
Mating between members of different species or varieties.
Disparate
Clearly different, or unlike each other.
Restrictive
Too narrow, so that it leaves out cases that should be included.

Reading tip: When the author mentions something, note which numbered point (“First … Second …”) it belongs to, and name the job it does in the argument.

p.87What counts as a “behavioural gene”?genetics · fruit flies · definitions

In short: Researchers who study how genes control animal behaviour face many uncertainties. Most behaviours are governed by more than one gene, and until recently geneticists had no method for finding the many genes involved. Even when a single gene is found to control a behaviour, researchers in different fields don’t necessarily agree that it is a “behavioural gene”. Neuroscientists study genes to understand the nervous system, which produces behaviour, so they define the term broadly. Ethologists, who specialise in animal behaviour, are interested in evolution, so they define it narrowly. For them, mutations in a behavioural gene must change a specific normal behaviour, not merely make the animal ill. That change then provides variation for natural selection to act on, possibly leading to a new species. An example comes from fruit flies. Mutations in the gene Shaker make flies shake violently under anaesthesia. Shaking is not healthy, so ethologists do not count Shaker as a behavioural gene. By contrast, they do count the gene period, or per, which controls the fruit fly’s … The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

Picture Thomas Hunt Morgan’s “fly room” at Columbia University in the 1910s. Ever since, the fruit fly, Drosophila melanogaster, has been the workhorse of genetics. It is cheap to keep, and it breeds in about ten days. Roughly three-quarters of known human disease genes have a matching gene in the fly.

A mutation is a change in a gene’s DNA. Fly genes are named after what goes wrong when they mutate, and the names are written in italics. A fly with a faulty dunce gene learns poorly, and a faulty fruitless gene leads to abnormal courtship. It is like naming a car part after the rattle the car makes when that part breaks.

Shaker was found in the late 1960s. In 1987 it turned out to carry the recipe for a potassium channel, a tiny pore that helps a nerve cell reset after it fires. It was the first such gene ever isolated. It opened up the study of channel defects behind some epilepsies and heart-rhythm disorders.

The gene period, or per, was first described in 1971 by Ronald Konopka and Seymour Benzer at Caltech. Benzer was the pioneer of fly behaviour genetics. The excerpt breaks off just as it reaches what per controls, so this guide does not fill in that gap.

Height is shaped by many genes of small effect, plus the environment. Most behaviours are shaped the same way, and such traits are called polygenic. Think of a cricket team’s total: many batters each add a few runs, and the pitch matters too. Modern genome-wide scans, which search all of an animal’s DNA, can now hunt for these many genes together.

Americans often see headlines announcing “the gene for” some behaviour, such as risk-taking or novelty-seeking. Scientists usually reply that genes shift the odds rather than dictate behaviour. The argument over what counts as a “behavioural gene” is part of that conversation.

Remember it like this: Fly genes are named after what goes wrong when they break. Break dunce and you get a dunce of a fly, one that learns poorly.

Words to know

Mutation
A change in a gene’s DNA.
Ethologist
A scientist who studies animal behaviour, usually from the point of view of evolution.
Neuroscientist
A scientist who studies the brain and nervous system.
Natural selection
The process by which inherited traits that help living things survive and reproduce become more common.
Induced
Brought about or caused.

Reading tip: To judge how a group would classify something, apply that group’s stated definition and criteria mechanically, even if you’d personally judge differently.

p.88Social learning of food preferencesanimal behaviour · social learning · food choice

In short: Social learning happens when contact with other animals, direct or indirect, helps an animal pick up a new behaviour. In the usual set-up, an experienced animal, the demonstrator, performs a behaviour. A naive animal, the observer, then shows it sooner or more fully than it otherwise would. One example is picking up preferences for new foods. Some experiments suggest that in mammals social learning helps animals identify beneficial foods, while in birds it helps them avoid toxic ones. Red-winged blackbirds watched others eat a coloured food, or food from a clearly marked container, and then fall ill. Afterwards, the watching birds avoided food linked with that colour or container. House sparrows ate less red food after watching others eat red food that had been made noxious. Studies on species other than birds have not produced similar results, so researchers speculate that social learning in birds … The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there. The full article goes on to experiments on domestic hens by Sherwin’s team.

The story

Imagine an animal facing a strange new food. Finding out by trial and error can be fatal, so watching others is a cheap shortcut. This is social learning, and it ranges from true imitation to simply being drawn to the places where others are feeding. Think of a new student in a college canteen who watches what the seniors order before choosing.

Some cases are famous. In 1920s Britain, blue tits learned to peck open the foil caps of milk bottles left on doorsteps, and the habit spread across the country. In 1953, on Koshima island in Japan, a young macaque started washing sweet potatoes in water, and her troop picked it up. Chimpanzee groups have their own tool “traditions”.

Food choice has a classic study of its own. In the 1980s, Bennett Galef showed that rats learn which foods are safe by smelling food on the breath of other rats.

Another key idea is taste aversion, the “Garcia effect”, studied in the 1950s–60s. One bad meal is enough: an animal that falls ill after eating a food avoids it from then on, even if the illness came hours later. Many people know this: after one bad night following a plate of prawns, they avoid prawns for a long time. That raises a question: can an animal learn such a dislike just by watching others, without getting sick itself?

Colour can carry a warning too. Many toxic animals are brightly coloured, and birds learn to avoid them. In the 1960s, Lincoln Brower showed that blue jays vomit after eating monarch butterflies, which carry poisons from the milkweed plant, and afterwards avoid them.

The red-winged blackbird is one of North America’s most common birds. The house sparrow was brought to New York from Europe in the early 1850s. It is the same gauraiya you see in Indian towns. An Indian conservation group started World Sparrow Day, 20 March, in 2010.

Remember it like this: Picture blue tits in 1920s Britain pecking open milk-bottle caps, a trick that spread across the country. Picking up a habit from others like this is social learning.

Words to know

Facilitate
To make easier.
Acquisition
Gaining something. Here, it means picking up a new behaviour.
Naive
Here, inexperienced. It is a technical term, not an insult.
Noxious
Harmful or unpleasant.
Avian
To do with birds.
Speculate
To form a theory without firm evidence.

Reading tip: For a specific study, return to the exact sentences on that study and read the wording carefully. A summary of the whole text must cover all of it, not just one study.

p.89Prokaryotes and eukaryotescell biology · classification of life

In short: It was once assumed that all living things fall into two basic groups, and that these groups are exhaustive: everything fits one or the other. Eukaryotes have large, complex cells with a well-formed nucleus and many organelles. They include the many-celled plants and animals, and many single-celled living things. The true bacteria are prokaryotes: simple cells that lack a nucleus. The difference was first defined by structures seen under a microscope. In time, it was carried down to the level of molecules. There, the two kinds of cell share many features. For instance, both turn genetic information into proteins using the same type of genetic code. But even where the molecular processes are the same, the details differ and are typical of each form. For example, the amino-acid sequences of various enzymes tend to be typically prokaryotic or typically eukaryotic. The text then continues, but the excerpt breaks off.

The story

Picture two cells side by side under a microscope. The first is a prokaryote, Greek for “before the kernel”. It is tiny, and its DNA floats loose inside it, usually as a single loop. It has no nucleus and no membrane-wrapped organelles, which are small, specialised structures inside a cell.

The second cell is a eukaryote, or “true kernel”. It is much larger, and its DNA is enclosed in a nucleus. Inside are organelles such as mitochondria, which supply energy, and, in plants, chloroplasts, which make food from sunlight by photosynthesis. Animals, plants, fungi such as yeast, and protists such as amoebas are all eukaryotes.

Mitochondria and chloroplasts have a surprising past. They were once free-living bacteria that an ancestral cell swallowed. This idea, endosymbiosis, was championed by Lynn Margulis from 1967, and it is now accepted.

Inside a cell, DNA is copied into RNA, and the RNA is “translated” into proteins. Proteins are chains built from 20 kinds of amino acids. The genetic code used for this translation is almost the same across all life, which is strong evidence of a common ancestor. Enzymes are proteins that speed up chemical reactions, and comparing their sequences across living things reveals how closely those living things are related.

From the 1960s on, biologists compared molecules more and more, not just structures seen under a microscope. Proteins, RNA and DNA helped them work out how living things are related. Indian school biology, in the NCERT books, is built around Whittaker’s five kingdoms from 1969. They are Monera, which are the prokaryotes, then Protista, Fungi, Plantae and Animalia.

In America, Yellowstone’s hot springs gave science Thermus aquaticus, a heat-loving bacterium found in the 1960s. Its heat-proof enzyme, “Taq”, powers PCR, the DNA-copying method behind COVID-19 tests. PCR was honoured with a Nobel in 1993.

Remember it like this: A prokaryote is a one-room hut with its DNA lying loose on the floor. A eukaryote is a house with many rooms, and its DNA has a room of its own, the nucleus.

Words to know

Exhaustive
Covering every possibility. With “two exhaustive categories”, everything must fit into one or the other.
Organelle
A small, specialised structure inside a cell.
Unicellular / multicellular
Made of one cell / made of many cells.
Subcellular
Smaller than a cell, or inside a cell.
Respective
Belonging to each one separately.

Reading tip: Note which features the text says are shared and which differ, and at what level (microscope or molecular) each comparison is made.

p.90Polio and the causes of epidemicsdisease ecology · public health · US history

In short: Genetic changes, or mutations, in bacteria and viruses can cause epidemics. But some epidemics come from microbes that have not changed genetically. By studying these, scientists discovered the importance of social and ecological factors. Polio, for example, emerged as an epidemic in the United States in the 20th century. By then, modern sanitation delayed people’s exposure to polio until their teenage or adult years, when infection produced paralysis. Earlier, infection had come in infancy, when it typically gave lifelong immunity without paralysis. So the hygiene that helped prevent typhoid epidemics indirectly fostered an epidemic of paralytic polio. The next sentence begins “Another …”. A second example follows, but the excerpt breaks off there.

The story

Picture a polio patient lying inside an “iron lung”, a tank-like machine that kept patients alive when their breathing muscles failed. Polio is caused by a virus that spreads mainly through dirty water, food and hands. Most infections cause mild symptoms or none at all. But in about 1 in 200, the virus destroys nerve cells in the spinal cord, causing paralysis.

Age mattered because babies carry protective antibodies, germ-fighting molecules, from their mothers. So infections in infancy were usually mild and left lasting immunity, or protection. The mother’s antibodies are like a borrowed umbrella that is soon handed back. Cleaner cities delayed the first infection until that umbrella was gone.

Typhoid is a different disease, caused by bacteria and spread through water and food dirtied by sewage. In the early 1900s, filtered, chlorinated water and sewer systems cut it sharply in US cities. A New York cook named Mary Mallon had no symptoms but infected dozens of people. Nicknamed “Typhoid Mary”, she was forcibly isolated for decades.

Scientists who study disease ecology see epidemics as the product of three things together: the microbe, the host it infects and the environment. The picture also includes any carriers, called “vectors”, and the way people live and use land.

In the United States, a major polio epidemic centred on New York in 1916, and cases peaked in 1952 at about 58,000. In 1955, Jonas Salk’s injected vaccine was declared safe and effective after a trial in about 1.8 million children. Albert Sabin’s oral drops followed in the early 1960s, and India’s Pulse Polio campaign used the same kind of drops from 1995. India’s last wild polio case came in January 2011, and the WHO certified the country polio-free in 2014.

President Franklin D. Roosevelt, himself paralysed at 39 in 1921, founded the charity that became the March of Dimes in 1938. Since 1946, his face has been on the dime, the ten-cent coin, in honour of the March of Dimes. In the 1950s, polio was America’s great fear, and pools and summer camps closed in “polio season”. Salk became a national hero, and when asked who owned the patent on his vaccine, he replied, “Could you patent the sun?”

Remember it like this: Salk’s shot, Sabin’s sip. Salk’s 1955 vaccine was an injection, while Sabin’s, from the early 1960s, was drops by mouth, the kind India used in Pulse Polio.

Words to know

Epidemic
A sudden rise in cases within one population. Compare pandemic, which is worldwide, and endemic, which is always present.
Sanitation
Clean water and getting rid of sewage.
Immunity
Protection against a disease that comes after catching it or being vaccinated.
Foster
To encourage or promote. Here, it happened without anyone meaning it to.
Paralysis
Losing the ability to move muscles.

Reading tip: Write the causal chain as arrows as you read. Each link matters on its own.

p.91The “amphibian crisis”conservation · extinction · amphibians

In short: Recent reports describe apparently drastic declines in amphibian populations, and the extinction of several of the world’s endangered amphibian species. If real, the declines may signal a general trend toward extinction. Many environmentalists call for immediate action to remedy this “amphibian crisis”. They see it as a sign of general, catastrophic environmental degradation caused by human activity. The author then draws a preliminary distinction that is “far too often ignored”: a declining population should not be confused with an endangered one. An endangered population is always rare and almost always small. By definition, it is under constant threat of extinction, even without a proximate cause in human activity. Its disappearance, however unfortunate, “should come as no great surprise”. Moreover, chance events, which may say nothing about the direction of population trends, can lead to its extinction. The probability of extinction from such random factors … The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

Picture a pond full of tadpoles that will one day hop about on land as frogs. Many amphibians live this kind of “double life”, and that is what their name means. The group includes frogs, toads, salamanders, newts and limbless caecilians. Their thin, permeable skin lets water and oxygen pass through, and pollutants too.

Because of that skin and double life, amphibians are often called early-warning signals for the environment, or indicator species. People call them the “canary in the coal mine”. Miners once carried canaries, which collapse from toxic gas before humans do.

The alarm spread among scientists around 1989–90. Researchers comparing notes found frog declines on several continents. Costa Rica’s brilliant orange golden toad, last seen in 1989, became the symbol of that alarm.

Ecologists separate steady trends, such as habitat loss and pollution, from random, or stochastic, shocks. A shock might be a drought, a disease outbreak or a run of bad breeding years. A large population absorbs such shocks, but a tiny one can be wiped out by them.

The Red List is kept by the IUCN, the global conservation authority. It sorts species into steps, from safest to worst: Least Concern, Near Threatened, Vulnerable, Endangered, Critically Endangered, Extinct in the Wild and Extinct.

Mark Twain’s famous story “The Celebrated Jumping Frog of Calaveras County”, from 1865, is taught in American schools. The California red-legged frog is thought to be Twain’s frog. The US government has listed it as threatened since 1996.

Remember it like this: Amphibian means “double life”: a frog starts as a tadpole in the water and grows up to live on land.

Words to know

Drastic
Severe and sudden.
Remedy
To fix or cure.
Degradation
Getting worse, or a decline in quality.
Preliminary
Coming first, to prepare for what follows.
Proximate cause
The immediate, direct cause, not an underlying or distant one.
Stochastic
Random, ruled by chance.

Reading tip: The author’s tone is in phrases like “if real”, “apparently”, and “far too often ignored”. Separate what the environmentalists claim from what the author claims. That line is easy to blur.

p.92Anole lizards of the Caribbeananoles · Caribbean islands · adaptation

In short: On certain Caribbean islands, anole lizard species that live together, or sympatrically, occupy different habitats. Some live only in grass, some only on tree trunks and some only on twigs. Their bodies differ too. Grass dwellers are slender with long tails, tree dwellers are stocky with long legs, and twig dwellers are slender but stubby-legged. What is striking is not that species living side by side differ. That is common among closely related species living together. It is that the same three habitat specialists are found on each of four islands: Puerto Rico, Cuba, Hispaniola and Jamaica. Puerto Rico’s twig species closely resembles the twig species of the other three islands in body form, habitat use and behaviour. The same holds for the other specialists. This could be explained in different ways. One possibility is that an ancestral species adapted to a particular niche on one island, then travelled over water to colonise the others. Or, the text continues, the ancestral species might … The excerpt breaks off there, as a second explanation begins.

The story

Picture a male lizard on a tree trunk, flashing a colourful throat fan called a dewlap as a signal. This is an anole. Anoles are small lizards, with hundreds of species across the Caribbean and the Americas. The green anole of the southeastern US is often sold as an “American chameleon”, because it turns from green to brown.

The Caribbean’s four big islands are called the Greater Antilles: Cuba, Hispaniola, Jamaica and Puerto Rico. Hispaniola is shared by two countries, Haiti and the Dominican Republic. Puerto Rico has been a US territory since 1898, and its people have been US citizens since 1917.

Biologists call anoles that specialise in one habitat ecomorphs, a term coined in 1972. They recognise six anole types, and the grass, trunk and twig types are three of them.

Each body fits its job. Long legs give speed on broad trunks. Short legs give careful footing on narrow twigs, just as people take small, careful steps along a narrow ledge. Long tails help with balance in grass.

Green anoles are everyday garden lizards in Florida and Georgia. In Florida, brown anoles from Cuba arrived as invaders and pushed the native green anoles higher into the trees. Within about 15 years, the green anoles evolved larger, stickier toe pads. This is a much-reported case of evolution happening in real time.

Remember it like this: An ecomorph is a lizard built for its address. Trunk lizards have long legs for speed, twig lizards have short legs for careful steps, and grass lizards have long tails for balance.

Words to know

Sympatric
Living in the same area. Compare allopatric: living in separate areas.
Morphology
The shape and build of a body.
Niche
A species’ role and way of life in its environment.
Colonize
To arrive in a new place and set up a population there.
Stocky
Short and solidly built.

Reading tip: To see why a sentence is there, read the sentences just before and after it and name its job in your own words.

p.93Sloth bears carrying their cubsanimal behaviour · bears · India

In short: The sloth bear, an insect-eater native to Nepal, has only one behaviour that truly sets it apart from other bears. Mothers carry their cubs, at least part of the time, until the cubs are about nine months old, even though the cubs can walk at six months. Explanation 1: cub-carrying also occurs in some other myrmecophagous, or ant-eating, mammals. So perhaps ant-eating requires it, since myrmecophagy entails a low metabolic rate and high energy costs when walking between food patches. However, polar bears move just as inefficiently, yet their cubs walk alongside their mother. And sloth bears and American black bears are similar in size, have home ranges of similar size, and travel at similar rates and over similar distances. If black bear cubs can keep up with their mother, sloth bear cubs should be able to as well. Explanation 2: defence against predators. Black bear cubs use trees for defence, while brown bears and polar bears, which regularly live in treeless places, rely on aggression to protect their cubs. The text then develops this explanation, but the excerpt breaks off.

The story

Picture a shaggy, black-coated bear ripping open a termite mound with its long, curved claws. It has a cream “V” on its chest. It can close its nostrils, and a gap in its front teeth plus mobile lips let it suck up termites like a vacuum cleaner, loudly. This is the sloth bear, named because early European naturalists thought it was related to the tree sloth.

The sloth bear lives mainly in India, with smaller populations in Sri Lanka and Nepal. Baloo in Kipling’s The Jungle Book, set in the Seoni forests, is usually identified as a sloth bear. For centuries, sloth bear cubs were captured to become street “dancing bears”. The practice ended in India in 2009, when the last ones were rescued.

There are eight bear species in the world: American black, brown, polar, Asiatic black, sun, sloth, spectacled and the giant panda. Brown bears include the grizzly. India hosts four of the eight: the sloth, Asiatic black, Himalayan brown and sun bears.

Eating ants and termites is called myrmecophagy, and these insects are tiny, low-energy food. So specialists such as anteaters and pangolins tend to have a low metabolic rate, burning energy slowly, like a small engine that sips fuel. Giant anteater pups ride on their mother’s back, and pangolin babies cling to their mother’s tail.

Biologists often test an explanation with the comparative method: they check other species. If the explanation is right, animals living under the same conditions should show the same behaviour. A species that has the condition but lacks the behaviour counts against the explanation.

America’s most familiar bear is the American black bear. Smokey Bear, the Forest Service’s fire-prevention mascot since 1944, is a black bear. The teddy bear is named after President Theodore Roosevelt. On a 1902 hunting trip, he refused to shoot a black bear that had been tied up.

Remember it like this: Baloo in The Jungle Book is usually identified as a sloth bear. Picture a real one: shaggy, with a cream “V” on its chest, sucking up termites like a noisy vacuum cleaner.

Words to know

Myrmecophagous
Feeding on ants and termites.
Necessitate
To make necessary.
Entail
To involve something as a necessary result.
Metabolic rate
How fast an animal uses energy.
Locomotion
Moving from place to place.
Home range
The area an animal normally travels in.

Reading tip: When the author mentions something, decide which explanation it serves and what step of the argument it supports.

p.94Maternal strategies of sealsmarine mammals · maternal strategy · pinnipeds

In short: Until recently, zoologists believed that all phocids, the true seals, used a different maternal strategy from otariids, the fur seals and sea lions. Both are families of pinnipeds. Otariid mothers forage. They build moderate fat stores, or blubber, before reaching the breeding sites, and fast for 5–11 days after giving birth. Then, for the rest of lactation, the nursing period, they go back and forth between feeding at sea to replenish their fat and nursing their pups on land. Depending on species, lactation lasts 4 months to 3 years. Phocid mothers were assumed to fast. They arrive with large energy stores and fast throughout lactation, which lasts 4 to 50 days depending on species. However, recent studies of harbour seals, a phocid species, found that nursing mothers began foraging about 6 days after giving birth. They made an average of 7 foraging trips during the rest of their 24-day lactation. The text then continues, but the excerpt breaks off.

The story

Picture the ball-balancing “seal” at a circus. It is usually a California sea lion. Seals, sea lions and walruses are pinnipeds, which means “fin-footed”. They are sea mammals that give birth on land or ice.

Pinnipeds fall into three families. Phocids, the “true” or earless seals, have no ear flaps. On land they wriggle on their bellies, and in water they swim with their hind flippers. Elephant seals and hooded seals are phocids.

Otariids, the eared seals, include sea lions and fur seals, so the circus sea lion is one of them. They have small ear flaps and can turn their hind flippers forward to “walk” on land. Walruses form a family of their own.

Biologists have names for two ways of paying for motherhood. Mothers who pay from stored reserves are capital breeders, like people living off their savings. Mothers who feed as they go are income breeders, like people living on a salary. A mother who fasts while nursing is a capital breeder, and one who forages is an income breeder.

Blubber is a thick layer of fat under the skin. It keeps the animal warm in cold water and also stores energy, like a warm blanket and a packed lunch in one.

Since 1990, hundreds of California sea lions have hauled themselves out onto the docks of San Francisco’s Pier 39. The pier is now a top tourist sight. All US marine mammals have been protected since the Marine Mammal Protection Act of 1972.

Remember it like this: Phocids are the earless wrigglers. Otariids have little ear flaps and can “walk” on their flippers, like the ball-balancing circus “seal”, which is usually a sea lion.

Words to know

Maternal strategy
How a mother shares out her energy and time in raising her young.
Forage
To search for and gather food.
Fast
To go without eating.
Lactation
Making milk. Also, the nursing period.
Replenish
To fill up again.
Commence
To begin.

Reading tip: Make a quick two-column table (fat stores, feeding, lactation length) and keep “all”, “some”, and “assumed” straight. A summary of the text must not include anything the text doesn’t do.

p.95“Commuter” diatoms and their tidal clockbiological clocks · tides · experiment logic

In short: Biologists have become interested in a tide-linked periodic behaviour of the diatom Hantzschia virgata. It is a microscopic golden-brown alga that lives in the intertidal zone, the part of the shore washed by tides. These “commuter” diatoms stay buried in the sand at high tide and come up onto the sand surface during the daytime low tide. Just before the rising tide floods the sand, they burrow down again. Hypothesis: some scientists think the diatoms sense low tide through a change in their surroundings, such as a shift in temperature or in pressure caused by tidal movement. Test: under constant laboratory conditions, the diatoms still burrow on schedule for several weeks. Conclusion: rather than relying on cues from their surroundings, they have an internal pacemaker, a biological clock, that lets them anticipate periodic changes. A commuter diatom’s clock is unusually accurate, a consequence of the … The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

Picture a single-celled alga living inside an intricate, glass-like shell of silica. This is a diatom, and diatoms live in oceans, lakes and wet sand. Like plants, they photosynthesise, making food from sunlight, so they need light. They are estimated to produce about a fifth of the oxygen on Earth.

Deposits of fossil diatoms, called diatomaceous earth, are used in filters and toothpaste. In 1867, Alfred Nobel used them to keep the nitroglycerine in his dynamite stable.

Tides are caused mainly by the Moon’s gravity. Most coasts get two high tides and two low tides a day. One tidal cycle averages about 12 hours 25 minutes. So each day’s low tide comes about 50 minutes later than the day before, like a train that runs a little later every day.

Circadian rhythms, from the Latin for “about a day”, run on about 24 hours. Sleep, hormones and jet lag all involve them (see p.62 and p.87). Circatidal rhythms run on about 12.4 hours. In the 1950s, fiddler crabs on US beaches were famously shown to keep their tidal activity rhythms even in lab tanks.

The classic test for an internal clock is to remove every outside cue, keeping light, temperature and pressure constant. If the rhythm keeps going, or “free-runs”, the timing must come from inside the living thing. Think of a person in a windowless room who still wakes at about the usual hour.

Remember it like this: Circadian means “about a day”: about 24 hours. Circatidal means “about a tide”: about 12.4 hours.

Words to know

Periodic
Recurring, or coming back, at regular intervals.
Intertidal zone
The strip of shore that is under water at high tide and uncovered at low tide.
Inundate
To flood.
Cue
A signal that sets off a response.
Pacemaker
Here, an internal timekeeper that sets a rhythm.
Anticipate
To act before an expected event happens.

Reading tip: Track the experiment’s logic: if the behaviour continues without the cue, the cue isn’t what times it. When you work out what the text suggests, rely only on reasons it states itself.

p.96Moth and butterfly population cyclespopulation ecology · insects · viruses

In short: Lepidoptera are moths and butterflies, which are caterpillars for most of their life cycle. A few forest species of lepidoptera rise and fall in numbers again and again at regular intervals. These ups and downs are called population cycles. Many things affect population levels, but a regular pattern seems to imply one dominant driving force. Despite much research, finding it has proved “surprisingly elusive”. The usual approach is to measure the deaths caused by different agents, such as predatory birds or parasites. For lepidoptera, this has not been productive. Attempts to change the cycles, by altering the caterpillars’ habitat or by reducing their numbers, have also failed. Taken together, the evidence implies that these populations, if they are not self-regulating, may be regulated by an agent more intimately connected with the insect than birds or parasites. Recent work suggests that this agent may be a virus. Viral disease had long been reported in caterpillar populations that were in decline, but … The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there. The full article goes on to discuss the nuclear polyhedrosis virus.

The story

Picture a forest in summer with bare trees, as if winter had come early. Caterpillar outbreaks can do this to millions of hectares of forest. The caterpillars belong to the lepidoptera, a name that means “scale-wings”. They go through complete metamorphosis, a life in four stages: egg, caterpillar or larva (the eating stage), pupa in a cocoon or chrysalis, and winged adult.

One famous cycling species is the larch budmoth of the European Alps. Tree rings there show its outbreaks every 8–10 years for over a thousand years. Beyond insects, the textbook case is Canada’s lynx, a wild cat, and the snowshoe hare, which cycle about every ten years. The Hudson’s Bay Company recorded this cycle in its fur-trading records from the 1800s.

Lemmings, which are small rodents, cycle every 3–4 years. Their famous “mass suicide” is a myth, staged for a Disney documentary. Another key idea is density dependence. Some forces hit a population harder as it gets more crowded, such as food shortage or disease spreading between neighbours.

The nuclear polyhedrosis virus, or NPV, attacks caterpillars. Its particles are packed inside protein crystals called “polyhedra”, and these give the virus its name.

Another famous cycling species is the spongy moth, called the “gypsy moth” until scientists renamed it in 2022. In 1869 a French amateur naturalist, Trouvelot, was trying to breed silk moths near Boston when he accidentally let it escape. It has stripped the leaves of eastern US forests ever since. Today the US Forest Service uses an NPV spray called “Gypchek” against it, as a natural pesticide.

In outbreak years, spongy moth caterpillars strip whole hillsides in New England and the Midwest. In 1981 they stripped about 13 million acres. Americans there remember caterpillars “raining” from the trees and their droppings pattering down like rain.

Remember it like this: Egg, caterpillar, cocoon, wings: four lives in one insect. The caterpillar is the hungry one, and in an outbreak year it can leave a whole forest bare.

Words to know

Recurring
Happening again and again.
Fluctuation
Rising and falling.
Elusive
Hard to find or pin down.
Mortality
Death, or the death rate.
Agent
Something that causes an effect, such as a predator, a parasite or a disease germ.
Self-regulating
Kept in check from within, by the population itself.

Reading tip: To judge what would weaken the case, first restate the exact conclusion and the evidence the text gives for it. Also be ready to say why researchers used a given method.

p.97Biological pest control (biocontrol)biocontrol · agriculture · ecology

In short: Biocontrol agents, such as insects, fungi and viruses, eat, infect or live as parasites on targeted pests. They are more selective than most chemical pesticides, because they ordinarily destroy only unwanted species. However, they can harm nontarget species too, for example by competing for resources. An agent might reduce the benefits that a desirable animal brings by eating the plant on which that animal prefers to lay its eggs. Another indirect harm happened in England. A virus brought in to control rabbits reduced the amount of open ground, because large rabbit populations keep ground cover short. That reduced underground ant nests, which set off the extinction of a blue butterfly whose young depended on the nests for shelter. Known extinctions or disruptions from indirect interactions are few. This paucity may reflect not their rarity but a failure to look for or detect them: most organisms likely to be harmed by indirect … The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

Picture the citrus groves of California under attack from a pest called the cottony cushion scale insect. In 1888–89 an Australian ladybird, the vedalia beetle, was brought in, and it rescued the citrus industry. This was the founding success of biological control, which fights a pest with its natural enemies: predators, parasites or disease organisms. Classical biocontrol imports those enemies from the pest’s native range, the region it first came from.

India has tried this too. In 1983 it released a Mexican leaf beetle against Parthenium, an invasive weed known as “congress grass”. But some releases have famously backfired. In 1935 cane toads were released in Queensland, Australia, to eat sugar-cane beetles.

The toads ignored the beetles, spread across the continent and poisoned native predators. The small Indian mongoose was brought to Jamaica in 1872 and Hawaii in 1883 to kill rats in the cane fields. Instead, it devastated native birds and reptiles. It was like hiring a guard to catch burglars, only to see him rob the neighbours.

In England, a rabbit disease set off a chain of events. Myxomatosis, a South American rabbit virus, was released in Australia in 1950 and reached Britain in 1953. It killed about 99% of British rabbits. With no rabbits grazing, the grassland turf grew tall and the soil surface cooled.

A heat-loving red ant declined, and the large blue butterfly depended on that ant. Its caterpillars trick the ants into carrying them into their nests, where the caterpillars eat the ant grubs. The large blue became extinct in Britain in 1979. Brought back from Sweden from 1983 onward, it is now a celebrated conservation comeback.

Chemical pesticides fell out of favour after the American writer Rachel Carson published Silent Spring in 1962. Its title imagines a spring with no birdsong, and the book exposed how DDT and other pesticides were harming wildlife, especially birds. A landmark of the US environmental movement, it led to the Environmental Protection Agency in 1970 and the US ban on DDT in 1972. The ban boosted interest in biological alternatives, and US schoolchildren still learn how the bald eagle recovered after it.

Remember it like this: Think of indirect harm as a row of dominoes. Push one piece, and the fall can run through several others before it knocks over a species nobody was aiming at.

Words to know

Parasitize
To live on or in a host and harm it.
Nontarget species
Species that a control method is not meant to affect.
Confer
To give or grant, as in “benefits conferred”.
Paucity
Scarcity, or a small amount.
Indirect interaction
An effect passed through a chain of other species, not by direct contact.
Adversely
Harmfully.

Reading tip: When you sum up the text, check your verb, and how strongly you state the claim, against the text as a whole, not against one example. Use only distinctions the text actually draws.

p.98Salmon habitat loss and genetic diversityconservation genetics · salmon · straying

In short: Over the last 150 years, human activity has wiped out large stretches of salmon habitat. The activities were mining, livestock grazing, logging, farming, and building for recreation and for towns and cities. The numerical effect is obvious, since damaged regions have fewer salmon than pristine, untouched ones. But habitat loss can also reduce genetic diversity, the variety of genes in a species. This is most evident when whole salmon populations go extinct. Most analysts believe some environmental damage lies behind the end of many extinct populations. Some rivers have been recolonised, but the original populations’ unique genes are lost. Large-scale disturbance in one place can also change the genetic make-up of populations in neighbouring areas, even pristine ones. Why? Salmon have a strong homing instinct for their natal stream, the stream where they were born. But a fraction of returning fish, rarely more than 15%, stray and spawn in nearby streams. Low levels of straying are crucial, since the process provides a … The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

Picture a young salmon hatching in a freshwater stream. Salmon are anadromous: born in fresh water, they migrate to the ocean to grow, then return years later to spawn. Pacific salmon die after spawning once, but Atlantic salmon can spawn more than once. North America’s five main Pacific species are chinook, also called king, coho, sockeye, pink and chum.

How do they find their way home? Young salmon imprint on, or lock into memory, the chemical “scent” of their home stream. Adults cross the ocean, possibly guided by Earth’s magnetic field, then follow that scent upstream. Arthur Hasler’s research in the 1950s explored this homing by smell.

Because most fish return home, each stream’s population, called a “run” or “stock”, adapts to local conditions. These include water temperature, migration distance and timing. Such varied populations steady the whole species, which is called the portfolio effect. It works like an investor who spreads savings across many different shares.

A classic warning about low diversity is the Irish potato famine of 1845–49. At that time Ireland relied largely on a single potato variety. Salmon face their own threats, summed up as the “four H’s”. These are habitat, hydropower dams, harvest, and hatcheries, the places where people breed fish.

From the early 1990s, many Pacific salmon populations were listed under the Endangered Species Act, the US law that protects species at risk. The agency in charge treats distinct salmon populations as units that it can protect separately. In 1992 a single sockeye, nicknamed “Lonesome Larry”, returned to Idaho’s Redfish Lake. The fish became a symbol of the decline.

Salmon are also central to the tribes of the Pacific Northwest (see Topic 2 for Native American history). In 1974 a court ruling, the Boldt decision, affirmed their treaty right to half the harvestable catch in Washington State. Salmon are the icon of the Pacific Northwest and Alaska, from Seattle’s fish-throwing Pike Place Market to Alaska’s Bristol Bay, the world’s largest sockeye run. Fights over dams and salmon are recurring news in the region.

Remember it like this: A salmon’s map is its nose: it learns the smell of its home stream when young and follows it back years later. It is like finding your mother’s kitchen by the smell of her cooking.

Words to know

Pristine
Untouched, still in its original condition.
Degraded
Damaged, or lower in quality.
Demise
End, or death.
Natal
Relating to birth. A natal stream is the stream where the fish was born.
Spawn
Of fish: to release eggs and sperm, or to breed.
Stray
To wander from the usual path. Here, to spawn in a stream other than the natal one.
Locale
A particular place.

Reading tip: Wherever the author asks “Why?”, read what follows slowly and pin down exactly what it explains. Draw conclusions only from the text’s own statements, without adding new assumptions.

p.99Removing duck-nest predators: what happens to songbirds?wildlife management · food webs · field experiment

In short: Grassland songbirds often nest in the same mix of grassland and wetland as waterfowl, especially in the upland, the drier ground around the wetlands. Some management aimed at waterfowl, such as improving or restoring habitat, may also benefit songbirds. But the impact of other measures is hard to predict, especially controlling waterfowl predators. Possibility 1: most predators of waterfowl nests also raid songbird nests when they get the chance. So removing them could directly increase songbird nesting success. Possibility 2: small mammals such as mice and ground squirrels are important in the diets of many waterfowl-nest predators. These small mammals can themselves be important predators of songbird nests. So removing the waterfowl-nest predators could affect songbird success indirectly, through later increases in small-mammal numbers. In 1995 and 1996, researchers trapped and removed certain waterfowl-nest predators, mainly raccoons and striped skunks. Then they tracked how many songbird nests survived. “Surprisingly, they observed …” The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

Picture grassland dotted with millions of shallow wetlands, left behind by retreating Ice Age glaciers. This is the Prairie Pothole Region, which stretches across the Dakotas, Minnesota, Iowa, Montana and Canada’s prairie provinces. It produces around half or more of North America’s ducks. That is why it is called “America’s duck factory”.

Wildlife managers focus on ducks for a reason. Waterfowl, meaning ducks, geese and swans, are game birds, hunted for sport. Since 1934, US duck hunters have had to buy a federal “Duck Stamp”, and the money buys wetland habitat. A hunters’ group, Ducks Unlimited, also pays for conservation.

Songbirds are often an afterthought, even though grassland birds such as meadowlarks and bobolinks are among North America’s fastest-declining birds. Raccoons, striped skunks, red foxes, badgers, crows and magpies all take eggs and chicks. Smaller ground squirrels and mice raid small songbird nests too. Trapping predators in the nesting season is a real management tool, but a controversial one.

A food web is the network of who eats whom, and like a spider’s web, a tug on one strand can move others. Removing one predator can let its prey or its smaller competitors multiply. When mid-sized predators boom after larger ones are removed, ecologists call it mesopredator release.

Duck hunting is a major American tradition, and the yearly Federal Duck Stamp art contest is a well-known institution. Raccoons, jokingly called “trash pandas”, and skunks are familiar backyard animals across the US.

Remember it like this: The Ice Age left the ponds, ducks fill them, and hunters’ Duck Stamp money protects them. That is America’s duck factory.

Words to know

Waterfowl
Ducks, geese and swans.
Upland
Here, the drier ground around a wetland.
Complex
As a noun: a group of connected parts, here grasslands and wetlands.
Opportunistically
By grabbing chances when they come up, not by specialising.
Nesting success
The share of nests that produce young.
Subsequent
Following, or later.

Reading tip: Before reading the result, note what each prediction expects, then see what the data showed. For one animal’s role, go back to the exact sentences that mention that animal.

p.100Bright colours and mate choice: why carotenoids mattersexual selection · mate choice · pigments

In short: Carotenoids, a family of natural pigments, are an important part of many animals’ colourful signals. Animals get them directly from the plants and algae that make them, or indirectly by eating insects, and store them in various tissues. Studies of several species show that females prefer males with brighter carotenoid-based colouring. Owens and Olson put forward a hypothesis: carotenoid colouring would matter in mate choice if carotenoids were either rare or required for health. The conventional view is that they matter because they are rare. On this view, healthier males can forage for, or find, more of the pigments than lower-quality rivals. That may be true, but growing evidence suggests they also matter because they are required. The immune system uses them, and so do the detoxification processes that keep the body healthy. Males may have to use scarce carotenoids either for immune defence and detoxification or for attracting females. Males more susceptible to disease and parasites will have to use their carotenoids to boost … The excerpt breaks off mid-sentence there.

The story

Think of carrots, tomatoes, mangoes, saffron and autumn leaves. Their yellow, orange and red colours come from carotenoids. Carrots owe their colour to beta-carotene, which the body turns into vitamin A. Only plants, algae, fungi and some microbes can make carotenoids, so animals must eat them.

Biologists call such colouring “diet-dependent”, and for these colours the saying “you are what you eat” comes true. Flamingos hatch grey-white and turn pink from the carotenoids in the algae and brine shrimp they eat. Zoos feed them supplements to keep them pink. Carotenoids also make salmon flesh pink and cooked shrimp red.

Male house finches range from pale yellow to bright red depending on their diet. In research in the 1990s, females preferred the redder males. The red of the northern cardinal and the orange spots of male guppies also come from carotenoids.

In 1871 Darwin described sexual selection: traits can evolve because they win mates, even if they cost survival. Darwin wrote that the sight of a peacock’s tail feather “makes me sick!”, because it was so hard to explain by survival alone. The peacock is, of course, India’s national bird.

Animal signals are colours, songs, scents and displays that pass information to other animals, such as rivals or possible mates. Bird song and the flashing of fireflies are other familiar examples.
