In the US, the bright-red northern cardinal is the state bird of seven states, more than any other bird. It gives its name to sports teams such as the St. Louis Cardinals. The pink plastic lawn flamingo is a beloved piece of American kitsch, or lovably tacky decoration.

Remember it like this: A flamingo is pink because of its dinner: the algae and brine shrimp it eats. No pigment on the menu, no pink in the feathers.

Words to know

Pigment
A substance that gives colour.
Coloration
An animal’s pattern of colour and how strong it is.
Counterpart
One that matches another in role. Here, rival males.
Detoxification
Removing harmful substances from the body, or making them harmless.
Susceptible
Easily affected or harmed, for example by disease.

Reading tip: Signal phrases such as “Although this may be true” and “also” show how a newer view relates to an older one, so mark them as you read. Keep the conventional view and the newer view separate, because both matter.

Topic 8 · Other recurring themes 6 primers · beyond the readings

Some subjects come back again and again in writing about America. Topics 1–7 cover the readings themselves. This section fills the remaining gaps: the US economy and markets, challenges to religion, American power, the Cold War, new technology and cell biology. Each primer is a short briefing, in plain words, on what American students pick up at school and from the news. The search box covers these too.

8.1American economy, finance & capital marketsWall Street · the Fed · crashes & reforms · corporations · labour

Why it matters: Business writing on company strategy, regulation, labour history or economic theory expects you to know how US markets and institutions work. For capitalism, socialism and the other “isms”, see Foundation 4.

How the system works

Picture a start-up in Silicon Valley, the centre of American venture capital, or VC. VC firms and private backers called angel investors fund young, high-risk companies in exchange for equity, a share of ownership. Later a company may sell stock, equity split into shares that sometimes pay dividends, and its first public sale of stock is its IPO. It can also borrow by issuing bonds, which pay fixed interest, and bond prices fall when interest rates rise.

Private equity firms buy whole companies, often with borrowed money, as in the “leveraged buyouts” of the 1980s. Shares trade on the New York Stock Exchange on Wall Street, dating from 1792, and on NASDAQ, an electronic, tech-heavy market from 1971. The main indexes are the Dow Jones Industrial Average of 30 giant firms, the S&P 500, the usual benchmark, and the Nasdaq Composite. A bull market is rising, and a bear market has fallen 20% or more.

Shareholders own a company but managers run it, a split that Berle and Means called the “separation of ownership and control” in 1932. It creates a principal–agent problem: the owners must trust hired managers, whose interests may differ from theirs. In 1970 Milton Friedman argued that a business’s social responsibility is to increase its profits. That view is now challenged by “stakeholder capitalism”, which looks beyond shareholders, and by ESG investing, which rates firms on environmental, social and governance grounds.

Two levers steer the economy: fiscal policy, which is Congress’s taxing and spending, and monetary policy, run by the Federal Reserve. The Fed, the central bank since 1913, sets interest rates with a dual mandate: maximum employment and stable prices. In the 1930s Keynes said governments should spend to fight slumps, but Friedman and the Chicago School said to trust markets and control the money supply. In the 1980s Reagan cut taxes and deregulated, an approach called “supply-side” economics.

The recurring American argument pits regulation against laissez-faire, the idea of leaving markets alone. On one side stand the antitrust laws, starting with the Sherman Act of 1890. Under them Standard Oil was broken up in 1911 and AT&T in 1984, and in 2024 Google was ruled an illegal search monopolist. On the other side stands faith in open competition.

Remember it like this: The Fed is to America what the Reserve Bank of India is to India.

Booms, busts & reforms

The modern story starts in the Gilded Age, from the 1870s to 1900. It was the age of railroads and of business giants such as Rockefeller in oil, Carnegie in steel and J.P. Morgan in banking. It was also the era of “robber barons” and violent strikes. In 1913 the Federal Reserve was created, and the 16th Amendment allowed a federal income tax.

Then came the Wall Street Crash of “Black Tuesday”, 29 October 1929, and the Great Depression, with unemployment at about 25% by 1933. President Roosevelt, known as FDR, answered with the New Deal of 1933–39. It brought bank-deposit insurance through the FDIC, the SEC to police Wall Street, and in 1935 Social Security and union rights under the Wagner Act. Glass–Steagall separated ordinary and investment banking until it was largely repealed in 1999.

At Bretton Woods in 1944, the dollar was tied to gold and other currencies to the dollar, and the IMF and World Bank were created (see 8.3). Nixon cut the dollar’s link to gold in 1971, and oil shocks then brought stagflation: rising prices and high unemployment at once. To crush inflation, Fed chair Paul Volcker raised interest rates to about 20%. On “Black Monday” in 1987, the Dow fell 22.6% in a single day.

Next came the dot-com bubble and bust of 1995–2002, when internet shares soared and then collapsed. The frauds at Enron in 2001 and at WorldCom led to the Sarbanes–Oxley Act of 2002. The global financial crisis of 2007–09 centred on risky “subprime” mortgages bundled into securities. Lehman Brothers collapsed on 15 September 2008, followed by the $700 billion TARP bailout and the Dodd–Frank reforms of 2010.

This was the era of “too big to fail” and of Occupy Wall Street, whose 2011 slogan was “We are the 99%”. Then came the COVID crash and rebound, and inflation peaked at 9.1% in June 2022, a 40-year high. The Fed raised rates rapidly, and Silicon Valley Bank failed in 2023.

Remember it like this: Boom, bust, new rules: 1929 and the New Deal, Enron and Sarbanes–Oxley, 2008 and Dodd–Frank.

Workers and unions

American unions grew from two roots. The AFL, founded in 1886, organised skilled crafts, while the CIO, from 1935, organised whole industries such as cars and steel. About a third of US workers were in unions in the mid-1950s, against about 10% today.

Some labour topics come up again and again: the eight-hour day, child labour, and women and immigrants in factories. Another is why the US, unlike Europe, never developed a strong socialist or labour party. That question is one strand of “American exceptionalism”, the idea that America is a special case.

Remember it like this: A third then, a tenth now: US union membership in the mid-1950s and today.

Words to know

Equity
A share of ownership in a company, held as stock.
Bond
A loan that can be bought and sold and that pays fixed interest.
Venture capital
Money put into young, high-risk companies in exchange for a share of ownership.
Economies of scale
The cost of each unit falls as a firm makes more.
Barriers to entry
Things that keep new competitors out, such as high start-up costs, patents, regulation and network effects.
Vertical / horizontal integration
Vertical integration means owning each stage of your own supply chain, as Carnegie owned mines, railways and mills. Horizontal integration means buying competitors at the same stage, as Standard Oil did.
Externality
A cost or benefit that falls on outsiders and is not reflected in the price. Pollution is a negative externality.
Moral hazard
Taking bigger risks because someone else will bear the cost, as when banks expect a bailout.
Stagflation
Inflation together with a stalled economy and high unemployment.
Antitrust
Laws against monopolies and price-fixing cartels. Other countries call this “competition law”.

Reading tip: Business writing usually covers a firm’s strategy and why it worked, a regulation and its unintended effects, or an economist’s model tested against evidence. Watch causal language (“led to”, “contributed to”, “coincided with”) and keep correlation separate from cause.

8.2Challenges to religion: science, secularism & the courtsGalileo · Darwin · Scopes trial · church and state · secularization

Why it matters: Readings on the Scientific Revolution, how people reacted to Darwin, Puritan New England or church–state law take this story for granted. America is unusual: it has no official church, yet it is far more religious than most rich countries.

From Galileo to Darwin

In 1543 Copernicus put the Sun, not Earth, at the centre, a model called heliocentrism (see Topic 6). In 1633 the Roman Inquisition, a Church court, tried Galileo for defending Copernicus. He was forced to recant, or publicly take back his views, and was kept under house arrest until his death in 1642. The Catholic Church formally admitted its error in 1992.

In 1687 Newton’s laws pictured a “clockwork universe”, a machine that runs by fixed laws. Many Enlightenment thinkers, including American Founders such as Jefferson and Franklin, leaned toward Deism, belief in a Creator who does not intervene. In the 1830s Lyell’s geology showed an Earth far older than a literal reading of the Bible allows. Earlier, Archbishop Ussher had dated Creation to 4004 BC.

In 1859 Darwin published Origin of Species, and the Oxford debate of 1860 pitted T.H. Huxley against Bishop Wilberforce. In 1871 The Descent of Man applied evolution to humans (see Topic 7).

Then came the critics, from the 1840s to the 1920s. Marx called religion “the opium of the people”, Nietzsche declared “God is dead” in 1882, and Freud described religion as an illusion in 1927. German “higher criticism” studied the Bible as a historical document.

Remember it like this: In Deism, God is a clockmaker who builds and winds the clock, then leaves it to tick on its own.

Religion in America

Picture the Pilgrims on the Mayflower in 1620 and the Puritans of Massachusetts Bay in 1630, who spoke of “a city upon a hill”. Both groups sought freedom for their own faith, not for everyone’s. Dissenters such as Roger Williams, who founded Rhode Island in 1636, and Anne Hutchinson were expelled. In 1692 came the Salem witch trials.

The First and Second Great Awakenings were waves of religious revival in the 1730s–40s and the early 1800s. They fuelled evangelical Protestantism, which stresses personal conversion, and reform movements for abolition, temperance and women’s rights. Abolition meant ending slavery, and temperance meant turning away from alcohol. Later, the civil rights movement was organised from Black churches, and Martin Luther King Jr. was a Baptist minister.

The First Amendment of 1791 bars an “establishment” of religion and protects its “free exercise”. The first part, the Establishment Clause, means there is no official church, and Jefferson called this a “wall of separation” in 1802. Yet “under God” entered the Pledge of Allegiance in 1954, and “In God We Trust” became the national motto in 1956. Both were Cold War gestures against “godless communism”.

In 1925 the Scopes “Monkey Trial” in Tennessee pitted Clarence Darrow against William Jennings Bryan. The teacher, John Scopes, was fined $100, and the trial was later dramatised in Inherit the Wind. Epperson v. Arkansas in 1968 struck down bans on teaching evolution, and Edwards v. Aguillard in 1987 struck down “creation science” laws. In 2005 Kitzmiller v. Dover rejected intelligent design, the claim that life’s complexity shows a designer, in science classes.

Engel v. Vitale in 1962 ended official school prayer, while Kennedy v. Bremerton in 2022 protected a football coach’s personal prayer on the field. Today the religiously unaffiliated, the “nones”, have risen to about 28% of US adults. Meanwhile the “religious right”, organised since the Moral Majority of 1979, remains a major political force.

Remember it like this: Picture a wall with the government on one side and the churches on the other. Like India, America has no state religion.

Words to know

Heliocentrism
The model of the solar system with the Sun at the centre.
Recant
To take back a belief or statement in public.
Deism
Belief in a Creator who does not step into the affairs of the world.
Secularization
The decline of religion’s influence over public life. Max Weber called the modern age the “disenchantment of the world”.
Establishment Clause
The part of the First Amendment that bans the government from setting up or backing a religion.
Evangelical
Protestant Christianity that stresses personal conversion, or being “born again”, and the authority of the Bible.
Intelligent design
The claim that life’s complexity shows a designer. US courts have ruled that it is religion, not science.

Reading tip: Scholarly writing on religion is not preachy. It asks how a belief spread, how a community organised, or how science and faith interacted. The author rarely mocks either side, so an interpretation that sounds strongly anti-religious or anti-science is usually too extreme.

8.3American dominance: how the US became a superpowerManifest Destiny · empire · the American Century · soft power · decline debates

Why it matters: Readings about US expansion, empire-building, the world economy or American “decline” all assume one storyline. A small republic on the Atlantic coast grows into a nation that spans a continent. Then it gains an empire overseas. After 1945 it leads the postwar world order. US textbooks often tell this as a success story. Scholarly readings often come from historians who question it.

Growing across the continent (1803–1893)

In 1803 the young United States bought a huge territory called Louisiana from France for $15 million. This Louisiana Purchase doubled the country’s size at a stroke.

In 1823 the Monroe Doctrine told Europe not to colonise the Americas or meddle in them. The US, for its part, would stay out of Europe’s quarrels. Later, the doctrine was stretched to justify the US stepping in across Latin America.

In 1845 a new phrase appeared, Manifest Destiny: the belief that Americans were destined to spread across the continent. That year the US annexed Texas, making it part of the country, and in 1846 it gained Oregon. The Mexican–American War of 1846–48 then added California and the Southwest.

In 1867 the US bought Alaska from Russia for $7.2 million. At the time, people mocked the deal as “Seward’s Folly”, a foolish buy. In 1890 the census declared the frontier, the moving western edge of settlement, “closed”.

In 1893 Frederick Jackson Turner argued that this westward expansion had shaped American democracy and individualism, the belief in standing on your own feet. His frontier thesis is a staple of US history classes. But later historians criticised it for leaving out Native Americans, women and Mexicans.

An empire overseas (1893–1941)

In 1893 American sugar planters and businessmen, backed by US Marines, overthrew the queen of Hawaii. The US annexed Hawaii in 1898.

That same year, the Spanish–American War brought the US Puerto Rico, Guam and the Philippines. A brutal war against Filipino independence fighters followed, from 1899 to 1902. At home, critics of this imperialism, or empire-building, protested, among them the writer Mark Twain.

In 1904 Theodore Roosevelt went further. His Roosevelt Corollary made the US the “police power” of the Western Hemisphere, meaning the Americas. His famous saying was “speak softly and carry a big stick”.

The Panama Canal opened in 1914. Again and again, the US stepped into Central America and the Caribbean. People called this “gunboat diplomacy”, pressure backed by warships, and “dollar diplomacy”, pressure through money.

In 1917 the US entered World War I. President Wilson proposed a League of Nations to keep the peace. But the US Senate rejected the peace treaty. America then retreated into isolationism, staying out of alliances and foreign wars, until 1941.

Remember it like this: Monroe put up a keep-out sign for Europe over the Americas. Roosevelt made the US the policeman behind it, with a big stick.

The American Century (1941–1949)

In 1941 the publisher Henry Luce called for an “American Century”. After Pearl Harbor, the US became the “arsenal of democracy”, a vast weapons factory. By 1945, on some estimates, it made about half the world’s manufactured goods. It was also the only nuclear power.

Then the US designed the postwar order. The Bretton Woods agreement of 1944 put the dollar at the centre of world money, alongside the IMF and the World Bank. The United Nations followed in 1945. The US became one of the five permanent members of its Security Council, each with a veto, the power to block decisions.

In 1948 the Marshall Plan provided about $13 billion to rebuild Western Europe. NATO, a military alliance, followed in 1949. The Cold War story is in 8.4.

Alone at the top, then doubts (1989–today)

Between 1989 and 1991 the Soviet bloc of communist states collapsed. The US was left as the sole superpower: the “unipolar moment”. Francis Fukuyama’s “End of History”, an essay of 1989 and a book of 1992, argued that liberal democracy had won the battle of ideas. The Gulf War of 1991 showed off US military dominance.

The 1990s and 2000s brought globalisation, the freer flow of trade and money across borders. Its landmarks were NAFTA in 1994, the World Trade Organization in 1995 and the free-market “Washington Consensus”. After 9/11 in 2001, the “War on Terror” brought long wars in Afghanistan in 2001–21 and Iraq in 2003–11.

In 2010 China overtook Japan as the world’s second-largest economy. In 2018 a US–China trade war began with tariffs, or taxes on imports. Debate has grown over American decline and over a “multipolar” world, one with several competing great powers.

Four pillars of US power today

The first pillar of American power today is the economy. The US has the largest economy, about a quarter of world GDP, and the deepest capital markets, where firms raise money (8.1). It also has the dominant tech firms (8.5).

The second pillar is the dollar, the main currency for world trade. Central banks hold well over half of their reserves in dollars, so it is the leading reserve currency. That lets the US borrow cheaply and punish others with financial sanctions. In the 1960s a French finance minister called this an “exorbitant privilege”, an unreasonably large advantage.

The third pillar is the military. The US accounts for more than a third of world military spending. It has alliances with NATO, Japan, South Korea and Australia, and hundreds of bases abroad.

The fourth pillar is soft power, named by Joseph Nye in 1990. It means getting what you want through attraction rather than force. Its sources are Hollywood, music, brands, universities, and ideals of freedom and opportunity. Critics call the same spread “Americanisation” or cultural imperialism.

Remember it like this: Force pushes, like Roosevelt with his big stick. Soft power pulls, like a magnet: Hollywood, brands and universities draw people in.

Three big arguments

The first big argument is isolationism against internationalism: should the US stay out of foreign quarrels, or lead the world? President Washington’s Farewell Address of 1796 warned against “permanent alliances”, and Jefferson in 1801 warned against “entangling alliances”. The argument came back with the America First Committee of 1940–41, and the slogan has returned today.

The second is realism against idealism. Should foreign policy serve the national interest and the balance of power? Or should it spread democracy and international law, the ideal called “Wilsonianism” after President Wilson?

Third, is America special? American exceptionalism says it is uniquely free and destined to lead, an idea often traced to Alexis de Tocqueville’s Democracy in America of 1835–40. Declinism sees America as an ordinary great power heading for “imperial overstretch”, taking on more abroad than it can sustain. Paul Kennedy made that case in The Rise and Fall of the Great Powers in 1987.

Americans learn Manifest Destiny, the Monroe Doctrine and the Marshall Plan as basic civic vocabulary. Many recite the Pledge of Allegiance every school morning, as many Indian pupils sing the national anthem at assembly. “Leader of the free world” patriotism is the default backdrop.

India offers a useful counterpoint. During the Cold War the two democracies were “estranged”, or distant: the US allied with Pakistan, while India stayed non-aligned, joining neither side. They grew closer after the civil nuclear deal of 2008.

Words to know

Manifest Destiny
The 19th-century belief that the US was destined, even ordered by God, to spread across North America.
Monroe Doctrine
The 1823 policy that told Europe to stay out of the Americas. Later it was used to justify US intervention there.
Imperialism
Spreading power over other peoples through colonies, conquest or control of their economies.
Isolationism
A policy of staying out of alliances and foreign wars.
Hegemony
When one state, or one class, dominates the others. A “hegemon” is the leader that sets the rules.
Soft power
Influence that comes from attraction, through culture, values and institutions, rather than from force or payment.
Reserve currency
A currency that central banks keep in large amounts and that is widely used in world trade. Today this is mainly the US dollar.
American exceptionalism
The belief that the US is unlike any other nation in its origins, values or mission.
Unipolar / multipolar
Unipolar means a world with one dominant power. Multipolar means a world with several great powers competing.

Reading tip: Watch the author’s stance. A text may present expansion as an idealistic mission or as self-interested empire, or set an older view against a revisionist one. Note how strongly the author actually commits. Most authors weigh evidence rather than cheer or condemn.

8.4Capitalism, communism, socialism & the Cold Warmixed economies · containment · McCarthyism · Sputnik · non-alignment · 1989–91

Why it matters: From 1947 to 1991, capitalist democracy, led by the US, competed with Soviet communism. That contest shaped American politics, science, schooling, civil rights and even family life. Many readings about America in the 1950s–80s are really set against this backdrop. For what the “isms” mean, see Foundation 4.

The isms in real life

In real life, every rich country is a mixed economy, combining markets with government action. The real question is the balance. The US leans towards markets. The Nordic countries pair markets with high taxes and generous welfare, a model called social democracy.

The USSR ran a command economy, in which the state, not the market, decided what to produce. From 1928, under one-party rule, central planners set output through Five-Year Plans. In 1951 Hannah Arendt called Nazi Germany and Stalin’s USSR totalitarian: regimes seeking total control over society.

Fascism ruled Italy under Mussolini from 1922 and Germany under Hitler from 1933. It was ultranationalist, putting the nation above everything. It was also anti-democratic and fiercely anti-communist.

In the US, “socialism” is a loaded word that stirs strong feelings. Medicare, the public health insurance for people over 65 that started in 1965, was attacked as “socialized medicine”. Since 1906, scholars have asked why there is “no socialism in the United States”. Their answers include high wages, the frontier, racial and ethnic divisions among workers, and the two-party system.

Bernie Sanders brought the label back into the mainstream with his “democratic socialist” campaigns for president in 2016 and 2020. India’s story is different. Nehru’s India borrowed Soviet-style Five-Year Plans from 1951, but within a democratic, mixed economy. India added “socialist” to its Constitution’s Preamble in 1976 and liberalised its economy in 1991.

Remember it like this: Picture a dial from market to state. The US leans to the market end. The USSR turned it fully to the state.

How the Cold War began (1917–1954)

The story starts in 1917 with the Bolshevik Revolution in Russia. America soon had its first Red Scare, a panic about communists, with raids on suspected radicals.

After 1945 the wartime allies split. In 1946, in Fulton, Missouri, Churchill warned of an “Iron Curtain” across Europe. In 1947 the Truman Doctrine pledged US support for “free peoples” resisting subjugation, or domination. George Kennan’s strategy of containment, stopping communism from spreading, became US policy.

In 1948–49 came the Berlin Airlift, when supplies were flown into the city. Then 1949 brought NATO, the first Soviet atomic bomb and Mao’s Communist victory in China.

The Korean War of 1950–53 ended in a truce, a ceasefire that still divides Korea. At home, a second Red Scare, from 1947, brought loyalty checks and Hollywood blacklists, lists of people barred from work. Senator Joseph McCarthy’s reckless accusations in 1950–54 gave it the name McCarthyism.

The Rosenbergs were executed as atomic spies in 1953. That same year, Arthur Miller’s play The Crucible used the Salem witch trials as an allegory, a story with a hidden meaning, for the hunt.

Remember it like this: Containment is a fence, not a bulldozer: stop communism spreading, without knocking it down.

Sputnik, Cuba and Vietnam (1957–1975)

In 1957 the Soviet satellite Sputnik shocked America. The US responded with NASA, its new space agency, and with ARPA, a research agency whose ARPANET later became the internet’s ancestor. The National Defense Education Act poured money into science, maths and language teaching.

In 1961 the Bay of Pigs invasion of Castro’s Cuba failed, and the Berlin Wall went up. Then, in October 1962, came the Cuban Missile Crisis. It was the closest the world has come to nuclear war.

Not every country picked a side. In 1961, in Belgrade, Nehru of India, Tito, Nasser, Sukarno and Nkrumah founded the Non-Aligned Movement, for countries that joined neither camp.

In the Vietnam War, US combat troops arrived in 1965, and mass protests followed at home. US forces left in 1973, and Saigon fell in 1975. Meanwhile, in 1969, Apollo 11 landed on the Moon.

Thaw, chill and the end (1971–1991)

The 1970s brought détente, a thaw in tensions. In 1971 India signed a friendship treaty with the USSR. That same year, during the Bangladesh war, the US sent an aircraft carrier into the Bay of Bengal.

In 1972 Nixon visited China. He also signed SALT I, the first agreement capping the superpowers’ long-range nuclear missiles. The thaw ended in 1979, when the USSR invaded Afghanistan.

In 1983 Reagan called the USSR an “evil empire”. He also proposed a space-based missile shield, the Strategic Defense Initiative, nicknamed “Star Wars”. Gorbachev, in power from 1985, launched glasnost, meaning openness, and perestroika, meaning restructuring. The 1987 INF Treaty scrapped a whole class of nuclear missiles.

Then came the end. The Berlin Wall fell on 9 November 1989, and communist regimes across Eastern Europe collapsed. On 26 December 1991 the USSR itself dissolved.

Remember it like this: Glasnost opens the windows. Perestroika rebuilds the house.

The Cold War at home

Containment had a home front too. The historian Elaine Tyler May, writing in 1988, calls it “domestic containment”. In 1950s culture, the suburban nuclear family of parents and children was idealised as a bulwark, a defensive wall, against communism.

Schoolchildren practised “duck and cover” drills in case of a nuclear attack. In the 1959 “Kitchen Debate”, Vice-President Nixon held up American appliances to the Soviet leader Khrushchev as proof that capitalism was superior.

Another argument, now standard in US history courses, links the Cold War to civil rights. It holds that Soviet propaganda exploited American segregation as a Cold War embarrassment. This, the argument goes, helped push Washington toward civil-rights reform. Mary Dudziak made the case in Cold War Civil Rights, published in 2000.

Science felt the rivalry too. Defence research money helped build American science, its research universities and Silicon Valley.

Words to know

Containment
The US strategy of stopping communism from spreading, rather than rolling it back by war.
Domino theory
Eisenhower’s 1954 claim that if one country fell to communism, its neighbours would follow, like a row of dominoes. It was used to justify the Vietnam War.
MAD (mutually assured destruction)
Nuclear deterrence. Neither side attacks, because both would be destroyed.
Proxy war
A war in which one or both superpowers back local sides instead of fighting each other directly. Examples are Korea, Vietnam, Afghanistan and Angola.
Détente
An easing of tension between rivals, especially between the US and the USSR in the 1970s.
McCarthyism
Reckless accusations of disloyalty made without proper evidence. It is named after Senator Joseph McCarthy.
Non-Aligned Movement
Countries that refused to join either side, or bloc, in the Cold War. It was led by India, Yugoslavia, Egypt, Indonesia and Ghana.
Third World
At first, in 1952, the countries in neither the Western nor the Soviet bloc. Later it was used loosely for poorer nations. Today “Global South” or “developing countries” is preferred.
Military–industrial complex
The combined influence of the armed forces and the arms industry. Eisenhower warned about it in 1961.
Command economy
An economy in which the state, not the market, decides what is produced and at what price.

Reading tip: Cold War writing is often about historiography, the competing schools of interpretation (see Foundation 5):

  • an orthodox view that blames Soviet expansion;
  • a revisionist view (1960s) that blames US economic expansion;
  • post-revisionist syntheses.

Work out which school each scholar belongs to and what evidence each side relies on.

8.5Latest technology: AI, chips, biotech, space & energygenerative AI · semiconductors · CRISPR & mRNA · NewSpace · clean energy · how innovations spread

Why it matters: Articles on technology, and business articles about new inventions, expect you to know two things. The first is today’s big picture: the main technologies of the moment. The second is the classic theories of how new technologies spread and push out the old market leaders. An American reader picks up most of this from the news. Exact facts go out of date quickly, so learn the big picture, not the numbers.

Artificial intelligence: from Turing to ChatGPT

In 1950 Alan Turing proposed a test for machine intelligence. In 1956 a workshop at Dartmouth launched “artificial intelligence”, or AI, as a field. After that, waves of hype were followed by “AI winters”, when funding dried up.

In 1997 IBM’s Deep Blue computer beat the world chess champion, Garry Kasparov. The deep learning breakthrough came in 2012–16. It uses neural networks, layered software that learns patterns from huge datasets, trained on graphics chips called GPUs. In 2016 DeepMind’s AlphaGo beat Lee Sedol, a champion of the board game Go.

In 2017 Google researchers invented the transformer, the “T” in GPT. It is the design behind large language models, AIs trained on vast amounts of text to predict the next word. OpenAI’s ChatGPT, released on 30 November 2022, set off the boom in generative AI, which creates new content.

In 2024 AI pioneers won Nobel Prizes. Physics went to John Hopfield and Geoffrey Hinton for neural networks. Chemistry went to David Baker for protein design, and to Demis Hassabis and John Jumper for AlphaFold, which predicts protein shapes. The same year, the EU’s AI Act became the first law covering AI as a whole.

Some debates keep coming back. AI can pick up bias, or unfair slants, from its training data, and it can “hallucinate”, or make things up. Some fear job losses, while others expect gains in productivity. People also argue about copyright, energy use and deepfakes, fake videos or pictures made to look real. Finally, some ask whether advanced AI can be kept safe, a problem called alignment.

Remember it like this: AI in four stepping stones: Turing’s test in 1950, chess in 1997, Go in 2016 and ChatGPT in 2022.

Chips and computing: from one switch to quantum bits

The transistor, a tiny electronic switch, was invented at Bell Labs in 1947. The integrated circuit, which puts many transistors on one chip, followed in 1958–59. These led to Moore’s law in 1965: the number of transistors on a chip doubles roughly every two years. In 1971 the area south of San Francisco got its name, “Silicon Valley”.

Today Nvidia designs the GPUs that train most AI. TSMC in Taiwan makes most of the world’s most advanced chips. That is one reason Taiwan is a geopolitical flashpoint, a place where tensions could flare into conflict. The US CHIPS and Science Act of 2022 offers about $52 billion to bring chipmaking back home.

Quantum computers use qubits, which rely on two strange effects. In superposition, a qubit holds a mix of 0 and 1 until it is measured. In entanglement, two qubits are linked, so measuring one tells you about the other. The 2025 physics Nobel honoured 1980s circuit experiments that form the basis of one leading type of qubit (see p.77).

Biotechnology: reading and editing life’s code

A genome is a person’s full set of DNA, and reading it is called genome sequencing. The Human Genome Project took 13 years and about $3 billion, finishing in 2003. Today it costs a few hundred dollars.

In 2012 Jennifer Doudna and Emmanuelle Charpentier developed CRISPR gene editing, which won them the 2020 Nobel Prize. It works like molecular scissors that cut DNA at a chosen spot. In 2018 a Chinese scientist’s gene-edited babies caused a global ethics scandal. The first approved CRISPR therapy, Casgevy, arrived in 2023 for sickle-cell disease, an inherited blood disease. The UK approved it in November and the US in December.

In 2020 the Pfizer–BioNTech and Moderna COVID vaccines used mRNA, or messenger RNA, which carries a gene’s instructions to the cell’s protein-making machinery. The vaccine instructs your cells to make a harmless protein from the virus, so your immune system learns to recognise it. Katalin Karikó and Drew Weissman won the 2023 Nobel Prize for the underlying work.

Finally, GLP-1 drugs such as Ozempic and Wegovy began as diabetes drugs. Now they are blockbuster, or best-selling, weight-loss treatments.

Space and energy: new players, cheaper power

Once, only governments could do big things in space. Now private companies do them too, a change called NewSpace. In 2015 SpaceX first landed an orbital rocket’s booster, its lower stage, for reuse. For astronomy background, see Topic 6.

On 25 December 2021 the James Webb Space Telescope launched. On 23 August 2023 India’s Chandrayaan-3 made the first landing near the south pole of the Moon. NASA’s Artemis programme aims to send humans back to the Moon.

In clean energy, solar-power costs have fallen roughly 90% since 2010. Lithium-ion batteries, which won a 2019 Nobel Prize, power phones and electric vehicles. In 2022 the US passed the Inflation Reduction Act, the largest climate investment in its history. In December 2022 a US laboratory first achieved fusion “ignition”. Fusion releases energy by merging atoms. Ignition meant getting more energy out than the lab’s lasers put in.

The internet and the platforms

The internet began as ARPANET in 1969. Tim Berners-Lee created the World Wide Web in 1989–91. Google arrived in 1998, Facebook in 2004, and the iPhone in 2007, opening the smartphone era. Then came cloud computing, which keeps data and programs on distant computers, and the gig economy of short, app-based jobs.

Three big policy fights surround these platforms. The first is data privacy, as in the EU’s GDPR rules of 2018. The second is Section 230, a 1996 US law that shields platforms from liability, or legal blame, for users’ posts. The third is antitrust, or anti-monopoly, cases against Big Tech, including a 2024 ruling that Google illegally monopolised search.

India’s UPI payments system, launched in 2016, is a widely studied example. It is public digital infrastructure, open to all, competing with private platforms.

How new ideas spread and topple leaders

Business schools teach a few classic theories. In 1962 Everett Rogers described the diffusion of innovations. Adoption follows an S-curve: slow, then fast, then levelling off. Innovators go first, then early adopters, the early majority and the late majority, and finally the laggards.

In his 1997 book The Innovator’s Dilemma, Clayton Christensen described disruptive innovation. A cheaper, simpler product starts at the low end of a market. It climbs until it pushes aside the leaders, who were busy serving their best customers. Kodak invented the digital camera yet went bankrupt in 2012. Netflix overtook Blockbuster, the video-rental chain.

Older still is Joseph Schumpeter’s creative destruction, from 1942: capitalism advances as new industries destroy old ones. Three more ideas help explain why tech markets tend toward a few dominant winners. Network effects make a product more valuable as more people use it. Switching costs make leaving for a rival expensive, and a first-mover advantage rewards arriving first.

In the humanities, writers debate whether technology drives social change, a view called technological determinism, or whether society shapes how technology is built and used. The original Luddites, in 1811–16, were English textile workers who smashed the machines threatening their jobs.

Remember it like this: Diffusion: a keen cousin buys the new phone first, the parents next, grandpa last. Disruption: a cheap newcomer climbs from the bottom rung.

Words to know

Machine learning
Software that learns patterns from data instead of following rules written by hand. Deep learning uses neural networks with many layers.
Large language model (LLM)
An AI trained on huge amounts of text to predict the next word, which lets it write and answer questions. ChatGPT, Gemini and Claude are examples.
GPU
A graphics processing unit: a chip that runs many calculations at the same time, which makes it ideal for training AI.
Moore’s law
The observation that the number of transistors on a chip doubles about every two years.
CRISPR
A gene-editing tool, copied from a defence system that bacteria use, that cuts DNA at a chosen spot.
mRNA
Messenger RNA. It carries a gene’s instructions to the cell’s protein-making machinery.
Network effects
When a product becomes more valuable as more people use it, as with phones and social media.
Disruptive innovation
A simpler, cheaper product that starts at the low end of a market and in the end pushes aside established firms.
Creative destruction
Schumpeter’s term for innovation replacing old firms and industries.
Luddite
Someone who opposes new technology. The name comes from the English workers who broke machines in 1811–16.

Reading tip: Tech articles usually do one of three things:

  • compare a technology with the expectations around it (hype vs reality);
  • weigh benefits against risks;
  • explain why adoption was faster or slower than predicted.

Judge each article by its own claims, even when you know newer facts, because articles can be years old.

8.6Cell biology & genetics: high-school biology in one cardorganelles · energy · cell division · Mendel & DNA · microbes & immunity

Why it matters: Cell structure is a topic that comes up again and again in science writing. Almost every American takes a year of biology in high school, and many also take AP Biology, a more advanced course. So science writing uses these words without explaining them. Foundation 6 gives the one-paragraph version, and this card is the fuller one.

The cell and its parts

In 1665 Robert Hooke looked at cork under a microscope and coined the word “cell”. Between the 1830s and the 1850s, cell theory took shape: all living things are made of cells, and every cell comes from an existing cell.

Cells come in two kinds. Prokaryotes, the bacteria and archaea, have no nucleus. Eukaryotes, meaning plants, animals, fungi and protists, have a nucleus and organelles, small parts wrapped in their own membranes, like organs in a body.

Every cell is wrapped in a cell membrane, a double layer of fat molecules called lipids. It controls what enters and leaves, so it is called “selectively permeable”. Every cell also has ribosomes, which build proteins from instructions carried by RNA.

In eukaryotes, the nucleus holds the DNA and acts as the control centre. The endoplasmic reticulum, or ER, makes proteins and fats, and the Golgi apparatus packages and ships them. Mitochondria, found in nearly all eukaryotes, turn food and oxygen into ATP, the cell’s energy currency. A famous American school meme says it simply: “The mitochondria is the powerhouse of the cell.”

Plants and algae also have chloroplasts, which capture sunlight for photosynthesis. Plants, fungi and bacteria have a rigid outer cell wall, made of cellulose in plants and chitin in fungi. Vacuoles, mainly in plants, are for storage, and a plant cell has one large central vacuole. Lysosomes, mainly in animal cells, digest and recycle waste.

Remember it like this: Picture the cell as a factory: the nucleus is the head office, ribosomes the workbenches, the Golgi the shipping department and mitochondria the power plant.

Endosymbiosis, energy, division and stem cells

In 1967 Lynn Margulis proposed the endosymbiotic theory: mitochondria and chloroplasts were once free-living bacteria that were swallowed by another cell. The evidence is that they have their own DNA and double membranes, and they divide like bacteria. Many journals rejected her paper before the idea became the standard textbook view. It is a classic case of an outsider overturning the consensus, the view most experts share.

Cells handle energy through two processes that are mirror images. In photosynthesis, plants use light to turn carbon dioxide and water into sugar, giving off oxygen. In cellular respiration, cells use sugar and oxygen to make ATP, giving off carbon dioxide and water. Without oxygen, cells fall back on fermentation, which is how yeast makes alcohol and why hard-working muscles build up lactic acid.

A cell’s DNA is packed with proteins into long strands called chromosomes, and human body cells have 46 of them, in 23 pairs. Mitosis is the division that makes two identical cells, for growth and repair. Meiosis makes sperm and egg cells with half the chromosomes, and it shuffles genes to create variation. Cancer is cell division out of control.

Stem cells can develop into many types of cell. In the 2000s, federal funding for research on stem cells from embryos was a major US political fight. In 2006 Shinya Yamanaka found a way to reprogramme adult cells, turning them back into stem cells. This eased the ethical problem, and he won the Nobel in 2012.

Genetics: from Mendel’s peas to the double helix

In 1866 Gregor Mendel, working with pea plants, showed that traits pass down in separate units, now called genes. Each gene comes in versions called alleles, such as one for blue eyes and one for brown. A dominant allele shows its trait even if only one parent passes it on, while a recessive one shows only if both parents do. Predicting the traits of offspring with a grid called a Punnett square is a classic ritual in US biology classrooms.

Mendel’s work was ignored until 1900. In 1953 James Watson and Francis Crick built their model of DNA’s double helix, a twisted ladder, partly from Rosalind Franklin’s X-ray image, “Photo 51”. Franklin died in 1958, before the 1962 Nobel went to Watson, Crick and Maurice Wilkins. Her story is now a staple of reading about women in science.

Biologists call the flow from DNA to RNA to protein the central dogma. In transcription, a gene’s DNA is copied into RNA. In translation, that RNA is used to build a protein. DNA has four chemical letters, called bases, which pair A–T and C–G.

The human genome, the full set of human DNA, has about 3 billion base pairs and roughly 20,000 genes that code for proteins. Genes can also be switched on or off. Epigenetics is about chemical tags that do this without changing the DNA sequence, sometimes in response to environment or diet.

Remember it like this: DNA is the cookbook. Transcription copies one recipe into RNA, and translation cooks that recipe into a protein.

Microbes and how the body fights them

Between the 1860s and the 1880s, Louis Pasteur and Robert Koch established germ theory, the idea that microbes, or germs, cause disease. Viruses are not cells, and they can reproduce only inside a host cell, the cell they infect. Antibiotics attack bacteria, so they don’t work on viruses.

Penicillin, found by Fleming in 1928 and mass-produced in the Second World War, began the antibiotic age. Antibiotic resistance is evolution by natural selection happening in real time. The bacteria that survive a drug are the ones that go on to multiply.

The immune system uses white blood cells and antibodies, Y-shaped proteins, to recognise invaders. Vaccines train it in advance. Edward Jenner’s smallpox vaccine dates from 1796, and smallpox was declared eradicated, meaning wiped out, in 1980.

Many US students read a 2010 book, The Immortal Life of Henrietta Lacks. Lacks was a Black woman whose cancer cells were taken without her consent in 1951. They became “HeLa”, the first immortal human cell line, able to go on dividing in a lab indefinitely, and they helped develop the polio vaccine. The book is a common reference point in debates about medical ethics, race and consent.

Words to know

Organelle
A small part inside a cell with its own special job, like an organ in a body.
Mitochondria
Organelles that make ATP by cellular respiration. They have their own DNA, which is passed down only from the mother.
ATP
Adenosine triphosphate, the molecule cells use to store and spend energy.
Photosynthesis
Using light to turn carbon dioxide and water into sugar, giving off oxygen.
Mitosis / meiosis
Mitosis divides a cell into two identical body cells. Meiosis makes four sex cells, each with half the chromosomes.
Allele
One version of a gene, such as the version for blue eyes or for brown eyes.
Genotype / phenotype
The genotype is an organism’s genetic makeup. The phenotype is the traits you can observe in it.
Chromosome
A long DNA molecule packed with proteins. Humans have 23 pairs.
Endosymbiosis
One organism living inside another. It explains where mitochondria and chloroplasts came from.
Epigenetics
Changes in gene activity that can be inherited but don’t alter the DNA sequence.
Antibody
A Y-shaped immune protein that locks onto one specific invader.
Stem cell
A cell that has not yet specialised and can develop into other types of cell.

Reading tip: Cell-biology articles usually describe an experiment and what it showed, or a new hypothesis displacing an old one. Separate what was observed from what was inferred, and track which evidence supports which hypothesis. Knowing the vocabulary frees your attention for the logic.

MantleThe dense rocky layer under the crust.p.74
MarginalisationPushing a group out to the edges of power or of economic life.p.51
Maternal strategyHow a mother shares out her energy and time in raising her young.p.94
Maternity insuranceState-funded support for mothers around the time of childbirth.p.30
MatrilinealTracing family descent through the mother’s line.p.39
McCarthyismReckless accusations of disloyalty made without proper evidence. It is named after Senator Joseph McCarthy.8.4
Means of productionThe land, tools, factories and capital used to make goods. Marx defined class by whether you own them.p.15
MegafaunaLarge animals, weighing over about 45 kg.p.43
Metabolic processesThe body’s chemistry for breaking down food and building tissue.p.59
Metabolic rateHow fast an animal uses energy.p.93
MetaphorDescribing one thing as if it were another, such as calling the brain a computer.p.61
MeteoriteA space rock that reaches the ground.p.66
MethodologyThe method or type of evidence a scholar uses.p.4
Micro-wearScratches and pits, too small to see without a microscope, that chewing leaves on teeth.p.83
MilitantReady to confront others aggressively.p.48
Military–industrial complexThe combined influence of the armed forces and the arms industry. Eisenhower warned about it in 1961.8.4
Mill girlsYoung New England women who worked in the Lowell textile mills in the 1820s–40s.p.11
MineralizedHardened with minerals, as bone, teeth and shell are.p.84
MitochondriaOrganelles that make ATP by cellular respiration. They have their own DNA, which is passed down only from the mother.8.6
Mitosis / meiosisMitosis divides a cell into two identical body cells. Meiosis makes four sex cells, each with half the chromosomes.8.6
MobiliseTo organise people to act together.p.44
Model / simulationA computer model works out what should happen under a set of assumptions. Examples are the meteor stream “pipe” (p.69) and asteroid collisions (p.72).F6
MonolithOne single large block of stone.p.72
Monroe DoctrineThe 1823 policy that told Europe to stay out of the Americas. Later it was used to justify US intervention there.8.3
Moore’s lawThe observation that the number of transistors on a chip doubles about every two years.8.5
Moral hazardTaking bigger risks because someone else will bear the cost, as when banks expect a bailout.8.1
MorphologyThe shape and build of a body.p.92
MortalityDeath, or the death rate.p.96
Mortality rateHow many deaths there are for a given number of people.p.53
mRNAMessenger RNA. It carries a gene’s instructions to the cell’s protein-making machinery.8.5
MuckrakerA journalist who exposes corruption and social wrongs.p.26
MutationA change in a gene’s DNA.p.87
MyrmecophagousFeeding on ants and termites.p.93
NaiveHere, inexperienced. It is a technical term, not an insult.p.88
NatalRelating to birth. A natal stream is the stream where the fish was born.p.98
Natural selectionThe process by which inherited traits that help living things survive and reproduce become more common.p.87
NebulaA cloud of gas and dust.p.70
NecessitateTo make necessary.p.93
Nesting successThe share of nests that produce young.p.99
Network effectsWhen a product becomes more valuable as more people use it, as with phones and social media.8.5
NeuroscientistA scientist who studies the brain and nervous system.p.87
New DealFDR’s programmes of the 1930s for relief, recovery and reform (see Foundation 1).p.18
NicheA species’ role and way of life in its environment.p.92
Non-Aligned MovementCountries that refused to join either side, or bloc, in the Cold War. It was led by India, Yugoslavia, Egypt, Indonesia and Ghana.8.4
Non-avianNot a bird. “Non-avian dinosaurs” means all the dinosaurs other than birds.p.80
NonexpertA teacher who comes from a neighbouring field.p.56
Nontarget speciesSpecies that a control method is not meant to affect.p.97
NoxiousHarmful or unpleasant.p.88
Nuclear familyParents and their children only, unlike an extended or joint family.p.31
Nuclei (in the brain)Clusters of neuron cell bodies. This is not the nucleus of a cell or of an atom.p.62
ObsolescenceBecoming out of date.p.46
Occupational segregationMen and women, or other groups, working in different jobs.p.9 p.51
Officer vs enlistedOfficers are the commanders. Enlisted men are the ordinary soldiers.p.53
OpportunisticallyBy grabbing chances when they come up, not by specialising.p.99
Oral historyRecorded interviews with eyewitnesses (p.4).F5
OrganelleA small, specialised structure inside a cell.p.89 8.6
PacemakerHere, an internal timekeeper that sets a rhythm.p.95
Paleo-IndianThe earliest people to live in the Americas.p.43
Pan-AfricanismUnity among people of African descent all over the world.p.50
ParalysisLosing the ability to move muscles.p.90
ParasitizeTo live on or in a host and harm it.p.97
Parlor carA luxury railway carriage for daytime travel, with individual seats.p.49
ParticulatesFine particles in the air, such as ash or sulphate droplets.p.67
PartisanLoyal to a political party.p.6
PatriarchalismThe political theory that a ruler’s power is like a father’s.p.25
PatriarchyA social system in which men hold the main authority in the family and in society.p.21
PaucityScarcity, or a small amount.p.97
Peak activityThe time of day when a gene is most active.p.62
PeriodicRecurring, or coming back, at regular intervals.p.95
PeriodicityRegular repetition at fixed intervals.p.67
PeriodizationHow history is divided into eras, such as the “Renaissance” or the “Progressive Era”. Feminists asked whether these eras mean the same thing for women (p.23).F5 p.23
PerpetuateTo keep something going.p.85
PersonhoodWhat a culture counts as a “person”.p.22
PerturbedDisturbed or pushed off a regular path.p.69
Perversion (of truth)Distortion: a twisting of the truth.p.34
PhosphaticMade of calcium phosphate, the mineral in bones and teeth.p.84
PhotonA particle of light.p.77
PhotosynthesisUsing light to turn carbon dioxide and water into sugar, giving off oxygen.8.6
PhysiologyHow a living thing’s body works, such as how it uses energy, controls its temperature and grows.p.81
Piece ratePay for each item made, not for each hour worked.p.9
PigmentA substance that gives colour.p.100
Plaintiff / defendantThe plaintiff is the side that sues. The defendant is the side being sued. A case is named “Plaintiff v. Defendant”.F2
PlausibleReasonable and believable.p.82
Plenary powerComplete authority: full power, with no conditions attached.p.35
Political affiliationBelonging to a party, or siding with one.p.20
Political rhetoricPublic language meant to persuade, such as wartime propaganda.p.4
PosteriorAt the back end. Its opposite is anterior, at the front.p.84
PosterityFuture generations.p.34
PostulateTo suggest or assume something as a starting point for an argument.p.84
Precedent / doctrineA rule from earlier cases that later courts follow, for example the “Winters doctrine”.F2
PrecursorA substance from which another is made. Tryptophan is a precursor of serotonin.p.59
Predator–prey dynamicsHow hunters and the animals they hunt affect each other’s numbers and behaviour.p.82
PrefigureTo hint at something before it happens, to foreshadow.p.29
PreindustrialFrom the time before factories.p.40
PreliminaryComing first, to prepare for what follows.p.91
PreponderanceThe greater part, or the majority.p.46
PrerequisiteSomething that must be in place before something else can happen.p.35
PrimacyBeing first or highest in authority.p.35
Primary vs secondary responsibilityPrimary means the main cause. Secondary means a contributing cause.p.43
Primary vs secondary sourcePrimary sources were made at the time: letters, diaries, census records, wills, court records and newspapers. Secondary sources are later interpretations, such as books by historians.F5
Primary vs secondary sourcesSee Foundation 5.p.17
PrimordialExisting from the very start, and basic.p.44 p.71
PristineUntouched, still in its original condition.p.98
Private property (fee simple)Exclusive ownership: the owner can keep others out. The owner can also sell it or pass it on to heirs.p.41
Procreative labourHaving and raising children, seen as work.p.8
Progressive EraA US reform period, usually dated about 1890–1920. It responded to industrialisation, poverty in cities and corruption.p.26
PropagandaMaterial spread to promote a cause. Here it means the Whig party’s own publicity.p.6
ProtogalaxyA galaxy that is still forming.p.70
Proximate causeThe immediate, direct cause, not an underlying or distant one.p.91
Proxy warA war in which one or both superpowers back local sides instead of fighting each other directly. Examples are Korea, Vietnam, Afghanistan and Angola.8.4
PsycheThe mind or personality.p.31
PsychoactiveAffecting the mind or behaviour.p.60
Public discourseSpeech and writing that is published or made in public.p.23
Public lands / withdrawalPublic lands are land owned by the federal government and open to private claims. A withdrawal takes land out of that pool for a particular purpose.p.33
Public vs private sphereThe public sphere is politics and work. The private sphere is home and family.p.17
Putting-out systemThe pre-factory system: merchants gave workers raw materials at home and paid them for each finished piece.p.9
Quakers (Religious Society of Friends)A Protestant group founded in 1650s England. It is known for the “Inner Light”, the spiritual equality of women and men, pacifism and early opposition to slavery.p.21
QuantitativeBased on numbers and statistics.p.29
RecantTo take back a belief or statement in public.8.2
ReceiptsAll the money that businesses take in.p.55
ReciprocityGive-and-take: duties that run both ways.p.41
RecognitionFormal acceptance of a union as the group that bargains for the workers.p.49
RecollectTo consciously remember.p.63
ReconcilableAble to be made to fit together.p.52
ReconstructionA map made later, pieced together from indirect evidence.p.36
RecurringHappening again and again.p.96
RegimentAn army unit of about 1,000 soldiers.p.53
RelicSomething that survives from an earlier time.p.70
RemedyTo fix or cure.p.91
RenegadeGoing against the usual view, outside the mainstream.p.70
ReplenishTo fill up again.p.94
Reproductive compatibilityThe ability to mate and have young together.p.86
Republican motherhoodLinda Kerber’s term for the idea, after the Revolution, that mothers served the nation by raising virtuous citizens. The idea justified educating women.p.24
RepublicanismAn 18th-century set of ideas: a republic survives only if its citizens have civic virtue.p.24
Reserve currencyA currency that central banks keep in large amounts and that is widely used in world trade. Today this is mainly the US dollar.8.3
Reserved rightA right kept by, or set aside for, someone when land was granted or reserved.p.33
RespectiveBelonging to each one separately.p.89
RestrictiveToo narrow, so that it leaves out cases that should be included.p.86
RevisionistA new interpretation that overturns the accepted one. In academic writing the word is neutral, not an insult. The sequence is “orthodox” or “traditional”, then “revisionist”, then “post-revisionist”, a synthesis.F5 p.47
Revolution vs rotationRevolution is going around something, as in an orbit. Rotation is spinning on one’s own axis.p.65
RivetingGrabbing and holding attention.p.20
Rotational velocityHow fast stars and gas orbit a galaxy’s centre.p.68
SanitationClean water and getting rid of sewage.p.90
SecularizationThe decline of religion’s influence over public life. Max Weber called the modern age the “disenchantment of the world”.8.2
Sedentary suspension feederAn animal that stays in one place and filters bits of food from the water, like a clam.p.84
Self-regulatingKept in check from within, by the population itself.p.96
SemanticsMeaning.p.61
SeniorityJob rights, such as protection from being laid off, based on how long a worker has served.p.14
Service sectorWork that provides services, not goods, such as sales, office work, domestic and personal service.p.11
Settlement houseA community centre in a poor immigrant neighbourhood. Middle-class reformers lived there and offered services. An example is Hull House in Chicago, opened in 1889.p.26
Sex-typed jobA job seen as suitable for only one sex, as in “women’s work” or “men’s work”.p.11
ShroudA covering that wraps around something.p.69
SimulateTo imitate a process with a model.p.61
Social DarwinismThe misuse of Herbert Spencer’s phrase “survival of the fittest” to justify inequality, laissez-faire economics and racism. It is not Darwin’s biology.F4
Social history / “history from below”History that started in the 1960s–70s. It studies ordinary people, such as workers, women, slaves and immigrants, rather than kings and presidents. It often uses quantitative data, such as census figures and election returns. Economic history done with statistics is called “cliometrics”.F5
Social typeA person who is typical of a group or generation.p.18
SocialismSociety or the state owns or controls the major industries, to reduce inequality. It ranges from democratic socialism, with elections and a welfare state, to revolutionary forms.F4
Socio-cultural systemThe way a society is organised, taken together with its culture.p.39
Socioeconomic classA person’s position in society, measured by income, job and education.p.13
Socioeconomic status (SES)One measure that combines income, education and job.p.15
Soft powerInfluence that comes from attraction, through culture, values and institutions, rather than from force or payment.8.3
SolidarityUnity behind a shared purpose.p.48
SovereigntyThe highest authority over a territory. Tribes are “domestic dependent nations” with limited sovereignty.F2 p.33
SpawnOf fish: to release eggs and sperm, or to breed.p.98
SpeculateTo form a theory without firm evidence.p.88
StagflationInflation together with a stalled economy and high unemployment.8.1
Status quoThe way things already are.p.52
Status vs economic standingStatus is a person’s position compared with others, here compared with men. Economic standing is a person’s own income or wealth, not compared with anyone.p.13
Statute / legislationA law passed by a legislature. Courts decide what statutes and treaties mean.F2
Stem cellA cell that has not yet specialised and can develop into other types of cell.8.6
StereotypeA fixed, oversimplified image of a group.p.28 p.37
StochasticRandom, ruled by chance.p.91
StockyShort and solidly built.p.92
StrayTo wander from the usual path. Here, to spawn in a stream other than the natal one.p.98
StrewnScattered about.p.38
Stumbling blockAn obstacle, here one that stands in the way of accepting a hypothesis.p.60
SubcellularSmaller than a cell, or inside a cell.p.89
SubsequentFollowing, or later.p.99
Suffrage / franchiseThe right to vote. To “enfranchise” someone is to give them the vote.p.5
SuperluminalFaster than light.p.77
SuppressTo hold down or keep in check.p.85
SusceptibleEasily affected or harmed, for example by disease.p.100
SympatricLiving in the same area. Compare allopatric: living in separate areas.p.92
SynchronisedKept in step.p.62
Synonymous withThe same as, or equal to.p.79
SyntaxThe rules of form: how symbols are arranged.p.61
SystematicFollowing a regular, orderly pattern.p.75
TabooA ban set by a culture’s customs.p.34
Take precedenceTo rank above something else, or override it.p.41
TaphonomyThe study of what happens to remains between death and becoming fossils.p.82
Tax revenuesThe money a government collects from taxes.p.57
TenaciouslyFirmly, refusing to let go.p.7
TensionThe pulling force carried by a string or rope.p.65
TerrestrialOf or from Earth.p.66
TerritorialBefore statehood.p.28
TerritorialityA group’s claim to an area, and its defence of that area.p.36
TestableAble to be checked against evidence. See “falsifiable” in Foundation 6.p.67
TheropodThe group of mostly two-legged, mostly meat-eating dinosaurs, such as T. rex and Velociraptor. Most palaeontologists now think birds descend from them.p.80
ThesisThe main argument.p.53
Third WorldAt first, in 1952, the countries in neither the Western nor the Soviet bloc. Later it was used loosely for poorer nations. Today “Global South” or “developing countries” is preferred.8.4
Trade unionismOrganising workers into unions.p.10
TributeGoods that conquered peoples must pay to the ruling state.p.16
TsaristUnder the Russian emperors, called tsars, before 1917.p.30
UnambiguousClear, with only one possible meaning.p.85
UndermineTo weaken the base or support of something.p.73
UnequivocalClear, with no doubt and no exception.p.27
Unicellular / multicellularMade of one cell / made of many cells.p.89
UnilateralDone by one side alone, without the other side’s consent.p.35
Unipolar / multipolarUnipolar means a world with one dominant power. Multipolar means a world with several great powers competing.8.3
UplandHere, the drier ground around a wetland.p.99
UsufructThe right to use land or what it produces, without owning it outright.p.41
UtilityUsefulness.p.83
VascularizedSupplied with blood vessels.p.81
Venture capitalMoney put into young, high-risk companies in exchange for a share of ownership.8.1
Vertical / horizontal integrationVertical integration means owning each stage of your own supply chain, as Carnegie owned mines, railways and mills. Horizontal integration means buying competitors at the same stage, as Standard Oil did.8.1
VindicatedShown to be right.p.43
Voting blocA group that votes together as a unit.p.5
WaterfowlDucks, geese and swans.p.99

Personal background guide to American history and society. Background is here to help you read faster and with more confidence; always follow what each author actually says. Figures that change over time are dated; check the news for the latest. “Got it” progress and the appearance settings are saved in this browser only. Search covers the reading cards, Topic 8 and the glossary; use Cmd/Ctrl+F for text inside the Foundations.
